```json
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-frozen-law",
        "exact_quote": "Let $Y_u$ be the stationary diffusion with invariant law $\\eta$ and driving Brownian motion $B_u$, and fix $t>0$. Make the matrix construction of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:matrix-blocks\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:matrix-blocks\">32</a> at $Y_0$, using an independent random block offset. In its orthonormal frame put $N=B_t/\\sqrt t$; conditionally on the initial data and block offset, $N$ is a standard Gaussian vector. Define, in the initial spatial coordinates, $$\\label{eq:matrix-frozen-covariance}\n \\sigma=\\bigoplus_\\beta\\beta^2\n       \\exp\\bigl(\\sqrt{8t}\\,H[N]-4t\\mathrm{Id}_\\beta\\bigr),$$ and set $\\sigma=0$ on the omitted kernel. Then $$\\label{eq:matrix-frozen-law}\n \\mathbb E\\|R(Y_t)-\\sigma^{1/2}\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}^2\\longrightarrow0.$$ The matrices defining $\\sigma$ obey the bounded-column and input-map bounds <a href=\"#eq:matrix-column-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:matrix-column-bound\">[eq:matrix-column-bound]</a> and <a href=\"#eq:flattening-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:flattening-bound\">[eq:flattening-bound]</a>, the weighted defects <a href=\"#eq:matrix-tracial-defects\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:matrix-tracial-defects\">[eq:matrix-tracial-defects]</a>, and the mixed moment law of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:matrix-gaussian-moments\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:matrix-gaussian-moments\">33</a>.",
        "source_id": "PM-093.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-frozen-law",
          "exact_quote": "Let $Y_u$ be the stationary diffusion with invariant law $\\eta$ and driving Brownian motion $B_u$, and fix $t>0$. In its orthonormal frame put $N=B_t/\\sqrt t$; conditionally on the initial data and block offset, $N$ is a standard Gaussian vector. Define, in the initial spatial coordinates, $$\\label{eq:matrix-frozen-covariance}\n \\sigma=\\bigoplus_\\beta\\beta^2\n       \\exp\\bigl(\\sqrt{8t}\\,H[N]-4t\\mathrm{Id}_\\beta\\bigr),$$ and set $\\sigma=0$ on the omitted kernel. Then $$\\label{eq:matrix-frozen-law}\n \\mathbb E\\|R(Y_t)-\\sigma^{1/2}\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}^2\\longrightarrow0.$$ The matrices defining $\\sigma$ obey the bounded-column and input-map bounds <a href=\"#eq:matrix-column-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:matrix-column-bound\">[eq:matrix-column-bound]</a> and <a href=\"#eq:flattening-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:flattening-bound\">[eq:flattening-bound]</a>, the weighted defects <a href=\"#eq:matrix-tracial-defects\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:matrix-tracial-defects\">[eq:matrix-tracial-defects]</a>, and the mixed moment law of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:matrix-gaussian-moments\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:matrix-gaussian-moments\">33</a>.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-obs-multipliers",
        "exact_quote": "For $v,w\\ge0$, not both zero, put $$\\label{eq:obs-multiplier}\n M_L(v,w)=(v+w)^2\\int_0^\\infty\n \\frac{dT}{\n (1+Tv^2/L)(1+Tw^2/L)\n (1+(1+1/L)Tv^2)(1+(1+1/L)Tw^2)}.$$ Set $M_L(0,0)=0$. Then $$\\label{eq:obs-multiplier-bounds}\n M_L(v,w)\\le4\\log(1+L)\n \\quad\\hbox{for all }v,w\\ge0,\n \\qquad\n M_L(v,w)\\le9\n \\quad\\hbox{if }\\tfrac12\\le v/w\\le2.$$ The second bound concerns positive $v,w$ and is independent of $L$.",

      },

Then $$\\label{eq:obs-multiplier-bounds}\n M_L(v,w)\\le4\\log(1+L)\n \\quad\\hbox{for all }v,w\\ge0,\n \\qquad\n M_L(v,w)\\le9\n \\quad\\hbox{if }\\tfrac12\\le v/w\\le2.$$ The second bound concerns positive $v,w$ and is independent of $L$.",
          "source_id": "PM-093.1"
        }

Then $$\\label{eq:obs-multiplier-bounds}\n M_L(v,w)\\le4\\log(1+L)\n \\quad\\hbox{for all }v,w\\ge0,\n \\qquad\n M_L(v,w)\\le9\n \\quad\\hbox{if }\\tfrac12\\le v/w\\le2.$$ The second bound concerns positive $v,w$ and is independent of $L$.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-information-upper",
        "exact_quote": "There is an absolute constant $C_0$ such that, for every fixed $t>0$ and $L\\ge1$, the observations in Equation <a href=\"#eq:obs-covariance-definition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:obs-covariance-definition\">[eq:obs-covariance-definition]</a> satisfy $$\\label{eq:obs-information-upper}\n \\limsup\\int_0^\\infty\\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\\,\\frac{dT}{T^2}\\le C_0.$$ In particular, one may take $C_0=9/4$.",
        "source_id": "PM-093.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "exact_quote": "There is an absolute constant $C_0$ such that, for every fixed $t>0$ and $L\\ge1$, the observations in Equation <a href=\"#eq:obs-covariance-definition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:obs-covariance-definition\">[eq:obs-covariance-definition]</a> satisfy $$\\label{eq:obs-information-upper}\n \\limsup\\int_0^\\infty\\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\\,\\frac{dT}{T^2}\\le C_0.$$ In particular, one may take $C_0=9/4$.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-obs-integrated-stability",
        "exact_quote": "For $T>0$ let $h_T(v)=\\sqrt{1+f_L(Tv^2)}$, $v\\ge0$, and put $$K_L=\\int_0^\\infty\n \\frac{dq}{(1+q/L)^3(1+(1+1/L)q)}.$$ Then $0<K_L\\le\\log(1+L)$, and for arbitrary positive semidefinite matrices $A,B$ of the same size, $$\\label{eq:obs-matrix-stability}\n \\int_0^\\infty\\|h_T(A)-h_T(B)\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}^2\\,\\frac{dT}{T^2}\n \\le K_L\\|A-B\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}^2.$$ Let $\\sigma$ be the frozen covariance in Proposition <a href=\"#prop:frozen-law\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:frozen-law\">34</a>, and couple $$\\widetilde Z_T=(\\mathrm{Id}+f_L(T\\sigma))^{1/2}G$$ to $Z_T$ using the same independent Gaussian. For every fixed $t,L$, $$\\label{eq:obs-observation-stability}\n \\int_0^\\infty\\mathbb E|Z_T-\\widetilde Z_T|^2\\,\\frac{dT}{T^2}=o(1).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-093.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-obs-integrated-stability",
          "exact_quote": "For $T>0$ let $h_T(v)=\\sqrt{1+f_L(Tv^2)}$, $v\\ge0$, and put $$K_L=\\int_0^\\infty\n \\frac{dq}{(1+q/L)^3(1+(1+1/L)q)}.$$ Then $0<K_L\\le\\log(1+L)$, and for arbitrary positive semidefinite matrices $A,B$ of the same size, $$\\label{eq:obs-matrix-stability}\n \\int_0^\\infty\\|h_T(A)-h_T(B)\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}^2\\,\\frac{dT}{T^2}\n \\le K_L\\|A-B\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}^2.$$ Let $\\sigma$ be the frozen covariance in Proposition <a href=\"#prop:frozen-law\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:frozen-law\">34</a>, and couple $$\\widetilde Z_T=(\\mathrm{Id}+f_L(T\\sigma))^{1/2}G$$ to $Z_T$ using the same independent Gaussian. For every fixed $t,L$, $$\\label{eq:obs-observation-stability}\n \\int_0^\\infty\\mathbb E|Z_T-\\widetilde Z_T|^2\\,\\frac{dT}{T^2}=o(1).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-093.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For $T>0$ let $h_T(v)=\\sqrt{1+f_L(Tv^2)}$, $v\\ge0$, and put $$K_L=\\int_0^\\infty\n \\frac{dq}{(1+q/L)^3(1+(1+1/L)q)}.$$ Then $0<K_L\\le\\log(1+L)$, and for arbitrary positive semidefinite matrices $A,B$ of the same size, $$\\label{eq:obs-matrix-stability}\n \\int_0^\\infty\\|h_T(A)-h_T(B)\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}^2\\,\\frac{dT}{T^2}\n \\le K_L\\|A-B\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}^2.$$ Let $\\sigma$ be the frozen covariance in Proposition <a href=\"#prop:frozen-law\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:frozen-law\">34</a>, and couple $$\\widetilde Z_T=(\\mathrm{Id}+f_L(T\\sigma))^{1/2}G$$ to $Z_T$ using the same independent Gaussian.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-obs-ou-score",
        "exact_quote": "Let $N$ be a standard Gaussian vector conditional on $\\mathcal D$, and let $Z$ be any observation for which $\\mathsf I(N;Z\\mid\\mathcal D)<\\infty$. With independent standard Gaussian $G'$ set $$N_u=\\alpha N+\\sqrt{1-\\alpha^2}\\,G',\\qquad \\alpha=e^{-u},\\quad u>0.$$ The relative score of its posterior, with respect to the standard Gaussian marginal, is $$\\label{eq:obs-ou-score}\n D\\log\\frac{p_{N_u\\mid Z,\\mathcal D}}{\\gamma}(N_u)\n =\\frac{\\alpha}{1-\\alpha^2}\n   \\mathbb E[N-\\alpha N_u\\mid N_u,Z,\\mathcal D].$$ For every compact interval $\\mathcal J\\subset(0,\\infty)$, $$\\label{eq:obs-ou-information}\n \\mathsf I(N;Z\\mid\\mathcal D)\n \\ge\\int_{\\mathcal J}\\frac{\\alpha^2}{(1-\\alpha^2)^2}\n \\mathbb E\\left|\\mathbb E[N-\\alpha N_u\\mid N_u,Z,\\mathcal D]\\right|^2\\,du.$$ For the covariance observation in Equation <a href=\"#eq:obs-covariance-definition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:obs-covariance-definition\">[eq:obs-covariance-definition]</a>, $$\\label{eq:obs-data-processing}\n \\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\\ge\\mathsf I(N;Z_T\\mid\\mathcal D).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-093.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-obs-ou-score",
          "exact_quote": "Let $N$ be a standard Gaussian vector conditional on $\\mathcal D$, and let $Z$ be any observation for which $\\mathsf I(N;Z\\mid\\mathcal D)<\\infty$.           "source_id": "PM-093.1"

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-obs-block-tests",
        "exact_quote": "Fix compact intervals $\\mathcal J,\\mathcal X\\subset(0,\\infty)$, and write $\\kappa(u)=\\alpha^2/(1-\\alpha^2)^2$, where $\\alpha=e^{-u}$. Let $P(u,x,a,b)$ be a real polynomial, evaluated on matrices with any one fixed ordering of each monomial. On each positive block define $$A_\\beta=H_\\beta[N_\\beta],\\qquad\n A_{u,\\beta}=H_\\beta[(N_u)_\\beta],\\qquad\n B_{\\beta,x}=H_\\beta[(Z_{x/\\beta^2})_\\beta],$$ and $P_{\\beta,u,x}=P(u,x,A_{u,\\beta},B_{\\beta,x})$. Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\int_0^\\infty\\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\\,\\frac{dT}{T^2}\n \\ge{}&\\int_{\\mathcal J}\\int_{\\mathcal X}\\kappa(u)\n \\mathbb E\\sum_\\beta\\beta^2\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}_\\beta\n \\bigl(2(A_\\beta-\\alpha A_{u,\\beta})P_{\\beta,u,x}\n \\notag\\\\[-2pt]\n &\\hspace{43mm}-C_1P_{\\beta,u,x}^*P_{\\beta,u,x}\\bigr)\n \\,\\frac{dx}{x^2}\\,du.\n \\label{eq:obs-block-test-bound}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Here the constant $C_1$ is the absolute constant in Equation <a href=\"#eq:obs-block-bounds\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:obs-block-bounds\">[eq:obs-block-bounds]</a>.",
        "source_id": "PM-093.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-obs-block-tests",
          "exact_quote": "Fix compact intervals $\\mathcal J,\\mathcal X\\subset(0,\\infty)$, and write $\\kappa(u)=\\alpha^2/(1-\\alpha^2)^2$, where $\\alpha=e^{-u}$. Let $P(u,x,a,b)$ be a real polynomial, evaluated on matrices with any one fixed ordering of each monomial. On each positive block define $$A_\\beta=H_\\beta[N_\\beta],\\qquad\n A_{u,\\beta}=H_\\beta[(N_u)_\\beta],\\qquad\n B_{\\beta,x}=H_\\beta[(Z_{x/\\beta^2})_\\beta],$$ and $P_{\\beta,u,x}=P(u,x,A_{u,\\beta},B_{\\beta,x})$. Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\int_0^\\infty\\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\\,\\frac{dT}{T^2}\n \\ge{}&\\int_{\\mathcal J}\\int_{\\mathcal X}\\kappa(u)\n \\mathbb E\\sum_\\beta\\beta^2\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}_\\beta\n \\bigl(2(A_\\beta-\\alpha A_{u,\\beta})P_{\\beta,u,x}\n \\notag\\\\[-2pt]\n &\\hspace{43mm}-C_1P_{\\beta,u,x}^*P_{\\beta,u,x}\\bigr)\n \\,\\frac{dx}{x^2}\\,du.\n \\label{eq:obs-block-test-bound}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Here the constant $C_1$ is the absolute constant in Equation <a href=\"#eq:obs-block-bounds\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:obs-block-bounds\">[eq:obs-block-bounds]</a>.",
          "source_id": "PM-093.1"
        }

Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\int_0^\\infty\\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\\,\\frac{dT}{T^2}\n \\ge{}&\\int_{\\mathcal J}\\int_{\\mathcal X}\\kappa(u)\n \\mathbb E\\sum_\\beta\\beta^2\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}_\\beta\n \\bigl(2(A_\\beta-\\alpha A_{u,\\beta})P_{\\beta,u,x}\n \\notag\\\\[-2pt]\n &\\hspace{43mm}-C_1P_{\\beta,u,x}^*P_{\\beta,u,x}\\bigr)\n \\,\\frac{dx}{x^2}\\,du.\n \\label{eq:obs-block-test-bound}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Here the constant $C_1$ is the absolute constant in Equation <a href=\"#eq:obs-block-bounds\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:obs-block-bounds\">[eq:obs-block-bounds]</a>.",

        "exact_quote": "Let $n,g',g$ be independent scalar standard Gaussians and let $n_u=\\alpha n+\\sqrt{1-\\alpha^2}\\,g'$. For every fixed mixed polynomial, the weighted trace moments of $$\\big(A_\\beta,A_{u,\\beta},\\widetilde B_{\\beta,x}\\big)$$ converge to the corresponding scalar moments of $$\\big(n,n_u,g_x(n)g\\big).$$ The assertion holds after integration over compact positive $u,x$ intervals against $\\kappa(u)\\,du\\,dx/x^2$, and permits fixed polynomial dependence on $u,x$.       },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-information-lower",
        "exact_quote": "For every fixed $t>0$ and $L\\ge1$, let $g_x$ be defined by Equation <a href=\"#eq:obs-g-function\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:obs-g-function\">[eq:obs-g-function]</a>, and let $n,g$ be independent scalar standard Gaussians. Then $$\\label{eq:obs-information-lower}\n \\liminf\\int_0^\\infty\\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\\,\\frac{dT}{T^2}\n \\ge C_1^{-1}\\int_0^\\infty\n       \\mathsf I\\big(n;g_x(n)g\\big)\\,\\frac{dx}{x^2}.$$ The constant $C_1$ is universal and is independent of $t,L$.",
        "source_id": "PM-093.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-information-lower",
          "exact_quote": "For every fixed $t>0$ and $L\\ge1$, let $g_x$ be defined by Equation <a href=\"#eq:obs-g-function\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:obs-g-function\">[eq:obs-g-function]</a>, and let $n,g$ be independent scalar standard Gaussians. Then $$\\label{eq:obs-information-lower}\n \\liminf\\int_0^\\infty\\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\\,\\frac{dT}{T^2}\n \\ge C_1^{-1}\\int_0^\\infty\n       \\mathsf I\\big(n;g_x(n)g\\big)\\,\\frac{dx}{x^2}.$$ The constant $C_1$ is universal and is independent of $t,L$.",
          "source_id": "PM-093.1"
        }

      "exact_statement": "For every fixed $t>0$ and $L\\ge1$, let $g_x$ be defined by Equation <a href=\"#eq:obs-g-function\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:obs-g-function\">[eq:obs-g-function]</a>, and let $n,g$ be independent scalar standard Gaussians. Then $$\\label{eq:obs-information-lower}\n \\liminf\\int_0^\\infty\\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\\,\\frac{dT}{T^2}\n \\ge C_1^{-1}\\int_0^\\infty\n       \\mathsf I\\big(n;g_x(n)g\\big)\\,\\frac{dx}{x^2}.$$ The constant $C_1$ is universal and is independent of $t,L$.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-scalar-divergence",
        "exact_quote": "Let $n,g$ be independent scalar standard Gaussians and, for $t\\ge1$, define $$\\ell=\\exp(\\sqrt{8t}\\,n-4t),\\qquad\n z_x=\\sqrt{1+f_t(x\\ell)}\\,g.$$ There are absolute constants $c>0$ and $h_0\\ge1$ such that, for all $t\\ge h_0$, $$\\label{eq:obs-scalar-divergence}\n \\int_0^\\infty\\mathsf I(n;z_x)\\,\\frac{dx}{x^2}\n \\ge c\\mathbb E\\big[\\ell\\mathbf 1_{\\{\\ell\\ge e^{2t}\\}}\\big]\n                  \\log(t/h_0)\n \\ge\\frac c2\\log(t/h_0).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-093.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-scalar-divergence",
          "exact_quote": "Let $n,g$ be independent scalar standard Gaussians and, for $t\\ge1$, define $$\\ell=\\exp(\\sqrt{8t}\\,n-4t),\\qquad\n z_x=\\sqrt{1+f_t(x\\ell)}\\,g.$$ There are absolute constants $c>0$ and $h_0\\ge1$ such that, for all $t\\ge h_0$, $$\\label{eq:obs-scalar-divergence}\n \\int_0^\\infty\\mathsf I(n;z_x)\\,\\frac{dx}{x^2}\n \\ge c\\mathbb E\\big[\\ell\\mathbf 1_{\\{\\ell\\ge e^{2t}\\}}\\big]\n                  \\log(t/h_0)\n \\ge\\frac c2\\log(t/h_0).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-093.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $n,g$ be independent scalar standard Gaussians and, for $t\\ge1$, define $$\\ell=\\exp(\\sqrt{8t}\\,n-4t),\\qquad\n z_x=\\sqrt{1+f_t(x\\ell)}\\,g.$$ There are absolute constants $c>0$ and $h_0\\ge1$ such that, for all $t\\ge h_0$, $$\\label{eq:obs-scalar-divergence}\n \\int_0^\\infty\\mathsf I(n;z_x)\\,\\frac{dx}{x^2}\n \\ge c\\mathbb E\\big[\\ell\\mathbf 1_{\\{\\ell\\ge e^{2t}\\}}\\big]\n                  \\log(t/h_0)\n \\ge\\frac c2\\log(t/h_0).$$",
      "explanation": "The scalar scale-integrated information diverges logarithmically with the time parameter.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-scalar-divergence",
          "exact_quote": "Let $n,g$ be independent scalar standard Gaussians and, for $t\\ge1$, define",
          "source_id": "PM-093.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "claim-scalar-divergence",
      "kind": "bound",
      "quantifiers": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-scalar-divergence",
          "exact_quote": "for all $t\\ge h_0$",
          "source_id": "PM-093.1"
        }
      ],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-lem-scalar-divergence"
    }
  ],
  "generation_status": "COMPLETE",
  "pm_id": "PM-093.1",
  "schema": "pm.claims.v2"
}
```

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Log-concave density** ([source](#eq:intro-subgaussian)): A Lebesgue density $\rho$ on $\mathbb R^n$ is log-concave if its positive set is convex and $\log\rho$ is concave there.
- **Euclidean gradient notation** ([source](#eq:intro-subgaussian)): We write $D$ for the Euclidean gradient.
- **Relative entropy** ([source](#sec:calculus)): For probabilities $p,\pi$ with $p\ll\pi$, define their relative entropy by $$H(p\mid\pi)=\int\log\frac{dp}{d\pi}\,dp.$$
- **Mutual information** ([source](#eq:calculus-entropy-duality)): Mutual information is $\mathsf I(U;Z)=H(P_{U,Z}\mid P_U\otimes P_Z)$.
- **Dirichlet operator of a density** ([source](#stmt-lem-dirichlet-core)): On $C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^n)$ define $$A_\pi=-\Delta-D\log\pi\cdot D.$$
- **One-slot flattening** ([source](#pm-h-19)): For a tensor $F$, a one-slot flattening is the matrix whose row index is one specified tensor slot and whose column index is the tuple of all remaining slots.
- **Poincare constant of regularized law** ([source](#pm-h-14)): Write $R_s^P$ for the optimal Poincaré constant of $\mu_s$.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-main-constant | entropy coefficient | ≤ | $C a^2$ | absolute constant C, subgaussian scale a |
| q-transport-constant | transport-entropy coefficient | ≤ | $Ca^2 H(\nu\mid\mu)$ | same constant as main theorem |
| q-regularized-constant | regularized logarithmic Sobolev constant | ≤ | $1+s$ | regularization time s>0 |
| q-critical-time | fixed regularization time | = | $1/8$ | fixed |
| q-prediction-limit | normalized prediction energy | ~ | $e^{-r}$ | noise variance r |
| q-column-bound | sum of squared matrix column | ≤ | $4\mathrm{Id}$ | pointwise |
| q-information-upper | universal information constant | = | $9/4$ | absolute constant |
| q-multiplier-log | integrated spectral multiplier | ≤ | $4\log(1+L)$ | cap L |
| q-scalar-divergence | scalar integrated information | ≥ | $\frac c2\log(t/h_0)$ | time t |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **s-curvature** [claim](#stmt-lem-curvature) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-curvature): semigroup contraction and convex approximation -> anisotropic functional inequalities
- **s-dirichlet** [claim](#stmt-lem-dirichlet-core) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-dirichlet-core): essential self-adjointness and local diffusion construction -> stationary diffusion semigroup
- **s-entropy-calc** [claim](#stmt-lem-entropy-calculus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-entropy-calculus): form chain rule and convex truncation -> entropy dissipation
- **s-signals** [claim](#stmt-lem-gaussian-signals) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-gaussian-signals): Ornstein-Uhlenbeck dissipation -> integrated prediction identity
- **s-normalization** [claim](#stmt-lem-critical-normalization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-critical-normalization): truncation, tilt, centering, dilation -> normalized counterexamples
- **s-flow-bounds** [claim](#stmt-lem-critical-flow-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-critical-flow-bounds): heat-flow transport contraction -> monotone Lipschitz constants
- **s-defect** [claim](#stmt-lem-critical-defect) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-critical-defect): Gaussian integration by parts and tail argument -> compactness and entropy defect
- **s-optimizer** [claim](#stmt-lem-optimizer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-optimizer): direct method, Euler equation, elliptic regularity -> positive optimizer
- **s-change-reference** [claim](#stmt-lem-critical-change-reference) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-critical-change-reference): change of reference identity -> tilted law with eigenfield
- **s-translation** [claim](#stmt-lem-translation-gain) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-translation-gain): translation by the mean and logarithmic Sobolev -> translation gain
- **s-two-alt** [claim](#stmt-lem-critical-two-alternatives) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-critical-two-alternatives): case split strict versus gap -> critical field with small mean
- **s-regularized** [claim](#stmt-prop-regularized-constants) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-regularized-constants): contact derivative along heat flow -> constants tend to one
- **s-critical-sequence** [claim](#stmt-prop-critical-sequence) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-critical-sequence): subsequence with fixed alternative -> critical sequence
- **s-probes** [claim](#stmt-lem-efficient-probes) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-efficient-probes): entropy dissipation with translation gain -> negligible probe information
- **s-entropy-loss** [claim](#stmt-lem-entropy-loss) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-entropy-loss): adaptive observations over independent copies -> entropy loss bound
- **s-prediction** [claim](#stmt-prop-prediction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-prediction): matching upper and lower entropy bounds -> prediction curve
- **s-outliers** [claim](#stmt-lem-posterior-outliers) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-posterior-outliers): affine prediction bound -> outlier subspace bounds
- **s-bessel** [claim](#stmt-lem-hierarchy-bessel) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-bessel): Hermite chaos Bessel inequality -> Bessel bound
- **s-smoothing** [claim](#stmt-lem-hierarchy-smoothing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-smoothing): spectral measure estimates -> smoothed near eigenfunction
- **s-construction** [claim](#stmt-lem-hierarchy-construction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-construction): induction with symmetrization -> tensor fields
- **s-hermite** [claim](#stmt-lem-hermite-expansion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hermite-expansion): iterated Ito formula -> chaos expansion
- **s-tails** [claim](#stmt-lem-hierarchy-operator-tails) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-operator-tails): conditional covariance representation -> vanishing operator tails
- **s-divergence** [claim](#stmt-lem-hierarchy-divergence) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-divergence): cutoff approximation of weighted integration by parts -> differentiated operator identity
- **s-hierarchy** [claim](#stmt-prop-hierarchy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-divergence-2): curvature integration by parts -> complete hierarchy
- **s-centering** [claim](#stmt-lem-matrix-centering) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-matrix-centering): square-root Lipschitz bound and Poincare -> centered square root
- **s-copied-slots** [claim](#stmt-lem-matrix-copied-slots) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-matrix-copied-slots): weighted Schatten interpolation -> slot averaging bound
- **s-saturation** [claim](#stmt-lem-matrix-saturation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-matrix-saturation): Sylvester equation and energy identities -> saturated matrix fields
- **s-bounded-column** [claim](#stmt-lem-matrix-bounded-column) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-matrix-bounded-column): weighted spectral truncation -> bounded column
- **s-index-exchange** [claim](#stmt-lem-matrix-index-exchange) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-matrix-index-exchange): slot permutation chain -> commutation and exchange
- **s-blocks** [claim](#stmt-lem-matrix-blocks) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-matrix-blocks): randomized spectral grouping -> block matrices
- **s-gaussian-moments** [claim](#stmt-lem-matrix-gaussian-moments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-matrix-gaussian-moments): Gaussian integration by parts and Wick pairing -> scalar Gaussian moments
- **s-frozen-law** [claim](#stmt-prop-frozen-law) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-frozen-law): Hermite series summation -> frozen lognormal covariance
- **s-multipliers** [claim](#stmt-lem-obs-multipliers) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-obs-multipliers): direct integral estimate -> multiplier bounds
- **s-info-upper** [claim](#stmt-prop-information-upper) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-information-upper): logarithmic Sobolev on posterior likelihood -> universal upper bound
- **s-stability** [claim](#stmt-lem-obs-integrated-stability) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-obs-integrated-stability): scalar Lipschitz integral and eigenbasis expansion -> frozen replacement
- **s-ou-score** [claim](#stmt-lem-obs-ou-score) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-obs-ou-score): kernel differentiation and entropy dissipation -> conditional score bound
- **s-block-tests** [claim](#stmt-lem-obs-block-tests) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-obs-block-tests): completing the square and scale substitution -> block test bound
- **s-replacement** [claim](#stmt-lem-obs-polynomial-replacement) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-obs-polynomial-replacement): interpolation of moment norms -> frozen test traces
- **s-poly-density** [claim](#stmt-lem-obs-polynomial-density) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-obs-polynomial-density): entire function and Fourier uniqueness -> polynomial density
- **s-nonlinear-trace** [claim](#stmt-lem-obs-nonlinear-trace-law) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-obs-nonlinear-trace-law): index swap comparison and polynomial approximation -> scalar trace law
- **s-info-lower** [claim](#stmt-prop-information-lower) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-information-lower): optimize polynomial tests, exhaust parameters -> scalar lower bound
- **s-scalar-divergence** [claim](#stmt-lem-scalar-divergence) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scalar-divergence): binary relative entropy on tail events -> logarithmic divergence
- **s-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scalar-divergence-2): contradiction between upper bound and divergence -> main theorem
- **s-transport** [claim](#stmt-cor-intro-transport) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-gaussian-signals-2): Gaussian regularization, Otto-Villani, limit -> transport-entropy inequality

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-dimension-free-lsi**: For every centered log-concave probability measure with a Lebesgue density on Euclidean space and every parameter a satisfying the linear subgaussian condition, the entropy of f squared is at most an absolute constant times a squared times the Dirichlet energy of f, for every compactly supported smooth f. The same constant serves in every dimension.
- **inv-transport-entropy**: Under the assumptions of the main theorem, the squared quadratic Wasserstein distance from any finite-second-moment probability to the measure is at most the same absolute constant times a squared times the relative entropy.
- **inv-anisotropic-curvature-lsi**: For a density proportional to the exponential of minus a positive definite quadratic form minus a proper lower semicontinuous convex function, with full dimensional support, entropy and variance are controlled by the energy weighted by the inverse of the quadratic matrix.
- **inv-regularized-constant-bounds**: Along a hypothetical counterexample sequence, the regularized logarithmic Sobolev constant is nondecreasing and 1-Lipschitz in the Gaussian time, and lies between 1 and 1 plus that time.
- **inv-prediction-curve**: For the strict and gap critical sequences, the normalized energy of the conditional mean of the score given a Gaussian observation tends to e to the minus r, for each fixed r in (0, 1/4).
- **inv-information-upper**: For every fixed diffusion time t>0 and cap L>=1, the limsup of the scale-integrated mutual information of the covariance observations is bounded by an absolute constant, which may be taken to be 9/4.
- **inv-scalar-information-divergence**: For the scalar lognormal Gaussian mixture, the scale-integrated mutual information is at least a positive absolute constant over two times the logarithm of t over h_0, for all sufficiently large t.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-non-logconcave-or-discrete**: Apply the dimension-free logarithmic Sobolev bound or the transport-entropy bound to a measure that is not centered or not log-concave with a Lebesgue density, for example a discrete distribution. -> The theorem gives no control; the constant guarantee is stated only for centered log-concave laws with a density.
- **anti-numeric-constant**: Insert a concrete numerical value for the absolute constant C into a sampler, mixing-time or concentration budget. -> The theorem only asserts existence of a finite absolute constant, derived through a contradiction argument, so no value is supplied.
- **anti-counterexample-lemmas**: Cite the normalization and critical-sequence lemmas as unconditional facts about real measures. -> These lemmas hold only under the hypothetical failure of the main theorem; unconditionally the objects they describe do not exist.
- **anti-nonlinear-subgaussian**: Assume the linear subgaussian parameter controls tails of arbitrary nonlinear functions directly. -> The hypothesis constrains only linear marginals; nonlinear control is obtained only through the logarithmic Sobolev conclusion.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main result is an existence statement for an unspecified absolute constant, proved by assuming a counterexample sequence and deriving a contradiction between an information upper bound and a divergent lower bound. No finite procedure, algorithm or explicit construction is produced that a program could compute and an oracle could check.

| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The block matrices are proof-internal analytic objects built from a spectral decomposition. They are not a stored or queryable representation. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The unnormalised matrix norms are analytic bookkeeping to avoid dimension factors. They imply nothing about arithmetic units or dataflow. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Side information appears only as conditioning in an information identity for Gaussian channels. There is no adversary, key or security notion. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Polynomial density is a functional-analytic tool for the information comparison. The paper has no learning problem, model class or generalisation claim. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The optimizer of an entropy quotient is shown to exist and be positive by compactness and regularity. No solver, rate or finite instance is given. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The hierarchy concerns sequences of densities in finite-dimensional Euclidean spaces. It has no nodes, messages or failure model. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The transport-entropy corollary is a purely mathematical consequence. It needs a log-concave target with a Lebesgue density and carries no stated engineering construction. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-hypothesis-centered-logconcave** (hypothesis): The dimension-free logarithmic Sobolev bound is asserted only for centered log-concave probability measures with a Lebesgue density, with the subgaussian parameter a satisfying the linear condition. Outside this class the theorem gives no control. Blocked use: ['Applying the bound to discrete, atomic or otherwise non-log-concave laws', 'Applying the bound to a law that is not centered without first re-centering and checking the hypotheses']
- **limit-constant-nonexplicit** (formal_scope): The theorem only asserts that some absolute constant exists. It is obtained by contradiction, so the statement carries no usable size for C. Blocked use: ['Inserting a concrete numerical value of C into a mixing-time, concentration or sampling budget', 'Presenting the result as an explicit quantitative guarantee']
- **limit-linear-marginals-only** (hypothesis): The subgaussian hypothesis constrains linear functions of the random vector only. Control of nonlinear functions comes solely from the proved logarithmic Sobolev conclusion, not from the hypothesis itself. Blocked use: ['Assuming the parameter a bounds tails of arbitrary nonlinear observables directly', 'Replacing the linear condition by a weaker covariance-only assumption']
- **limit-conditional-counterexample-scope** (counterexample_scope): The intermediate lemmas and propositions on normalized laws, critical sequences, prediction curves, tensor hierarchies and matrix fields are proved under the hypothetical failure of the main theorem. They describe objects that do not exist unconditionally. Blocked use: ['Citing these results as unconditional facts about any actual log-concave measure', 'Using the intermediate constructions as stand-alone estimates outside the contradiction argument']
- **limit-fixed-regularization-time** (edge_case): The critical sequence is extracted at one fixed Gaussian regularization time, which is not sent to zero. The near-eigenfunction estimates are tied to this fixed time and to the chosen subsequence. Blocked use: ['Extrapolating the critical-sequence estimates to other regularization times or to the unregularized law', 'Treating the o-terms as uniform in dimension or in the energy parameter I']
- **limit-prediction-noise-range** (edge_case): The prediction-curve limit is proved only for noise variances in a bounded open interval, with the critical sequence fixed. It says nothing at larger noise levels or at the interval endpoints. Blocked use: ['Using the exponential limit outside the stated range of r', 'Reading the limit as a uniform statement down to r near zero']
- **limit-observation-parameters-fixed** (approximation): The information upper bound holds in the limsup along the counterexample sequence with the diffusion time and the covariance cap held fixed. The contradiction is reached only by choosing these parameters large after the sequence limit; no uniform estimate as they grow is available. Blocked use: ['Exchanging the sequence limit with growth of the time or cap parameters', 'Using the bound as a finite-dimension, finite-sample estimate']
- **limit-optimizer-fixed-law** (hypothesis): Existence and positivity of the entropy optimizer require the logarithmic Sobolev constant to exceed both the Poincare constant and the regularization time. The constants in this stage depend on one fixed law and its dimension, so no uniform estimate along the sequence comes from it. Blocked use: ['Invoking the optimizer when the logarithmic Sobolev and Poincare constants coincide', 'Using its constants as dimension-free bounds']
- **limit-constant-value-absent** (unknown): The paper states no numerical value, upper bound or effectivity for the absolute constant C of the main theorem and its transport corollary. Blocked use: ['Quoting any specific value or range for C as coming from the paper']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that every centered log-concave probability measure with a Lebesgue density on ℝ<sup><i>n</i></sup> and linear subgaussian parameter <i>a</i> satisfies $`\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}\nolimits _\mu(f^2)\le Ca^2\int|Df|^2\,d\mu`$ for compactly supported smooth <i>f</i>, with one universal constant <i>C</i>. This resolves positively the dimension-free logarithmic Sobolev conjecture for subgaussian log-concave measures.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

A logarithmic Sobolev inequality controls the entropy of a function by its Dirichlet energy. Besides implying a Poincaré inequality, it gives Gaussian concentration for Lipschitz functions. For a log-concave measure, a natural question is whether Gaussian tails of all *linear* functions already force this stronger conclusion. We prove that they do, with a constant independent of dimension.

<a id="eq:intro-subgaussian"></a>
For a probability measure $\nu$ and a nonnegative integrable function $g$, write $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_\nu(g)=\int g\log g\,d\nu
       -\left(\int g\,d\nu\right)\log\left(\int g\,d\nu\right),$$ with $0\log0=0$. A Lebesgue density $\rho$ on $\mathbb R^n$ is log-concave if its positive set is convex and $\log\rho$ is concave there. We call a centered random vector $X$ *linearly $a$-subgaussian* if $$\label{eq:intro-subgaussian}
 \sup_{|\theta|=1}\mathbb E\exp\!\left(\frac{\langle X,\theta\rangle^2}{a^2}\right)
 \le2.$$ This definition concerns only linear functions of $X$. We write $D$ for the Euclidean gradient.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is an absolute constant $C<\infty$ with the following property. For every $n\ge1$, every centered log-concave probability measure $\mu$ with a Lebesgue density on $\mathbb R^n$, and every $a>0$ satisfying <a href="#eq:intro-subgaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-subgaussian">[eq:intro-subgaussian]</a> for $X\sim\mu$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_\mu(f^2)\le C a^2\int_{\mathbb R^n}|Df|^2\,d\mu
 \qquad\text{for every }f\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^n).$$ The same constant works in every dimension.*

</div>

No smoothness of the density or positive lower curvature bound is required. In particular, the theorem includes uniform measures on convex bodies. The proof will use smooth positive densities only after a reduction that preserves any hypothetical sequence of counterexamples.

The Otto–Villani implication from logarithmic Sobolev to quadratic transport–entropy inequalities (Otto and Villani 2000, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[OttoVillani2000](#ref-OttoVillani2000)] gives the following standard consequence, with the same constant. Write $\mathcal P_2(\mathbb R^n)$ for the Borel probabilities with finite second moment. For $\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal P_2(\mathbb R^n)$, set $$W_2(\alpha,\beta)^2
 =\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\alpha,\beta)}\int|x-y|^2\,d\pi(x,y),$$ where $\Pi(\alpha,\beta)$ is the set of their couplings. Relative entropy is $H(\alpha\mid\beta)=\int f\log f\,d\beta$ if $\alpha=f\beta$, with its possible value $+\infty$, and is $+\infty$ if $\alpha\not\ll\beta$.

<a id="stmt-cor-intro-transport"></a>
<div id="cor:intro-transport" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Quadratic transport–entropy inequality). *Under the assumptions of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, every $\nu\in\mathcal P_2(\mathbb R^n)$ satisfies $$W_2(\nu,\mu)^2\le Ca^2 H(\nu\mid\mu),$$ with the same absolute constant $C$ as in that theorem.*

</div>

The proof in Section <a href="#sec:transport-consequence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transport-consequence">2.1</a> gives the approximation argument for nonsmooth log-concave densities.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### History and significance

Gross’s work on logarithmic Sobolev inequalities connected the Gaussian inequality with hypercontractivity and established a framework in which the constants remain independent of dimension (Gross 1975) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Gross1975](#ref-Gross1975)]. The diffusion approach of Bakry and Émery (Bakry and Émery 1985) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[BakryEmery1985](#ref-BakryEmery1985)] gives the inequality for a density $e^{-V}$ when $D^2V\ge\kappa\mathrm{Id}$ with $\kappa>0$: in the convention used below, its constant is at most $\kappa^{-1}$. Log-concavity alone asserts only $D^2V\ge0$ when the potential is smooth. The question addressed here is whether a global assumption on the tails of linear functions can supply the logarithmic Sobolev bound without positive curvature.

A logarithmic Sobolev inequality implies Gaussian concentration for all Lipschitz functions through Herbst’s exponential-moment argument; see (Ledoux 1999, sec. 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Ledoux1999](#ref-Ledoux1999)]. Under log-concavity, Gaussian concentration for all Lipschitz functions also gives Gaussian isoperimetry by Milman’s theorem (Milman 2010, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Milman2010](#ref-Milman2010)], and hence a logarithmic Sobolev inequality (Bobkov 1999, Lemma 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Bobkov1999](#ref-Bobkov1999)], with dimension-independent bounds. The question here asks whether control of linear functions suffices. Uniformly subgaussian linear marginals are necessary, up to universal constants, by the same Herbst argument. Bizeul formulated their sufficiency within the log-concave class in 2023 (Bizeul 2026, Conjecture 2) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Bizeul2026](#ref-Bizeul2026)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> resolves this conjecture for centered log-concave laws with a Lebesgue density, under the precise marginal condition <a href="#eq:intro-subgaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-subgaussian">[eq:intro-subgaussian]</a>.

Bobkov’s norm-based criterion (Bobkov 1999, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Bobkov1999](#ref-Bobkov1999)] bounds the entropy coefficient by a universal multiple of the squared subgaussian norm of $|X|$. Under <a href="#eq:intro-subgaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-subgaussian">[eq:intro-subgaussian]</a>, Hölder’s inequality gives $\mathbb E\exp(|X|^2/(na^2))\le2$, so that criterion yields a coefficient of order $na^2$. Bizeul’s improvement (Bizeul 2026, Theorem 4) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Bizeul2026](#ref-Bizeul2026)] has dimension dependence $n^{1/4}$ in the parameter $\rho_{\mathrm{LS}}$ defined by $\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}(f^2)\le2\rho_{\mathrm{LS}}^2\int|Df|^2$. Thus for linear subgaussian scale $a$ it gives an entropy coefficient of order $\sqrt n\,a^2$. Bizeul also obtained the dimension-free conclusion for rotationally invariant log-concave laws (Bizeul 2026, Theorem 8) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Bizeul2026](#ref-Bizeul2026)]. Klartag and Lehec discuss the conjecture and these bounds in (Klartag and Lehec 2025, sec. 8.3, Conjecture 76 and Theorem 77) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[KlartagLehec2024](#ref-KlartagLehec2024)]. The theorem’s marginal hypothesis concerns more than covariance: it controls the tails of every linear function, while its conclusion controls the entropy of nonlinear tests.

Our preliminary calculus uses the Bakry–Émery semigroup argument, including its entropy dissipation identity. We give the anisotropic and approximation forms needed for Gaussian posteriors in Section <a href="#sec:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:calculus">2</a>. The other classical input to that calculus comes from Gaussian channels. Guo, Shamai, and Verdú relate the derivative of mutual information to minimum mean-square estimation error (Guo, Shamai (Shitz), and Verdú 2005, Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[GuoShamaiVerdu2005](#ref-GuoShamaiVerdu2005)]; their relative-entropy formulation (Guo, Shamai (Shitz), and Verdú 2005, Theorem 5, Equation (66)) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[GuoShamaiVerdu2005](#ref-GuoShamaiVerdu2005)] gives the Gaussian prediction identity used here. We prove its conditional integrated version with our variance and diffusion-time conventions. The entropy-loss comparison in Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-loss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-loss">17</a> requires an additional argument: repeated observations of independent copies make the cost of discarding conditional-mean directions negligible. That comparison links Gaussian prediction to the tensor near-equalities described next.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof strategy

Write $R(\nu)$ for the best constant in the convention <a id="eq:intro-lsi-convention"></a>$$\label{eq:intro-lsi-convention}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_\nu(f^2)\le2R(\nu)\int|Df|^2\,d\nu.$$ Failure of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> would give, after truncation, a weak quadratic tilt, and dilation, a sequence of compactly supported log-concave laws $\mu$ such that $R(\mu)=1$ while their linear subgaussian parameters tend to zero. The argument derives incompatible descriptions of fluctuations associated with these normalized laws.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### From an extremal function to a predictable vector field.

For a fixed $s>0$, let $G$ be a standard Gaussian independent of $X\sim\mu$. An extremizer for the entropy inequality of $X+\sqrt sG$ yields a second probability law $\eta$ and a vector field $$w=D\log\frac{d\eta}{d\mu_s},
 \qquad \mu_s=\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(X+\sqrt s\,G).$$ An exact change-of-reference identity shows that $\eta$ itself satisfies a logarithmic Sobolev inequality. The Euler equation and translation tests make $w$ an approximate first eigenfunction of the diffusion associated with $\eta$. If the entropy constant instead coincides with the Poincaré constant, take $\eta=\mu_s$ and $w=D\varphi$, where $\varphi$ is a gap eigenfunction. Both cases are treated directly.

The crucial extra information concerns prediction of $w(Y)$ from a noisy observation of $Y\sim\eta$. With $I=\mathbb E_\eta|w|^2$ and a fresh independent standard Gaussian $G$, we prove the limiting identity $$\frac{\mathbb E\big|\mathbb E[w(Y)\mid Y+\sqrt r\,G]\big|^2}{I}
 \longrightarrow e^{-r}$$ on a fixed positive interval of noise variances. It follows by matching entropy contraction with an entropy-loss comparison proved using repeated Gaussian observations. At each observation, we discard at most one direction: the difference between the conditional means under two competing priors. Independent copies make the accumulated cost of discarding these directions negligible.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### From prediction to tensor near-equalities.

Gaussian polynomial orthogonality turns the exact prediction curve into nearly sharp derivative estimates. Starting with $T_0=w$, we construct symmetric tensor fields $T_l$ whose derivatives approximate $T_{l+1}$, all with asymptotically the same squared norm and all concentrated near the first nonzero spectral value of the diffusion. The tensor estimates also control curvature and exclude concentration in the relevant small-dimensional subspaces.

For each tensor $T_l$, regard one index as a row and all remaining indices as a column, and form the positive matrix $T_lT_l^*$. Averaging $I^{-1}T_lT_l^*$ over a long band of levels gives a positive matrix field $\rho$ of asymptotic mean trace one. Its square root is nearly an equality case in Hilbert–Schmidt Poincaré. Symmetry in the many tensor indices forces the infinitesimal matrix coefficients to commute asymptotically in a weighted Hilbert–Schmidt norm. Gaussian linear combinations of these matrices, frozen at the initial diffusion point, consequently have scalar Gaussian moments under the weighted trace. A Hermite expansion along the stationary diffusion then gives covariance fluctuations with the lognormal factor $$\exp(\sqrt{8t}\,Z-4t),\qquad Z\sim N(0,1),$$ whose mean is one.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### An information contradiction.

Conditionally on the stationary point, observe a centered Gaussian vector whose covariance is a bounded increasing function of $T\rho$, where $T>0$ is a scale and the covariance cap is adjustable. Its marginal law is a mixture of these Gaussian laws. Together with the square-root energy estimates, the logarithmic Sobolev inequality of $\eta$ gives a universal asymptotic upper bound on the mutual information, integrated against $dT/T^2$. The matrix fluctuation law gives a lower bound by a one-dimensional Gaussian mixture with the lognormal variance above. After taking the sequence limit at fixed diffusion time and covariance cap, that lower bound becomes arbitrarily large as these two parameters increase together.

The scale integral is essential. In a spectral block where $\rho$ has size $\beta^2$, the substitution $x=T\beta^2$ gives $$\frac{dT}{T^2}=\beta^2\frac{dx}{x^2}.$$ This is exactly the coefficient multiplying the unnormalized trace on that block. Consequently the information lower bound retains all of the matrix mass even when the spectrum is arbitrarily diffuse.

Figure <a href="#fig:proof-dependencies" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:proof-dependencies">1</a> records the two constructions of the critical law and the main dependencies of the information comparison.

<figure id="fig:proof-dependencies">

<figcaption>Main stages of the contradiction argument. At the regularized law, the strict entropy branch means that the logarithmic Sobolev constant exceeds the Poincaré constant; in the gap branch the two constants coincide. The logarithmic Sobolev inequality of the common critical law, together with the square-root energy estimates of Section <a href="#sec:matrices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:matrices">6</a>, supplies the information upper bound. The frozen matrix law supplies the scalar lower bound. After the sequence limit at fixed parameters, its scale integral becomes arbitrarily large as the diffusion time and covariance cap grow together.</figcaption>
</figure>

```text
A directed flowchart diagram illustrating the workflow of the contradiction argument:
- Top level: Two rectangular boxes side by side:
  - Left: "Strict entropy branch \n Entropy optimizer \n Lemma 3.7(i)"
  - Right: "Gap branch \n Gap eigenfunction \n Lemma 3.7(ii)"
- Second level: A centered rectangular box:
  - "Common critical law and vector field \n Proposition 3.9"
  - Arrows point from the bottom of each top-level box to the top of this box.
- Third level: Three rectangular boxes side by side:
  - Left: "Prediction curve \n and posterior estimates \n Prop. 4.3, Lemma 4.4"
  - Center: "Symmetric \n tensor hierarchy \n Proposition 5.1"
  - Right: "Matrix estimates \n and frozen law \n Proposition 6.8"
  - Three downward arrows lead from the second-level box to each of the three third-level boxes.
  - A horizontal arrow points from the left box to the center box.
  - A horizontal arrow points from the right box to the center box.
- Fourth level: A centered rectangular box:
  - "Integrated information contradiction \n Propositions 7.2 and 7.9, \n Lemma 7.10"
  - A vertical downward arrow connects the center third-level box to this box, labeled with text "matrix bounds".
  - A horizontal incoming arrow points from the right into this fourth-level box, labeled with text "entropy bound".
```


<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Components of the argument

Two ingredients have formulations beyond the final contradiction. The entropy-loss comparison of Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-loss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-loss">17</a> relates relative entropy under common Gaussian convolution to conditional prediction of the original relative score. Its proof separates the convexity input from the finite-copy observation argument. The matrix reduction in Section <a href="#sec:matrices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:matrices">6</a> converts symmetric tensor near-equalities into a weighted scalar fluctuation law, without requiring simultaneous diagonalization in varying dimensions. Section <a href="#sec:observations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:observations">7</a> then tests that law through bounded covariance observations and integrates over scale.

For orientation, Section <a href="#sec:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:calculus">2</a> records the analytic conventions and approximation facts. Section <a href="#sec:critical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:critical">3</a> constructs the critical laws and vector fields. Section <a href="#sec:prediction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prediction">4</a> proves the prediction identity, and Section <a href="#sec:hierarchy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:hierarchy">5</a> constructs the tensor hierarchy. Sections <a href="#sec:matrices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:matrices">6</a> and <a href="#sec:observations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:observations">7</a> turn that hierarchy into the contradiction.

<a id="sec:calculus"></a>
### Curvature, diffusion, and entropy calculus

We first establish the analytic facts used to construct and study the probability measures in the proof. Two time conventions will be kept distinct: convolution with a Gaussian of variance $r$ has generator $\Delta/2$, whereas a stationary diffusion has generator $-A$ and noise coefficient $\sqrt2$. These conventions determine every entropy dissipation factor below.

For probabilities $p,\pi$ with $p\ll\pi$, define their relative entropy by $$H(p\mid\pi)=\int\log\frac{dp}{d\pi}\,dp.$$ Set $H(p\mid\pi)=+\infty$ when absolute continuity fails. Write $\gamma$ for standard Gaussian measure in the relevant dimension, and set $$\mathcal I(p\mid\pi)=4\int\left|D\sqrt{\frac{dp}{d\pi}}\right|^2d\pi.$$ The value is $+\infty$ unless the density exists and its square root belongs to the weighted Sobolev space. When the densities are positive and smooth this is $\int|D\log(dp/d\pi)|^2dp$. Our convention is $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_\pi(f^2)\le 2R(\pi)\int|Df|^2d\pi,
 \qquad 2H(p\mid\pi)\le R(\pi)\mathcal I(p\mid\pi).$$ The Poincaré constant $R^P(\pi)$ uses the convention $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\pi(f)=\int f^2d\pi-\left(\int f\,d\pi\right)^2
 \le R^P(\pi)\int|Df|^2d\pi.$$ Linearization of the entropy inequality gives $R^P(\pi)\le R(\pi)$.

<a id="eq:calculus-entropy-duality"></a>
For smooth positive densities the Sobolev space is the completion of $C_c^\infty$ in the squared norm $\int(f^2+|Df|^2)d\pi$. Cutoffs, truncation of the values, and smoothing on compact sets show that it also consists of the functions with a weak gradient in this norm. The same approximation and lower semicontinuity of entropy extend a compact-test logarithmic Sobolev inequality to this space. One convenient proof of the latter lower semicontinuity is the formula $$\label{eq:calculus-entropy-duality}
 H(p\mid\pi)=\sup_{g\ \mathrm{bounded}}
 \left\{\int g\,dp-\log\int e^g d\pi\right\}.$$ Jensen’s inequality proves the upper bound; bounded truncations of $\log(dp/d\pi)$ give equality. The same formula implies data processing under a measurable map or a Markov kernel, by conditional Jensen. Conditional entropy identities below are always averaged over the conditioning variable. In particular, disintegrating a joint density and splitting its logarithm gives the chain rule $$H(P_{U,Z}\mid Q_U\otimes\nu)
 =H(P_U\mid Q_U)+\mathbb E_{P_U}H(P_{Z\mid U}\mid\nu).$$ It holds in the extended nonnegative sense by truncation. Mutual information is $\mathsf I(U;Z)=H(P_{U,Z}\mid P_U\otimes P_Z)$. Taking $g(z)=v\cdot z$ in Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-entropy-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-entropy-duality">[eq:calculus-entropy-duality]</a> and optimizing over $v$ also gives $H(p\mid\gamma)\ge|\mathbb E_pZ|^2/2$ when the mean exists.

The following is the anisotropic Bakry–Émery curvature criterion (Bakry and Émery 1985) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[BakryEmery1985](#ref-BakryEmery1985)]. We include its semigroup proof and the convex approximation needed for Gaussian posteriors with nonsmooth support.

<a id="stmt-lem-curvature"></a>
<div id="lem:curvature" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Positive curvature). *Let $S$ be a positive definite matrix on $\mathbb R^n$, and let $U$ be a proper lower semicontinuous convex function, possibly taking the value $+\infty$. Suppose $$d\pi(x)=Z^{-1}\exp\{-\tfrac12\langle x,Sx\rangle-U(x)\}\,dx$$ is a probability with a full dimensional support. Then $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_\pi(f^2)\le2\mathbb E_\pi\langle Df,S^{-1}Df\rangle,
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\pi(f)\le\mathbb E_\pi\langle Df,S^{-1}Df\rangle.$$ The inequalities hold first for compact smooth tests, and then for functions obtained by finite-energy approximation.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-curvature"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-curvature</summary>

*Proof.* A linear change of variables reduces the assertion to $S=\mathrm{Id}$. First suppose $V(x)=|x|^2/2+U(x)$ is smooth with bounded Hessian. The diffusion with generator $L=\Delta-DV\cdot D$ has globally Lipschitz drift and invariant law $\pi$. If $J_t$ is the derivative of its trajectory with respect to the starting point, then $\dot J_t=-D^2V(X_t)J_t$, whence $\|J_t\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le e^{-t}$. For smooth bounded positive $q$, bounded away from zero, weighted Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\frac{|DP_tq|^2}{P_tq}
 \le e^{-2t}P_t\left(\frac{|Dq|^2}{q}\right).$$ Integration by parts yields $$\frac{d}{dt}\int (P_tq)\log(P_tq)\,d\pi
 =-\int\frac{|DP_tq|^2}{P_tq}\,d\pi.$$ Couple a trajectory started at $x$ to an independent stationary initial point with the same driving Brownian motion. Their distance is at most $e^{-t}$ times their initial distance. Since $\pi$ has Gaussian tails, this proves convergence of $P_tq$ to $\mathbb E_\pi q$ for bounded Lipschitz $q$, and hence convergence of its entropy to zero. Integrating the preceding two displays gives $\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_\pi(q)\le\frac12\int|Dq|^2/q\,d\pi$. Apply this to $q=f^2+\varepsilon$ and let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$.

For general $U$, take its quadratic infimal convolutions $$U_j(x)=\inf_z\{U(z)+j|x-z|^2\}.$$ The minimizer is unique, and monotonicity of the subgradient of $U$ shows that $DU_j$ is Lipschitz. The functions $U_j$ are convex and increase to $U$. A fixed affine minorant $U(z)\ge\ell\cdot z+c$ gives $U_j(x)\ge\ell\cdot x+c-|\ell|^2/(4j)$. Mollify $U_j$ with a smooth compactly supported kernel at radii tending to zero. The resulting functions are smooth, convex, and have bounded Hessian; the affine lower bound persists with a uniform additive error. Consequently their normalized densities converge almost everywhere and in $L^1$, dominated by an integrable Gaussian times an exponential of a linear function. The compact-test inequality passes to the limit. Boundary values of $U$ do not matter, since the boundary of a full dimensional convex support has Lebesgue measure zero. Finally, insert $f=1+\varepsilon g$, using finite-energy cutoffs of the constant, and compare the coefficients of $\varepsilon^2$ to obtain the variance inequality. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:calculus-convolution"></a>
We shall also use two immediate consequences with their constants. If independent laws satisfy inequalities with quadratic energy forms $Q_1,Q_2$, their product satisfies the inequality with energy $Q_1+Q_2$. Indeed, decompose entropy into conditional entropy and the entropy of the fiber integral, and use $$\left|D_x\left(\int f(x,y)^2d\pi_2(y)\right)^{1/2}\right|^2
 \le\int|D_xf(x,y)|^2d\pi_2(y).$$ Approximation removes zeros of the fiber norm. The variance version uses conditional variance and differentiation of the fiber mean. In particular, independent addition gives $$\label{eq:calculus-convolution}
 R(\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(X+Z))\le R(\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(X))+R(\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(Z)).$$ Second, Herbst’s argument (see (Ledoux 1999, sec. 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Ledoux1999](#ref-Ledoux1999)]) shows that, if $R(\pi)\le R$, every integrable $1$-Lipschitz function $u$ satisfies <a id="eq:calculus-herbst"></a>$$\label{eq:calculus-herbst}
 \log\mathbb E_\pi e^{t(u-\mathbb E_\pi u)}\le Rt^2/2.$$ For a bounded smooth $u$, applying the inequality to $e^{tu/2}$, and writing $\psi(t)=\log\mathbb Ee^{tu}$, gives $t\psi'(t)-\psi(t)\le Rt^2/2$. Integrate the derivative of $\psi(t)/t$ from zero. Bounded Lipschitz truncations and Fatou’s lemma prove the assertion in general. In particular the conclusion applies to linear forms whenever their first moments are finite.

<a id="stmt-lem-dirichlet-core"></a>
<div id="lem:dirichlet-core" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Dirichlet operator and stationary diffusion). *Let $\pi$ be a smooth, strictly positive probability density on $\mathbb R^n$. On $C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^n)$ define $$A_\pi=-\Delta-D\log\pi\cdot D.$$ This nonnegative symmetric operator is essentially self-adjoint in $L^2(\pi)$, and $C_c^\infty$ is a core for its closure. In particular, if $f\in L^2(\pi)$ and the distribution $A_\pi f$ belongs to $L^2(\pi)$, then $f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(A_\pi)$. Its semigroup $P_u=e^{-uA_\pi}$ preserves constants and is realized by the stationary diffusion <a id="eq:calculus-diffusion"></a>$$\label{eq:calculus-diffusion}
 dY_u=\sqrt2\,dB_u+D\log\pi(Y_u)\,du,
 \qquad Y_0\sim\pi.$$ For $f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(A_\pi)$ the stationary Itô formula holds in $L^2$, with drift $-A_\pi f$ and martingale integrand $\sqrt2\,Df$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dirichlet-core"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dirichlet-core</summary>

*Proof.* Integration by parts gives $\langle f,A_\pi g\rangle=\int Df\cdot Dg\,d\pi$. If $v\in L^2(\pi)$ is orthogonal to $(1+A_\pi)C_c^\infty$, then $(1+A_\pi)v=0$ distributionally. Conjugating by $\sqrt\pi$ reduces this to a Poisson equation with a smooth local potential. The local Newtonian potential estimates and bootstrap make $v$ smooth; see (Viaclovsky 2004, Lecture 24, p. 2; Lecture 16, p. 2) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Viaclovsky2004](#ref-Viaclovsky2004)]. Take a compact cutoff $\chi_R$, equal to one on the ball of radius $R$, with $|D\chi_R|\le C/R$. Testing by $\chi_R^2v$ and using $2ab\le a^2/2+2b^2$ gives $$\int\chi_R^2v^2d\pi+\tfrac12\int\chi_R^2|Dv|^2d\pi
 \le2\int|D\chi_R|^2v^2d\pi.$$ Thus $v=0$. The range of $1+A_\pi$ is dense. Nonnegativity gives $\|(1+A_\pi)f\|_2\ge\|f\|_2$, so the range of the closure is also closed and therefore equals $L^2$. The closure is self-adjoint: for $g$ in its adjoint domain, solve $(1+\overline A)h=(1+A^*)g$; then $g-h$ is in the orthogonal complement of the range of $1+A$, hence vanishes. This also proves the asserted maximal distributional domain characterization.

The cutoffs $\chi_R$ converge to $1$ in the form norm with energy tending to zero. Hence $1$ belongs to the nullspace of the closed form and $P_u1=1$. To identify the diffusion, solve the stochastic equation locally and kill it on exiting each ball. Its kernels are symmetric and sub-Markov, as follows either from the Dirichlet heat equation or integration by parts. Increasing the balls gives the minimal sub-Markov semigroup. The stopped Itô formula on compact tests identifies its generator as an extension of $-A_\pi$. Essential self-adjointness identifies it with $P_u$, which loses no mass. Started in $\pi$, the resulting diffusion is stationary and nonexplosive. Finally approximate $f$ in graph norm by compact smooth functions. Their gradients converge in $L^2(\pi)$ since $\|Dg\|_2^2=\langle g,Ag\rangle$. Stationarity and the Itô isometry pass every term of their formulas to the limit. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-entropy-calculus"></a>
<div id="lem:entropy-calculus" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:calculus-stationary-entropy"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Entropy dissipation). *For the semigroup of Lemma <a href="#lem:dirichlet-core" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dirichlet-core">4</a>, if $p_0=q_0\pi$ has finite relative entropy and $p_u=(P_uq_0)\pi$, then, for $0\le a\le b$, $$\label{eq:calculus-stationary-entropy}
 H(p_a\mid\pi)-H(p_b\mid\pi)
 \ge\int_a^b\mathcal I(p_u\mid\pi)\,du.$$ If instead both probability measures evolve by Gaussian convolution, $p_r=p_0*\gamma_r$ and $q_r=q_0*\gamma_r$, then <a id="eq:calculus-heat-entropy"></a>$$\label{eq:calculus-heat-entropy}
 H(p_a\mid q_a)-H(p_b\mid q_b)
 \ge\frac12\int_a^b\mathcal I(p_r\mid q_r)\,dr$$ whenever the entropy on the left at time $a$ is finite. Here $\gamma_r$ is the centered Gaussian with covariance $r\mathrm{Id}$, and time zero is allowed. For the standard Gaussian Ornstein–Uhlenbeck semigroup, Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-stationary-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-stationary-entropy">[eq:calculus-stationary-entropy]</a> is an equality, including time zero when the initial entropy is finite. Each assertion also holds after integration over conditioning labels whose noises are independent conditional on those labels.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-entropy-calculus"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-entropy-calculus</summary>

*Proof.* For a bounded density $q$ bounded away from zero, the form chain rule, first for smooth functions and then by form approximation, gives $$\frac{d}{du}\int(P_uq)\log(P_uq)\,d\pi
 =-\int\frac{|DP_uq|^2}{P_uq}\,d\pi.$$ Spectral smoothing puts $P_uq$ in the operator domain for $u>0$; the bounded range permits $1+\log(P_uq)$ as a form test. Approximate an arbitrary finite-entropy density by truncating it above and below and normalizing. The initial entropies converge. The semigroup contracts $L^1$, so densities converge at every later time. Lower semicontinuity of entropy and of $4\int|D\sqrt q|^2d\pi$, followed by Fatou’s lemma in time, gives Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-stationary-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-stationary-entropy">[eq:calculus-stationary-entropy]</a> from time zero. Restarting the argument gives arbitrary $a$.

<a id="eq:calculus-heat-chain"></a>
For simultaneous heat evolution put $z=p_r/q_r$. Direct differentiation, using $\partial_r p_r=\Delta p_r/2$ and the same equation for $q_r$, gives $$\label{eq:calculus-heat-chain}
 (\partial_r-\tfrac12\Delta)\{q_r\Phi(z)\}
 =-\tfrac12q_r\Phi''(z)|Dz|^2.$$ Here use smooth convex $\Phi_k$ with $\Phi_k(1)=\Phi_k'(1)=0$, with second derivatives increasing to $1/z$, and with second derivatives supported in compact subsets of $(0,\infty)$. Such functions have at most linear growth and increase to $z\log z-z+1$. Integrate Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-heat-chain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-heat-chain">[eq:calculus-heat-chain]</a> against $\chi_R$ with $|\Delta\chi_R|\le C/R^2$. At each fixed $k$ the cutoff error vanishes because $q_r\Phi_k(p_r/q_r)\le C_k(p_r+q_r)$. Let $R\to\infty$ and then $k\to\infty$. Monotone convergence gives the entropy and information terms, and in particular the stated inequality on positive time intervals. Data processing gives $H(p_\varepsilon\mid q_\varepsilon)\le H(p_0\mid q_0)$; let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ to include the initial time.

For Ornstein–Uhlenbeck evolution relative to $\gamma$, apply the same convex truncations to the ratio. Its generator is $\Delta-x\cdot D$. Expanding radial cutoffs have bounded generator, tending pointwise to zero, so the error vanishes by domination at fixed $k$. This gives equality on positive time intervals. At time zero, relative entropy is continuous: data processing gives its upper bound and lower semicontinuity under weak convergence gives its lower bound. Monotone convergence of the nonnegative time integral includes zero. Tonelli’s theorem proves all conditional versions. ◻

</details>

We use the relative-entropy form of the Gaussian-channel identity of Guo, Shamai, and Verdú (Guo, Shamai (Shitz), and Verdú 2005, Theorem 5, Equation (66)) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[GuoShamaiVerdu2005](#ref-GuoShamaiVerdu2005)]. The proof records explicitly its conditional integrated form.

<a id="stmt-lem-gaussian-signals"></a>
<div id="lem:gaussian-signals" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Gaussian signals). *Let $S$ be an $\mathbb R^d$-valued random vector with finite second moment, let $U$ be any side information, and let $G$ be standard Gaussian independent of $(S,U)$. Write $Z_t=\sqrt tS+G$ and $$d(t)=\mathbb E_U H(\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(Z_t\mid U)\mid\gamma).$$ Then $d(0)=0$, $d(t)\le t\mathbb E|S|^2/2$, and <a id="eq:calculus-gaussian-signal"></a>$$\label{eq:calculus-gaussian-signal}
 d(t)=\frac12\int_0^t
 \mathbb E\left|\mathbb E[S\mid Z_v,U]\right|^2dv.$$ The integrand is nondecreasing in $v$. The formula without side information is obtained by taking $U$ constant.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-gaussian-signals"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-gaussian-signals</summary>

*Proof.* Conditional convexity of relative entropy and $H(\gamma(\cdot-\sqrt t s)\mid\gamma)=t|s|^2/2$ give the upper bound. Gaussian kernel differentiation gives $$D_z\log\frac{d\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(Z_t\mid U)}{d\gamma}(z)
 =\sqrt t\,\mathbb E[S\mid Z_t=z,U].$$ The differentiation is justified at each positive $t$ by the Gaussian kernel and the finite second moment, or by truncating $S$ and passing locally and in the conditional squared norm. Applying Ornstein–Uhlenbeck evolution for time $u$ replaces $t$ by $te^{-2u}$. Its entropy dissipation identity and $dt/du=-2t$ give $d'(t)=\frac12\mathbb E|\mathbb E[S\mid Z_t,U]|^2$ almost everywhere on $(0,\infty)$. The upper bound implies $d(t)\to0$ at zero, proving the integrated identity. For $0<v<t$, the channel at $v$ is obtained from the channel at $t$ by multiplying its output by $\sqrt{v/t}$ and adding fresh Gaussian noise of covariance $(1-v/t)\mathrm{Id}$. Conditional Jensen then proves monotonicity of prediction energy. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:transport-consequence"></a>
#### The transport–entropy consequence

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-gaussian-signals-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-gaussian-signals</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:intro-transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:intro-transport">2</a>.* Jensen’s inequality in each coordinate gives $\int|x|^2d\mu\le na^2\log2$, so $\mu\in\mathcal P_2(\mathbb R^n)$. Only the case $H(\nu\mid\mu)<\infty$ needs proof. For $s>0$, put $\gamma_s=N(0,s\mathrm{Id})$, $\mu_s=\mu*\gamma_s$, and $\nu_s=\nu*\gamma_s$. The density of $\mu_s$ is smooth and strictly positive. For $X\sim\mu$ and an independent standard Gaussian $G$, its potential $V_s$ satisfies $$D^2V_s(y)=s^{-1}\mathrm{Id}-s^{-2}\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(X\mid X+\sqrt sG=y)\ge0.$$ Indeed, the posterior has the original convex potential plus $|x-y|^2/(2s)$, so Lemma <a href="#lem:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curvature">3</a> bounds its covariance by $s\mathrm{Id}$. Gaussian differentiation is valid without compact support. Thus $\mu_s$ is centered and log-concave, with a smooth convex potential.

Set $\beta_s=\sqrt{8s/3}$ and $a_s=a+\beta_s$. For a scalar standard Gaussian $Z$, $\mathbb E\exp(sZ^2/\beta_s^2)=2$. The inequality $$\frac{(u+t)^2}{(a+\beta_s)^2}
 \le\frac{a}{a+\beta_s}\frac{u^2}{a^2}
     +\frac{\beta_s}{a+\beta_s}\frac{t^2}{\beta_s^2}$$ and Hölder’s inequality show that $\mu_s$ satisfies <a href="#eq:intro-subgaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-subgaussian">[eq:intro-subgaussian]</a> with parameter $a_s$. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, with the Sobolev extension in Section <a href="#sec:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:calculus">2</a>, gives entropy coefficient $Ca_s^2$. In the conventions of (Otto and Villani 2000, Definitions 1–2) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[OttoVillani2000](#ref-OttoVillani2000)], $\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}(f^2)\le L\int|Df|^2$ is $\mathrm{LSI}(2/L)$, whose transport conclusion is $W_2^2\le LH$. Their Theorem 1 applies to the smooth convex potential of $\mu_s$, and hence $$W_2(\nu_s,\mu_s)^2\le Ca_s^2H(\nu_s\mid\mu_s)
 \le Ca_s^2H(\nu\mid\mu).$$ The last inequality is data processing for the common Gaussian kernel, as in Section <a href="#sec:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:calculus">2</a>. Coupling a vector with itself plus independent Gaussian noise gives $W_2(\mu_s,\mu),W_2(\nu_s,\nu)\le\sqrt{ns}$. Letting $s\downarrow0$ at fixed $n$, with $a_s\to a$, proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:critical"></a>
### Critical laws after Gaussian regularization

Suppose that the conclusion of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is false. This section constructs, from a sequence of counterexamples, smooth probabilities $\eta$ satisfying a logarithmic Sobolev inequality and vector fields $w$ that nearly satisfy $A_\eta w=w$. There are two possible constructions. An attained nonlinear entropy quotient gives a score $w$; equality of the logarithmic Sobolev and Poincaré constants gives the gradient of a gap eigenfunction. We shall retain both alternatives throughout.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Normalization and Gaussian regularization

<a id="stmt-lem-critical-normalization"></a>
<div id="lem:critical-normalization" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:critical-normalization"></a>
<a id="eq:critical-linear-mgf"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Normalized counterexamples). *Failure of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> produces a sequence of centered, compactly supported, full dimensional log-concave probabilities $\mu$ such that $$\label{eq:critical-normalization}
 R(\mu)=1,\qquad
 \sup_{|\theta|=1}\mathbb E_\mu e^{\langle X,\theta\rangle^2/a^2}
 \le2,\qquad a\longrightarrow0.$$ For an absolute constant $C$, they also satisfy $$\label{eq:critical-linear-mgf}
 \log\mathbb E_\mu e^{v\cdot X}\le Ca^2|v|^2,
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_\mu(X)\le Ca^2\mathrm{Id}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-critical-normalization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-critical-normalization</summary>

*Proof.* Choose laws and compact smooth tests for which the entropy-to-energy ratio divided by the squared marginal parameter tends to infinity. The tests may be chosen with positive energy: a smooth test of zero energy is constant on the connected interior of the convex support, and its entropy vanishes. Restriction of the law to a sufficiently large ball preserves each test quotient arbitrarily closely. Multiply the restricted density by $e^{-\varepsilon|x|^2/2}$ and normalize, with $\varepsilon>0$ sufficiently small to preserve that quotient again. Both operations preserve log-concavity, their combined density relative to the original law can be bounded by $2$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curvature">3</a> gives a finite logarithmic Sobolev constant for the resulting compact law.

A bounded density increase changes the exponential-square expectation by at most that factor. If $\mathbb Ee^{Z^2/A^2}\le4$, Jensen gives $\mathbb Ee^{Z^2/(2A^2)}\le2$. Centering costs only another absolute factor: $\mathbb EZ^2\le A^2\log4$, while $(Z-\mathbb EZ)^2\le2Z^2+2(\mathbb EZ)^2$; increase the denominator first to make the resulting exponential expectation bounded by an absolute constant, and once more by Jensen to make it at most $2$. These arguments apply separately to every unit linear form with the same constants. Translation preserves the test quotient. Thus the centered compact laws still have $R/A^2\to\infty$ with $0<R<\infty$. Dilation by $R^{-1/2}$ gives Equation <a href="#eq:critical-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-normalization">[eq:critical-normalization]</a>.

For completeness, if a centered scalar $Z$ obeys $\mathbb Ee^{Z^2/a^2}\le2$, then $\mathbb E|Z|^k\le2a^k\Gamma(k/2+1)$, by integrating its tail. Expansion of $e^{tZ}$, with the linear term absent, therefore gives $\log\mathbb Ee^{tZ}\le Ca^2t^2$ for $a|t|\le c$. For $a|t|\ge c$, use $tZ\le Z^2/a^2+a^2t^2/4$ and absorb $\log2$ into $Ca^2t^2$. Apply this to $Z=X\cdot\theta$. The second moment bound follows either from the exponential-square bound or by differentiating the centered moment generating function at zero. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:critical-posterior"></a>
We suppress the counterexample index, but all limits in this section refer to this sequence. Let $G$ be a fresh standard Gaussian and set $$\mu_s=\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(X+\sqrt sG),\quad R_s=R(\mu_s),\quad
 b_s(y)=\mathbb E[X\mid X+\sqrt sG=y],\quad V_s=-\log\mu_s.$$ Here and below $\mu_s$ also denotes its strictly positive density. Gaussian differentiation and posterior covariance give $$\label{eq:critical-posterior}
 DV_s(y)=\frac{y-b_s(y)}s,\qquad
 M_s(y)=Db_s(y)=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(X\mid y)}s,\qquad
 K_s(y)=D^2V_s(y)=\frac{\mathrm{Id}-M_s(y)}s.$$ The posterior of $X$ has a convex potential plus $|x-y|^2/(2s)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curvature">3</a>, applied to its linear forms, therefore implies <a id="eq:critical-curvature"></a>$$\label{eq:critical-curvature}
 0\le M_s\le\mathrm{Id},\qquad 0\le K_s\le s^{-1}\mathrm{Id}.$$ All these functions are smooth. Compact support of $X$ also gives $|b_s|\le\sup_{\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu}|x|$.

The monotonicity below follows from the heat-flow contraction of Klartag and Putterman (Klartag and Putterman 2023, Theorem 1.2 and Section 3) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[KlartagPutterman2023](#ref-KlartagPutterman2023)], which uses the transport construction of Kim and Milman (Kim and Milman 2012) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[KimMilman2012](#ref-KimMilman2012)]. We recall the argument for our regularized laws.

<a id="stmt-lem-critical-flow-bounds"></a>
<div id="lem:critical-flow-bounds" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Bounds for the regularized constants). *The function $s\mapsto R_s$ is nondecreasing and $1$-Lipschitz on $(0,\infty)$, and <a id="eq:critical-basic-R"></a>$$\label{eq:critical-basic-R}
 1\le R_s\le1+s.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-critical-flow-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-critical-flow-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* The heat equation for $\mu_s$ is the continuity equation with velocity $DV_s/2$. On a compact positive-time interval this velocity is globally Lipschitz and grows at most linearly, so its smooth flow is a diffeomorphism carrying $\mu_s$ to $\mu_t$. Its Jacobian satisfies $\dot J=K_tJ/2$. By positivity of $K_t$, the flow expands distances and its inverse is $1$-Lipschitz when $t\ge s$. Transport a test by that inverse. Its entropy is unchanged and its Dirichlet energy cannot increase, proving $R_t\ge R_s$. Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-convolution">[eq:calculus-convolution]</a> gives $R_{s+h}\le R_s+h$ and $R_s\le1+s$. For every compact smooth test, its entropy and energy converge to those under $\mu$ as $s\downarrow0$. Taking the supremum of its quotient gives $\liminf_{s\downarrow0}R_s\ge R(\mu)=1$. Monotonicity now proves the remaining bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Attainment above the Gaussian and Poincaré thresholds

On compact manifolds, Rothaus obtains nonconstant positive solutions of the logarithmic Euler equation when the sharp logarithmic Sobolev constant exceeds the Poincaré constant (Rothaus 1986, 358) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Rothaus1986](#ref-Rothaus1986)]. The proof below must also control loss of entropy in the Gaussian tails. The next two lemmas establish this control and attainment. Their constants may depend on a single fixed law and its dimension. Uniform estimates along the counterexample sequence begin with the translation argument.

<a id="stmt-lem-critical-defect"></a>
<div id="lem:critical-defect" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:critical-defective"></a>
<a id="eq:critical-entropy-defect"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Compactness and the Gaussian entropy defect). *Fix $s>0$ and a compactly supported probability $\mu$ on $\mathbb R^n$, and put $\nu=\mu*\gamma_s$. Its form domain embeds compactly into $L^2(\nu)$. For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is a finite $C_\varepsilon$, depending on this fixed law, such that $$\label{eq:critical-defective}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_\nu(u^2)\le2(s+\varepsilon)\|Du\|_2^2
                  +C_\varepsilon\|u\|_2^2.$$ If $u_j\to u$ in $L^2(\nu)$, $\|u_j\|_2=1$, and $\|Du_j\|_2^2\to E_*<\infty$, then $$\label{eq:critical-entropy-defect}
 \limsup_j\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_\nu(u_j^2)
 \le\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_\nu(u^2)+2s(E_*-\|Du\|_2^2).$$*

</div>
