<details id="proof-stmt-lem-critical-defect"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-critical-defect</summary>

*Proof.* Write $r=d\nu/d\gamma_s$. The Gaussian kernel formula gives $D\log r=b_s/s$, which is bounded, and hence $|\log r(y)|\le C(1+|y|)$. For $v=u\sqrt r$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_\nu(u^2)=\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_{\gamma_s}(v^2)
                         -\int v^2\log r\,d\gamma_s,
 \qquad Dv=\sqrt r\,(Du+\tfrac12uD\log r).$$ Gaussian integration by parts followed by Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\int |y|^2v^2d\gamma_s
 =sn\|v\|_2^2+2s\int v\,y\cdot Dv\,d\gamma_s
 \le2sn\|v\|_2^2+4s^2\|Dv\|_2^2.$$ Initially this calculation is for compact tests, and cutoffs extend it by lower semicontinuity. The linear-growth entropy correction is consequently bounded by $\delta\|Dv\|_2^2+C_\delta\|v\|_2^2$ for every $\delta>0$. Gaussian logarithmic Sobolev and the displayed derivative formula, with another arbitrarily small Cauchy–Schwarz parameter, prove Equation <a href="#eq:critical-defective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-defective">[eq:critical-defective]</a>. The same moment estimate shows tightness of the $L^2(\nu)$ mass of any bounded form sequence. On a fixed ball, the smooth density is bounded above and away from zero, so the ordinary local Rellich theorem gives $L^2$ precompactness. A diagonal extraction and tightness prove compactness globally.

<a id="eq:critical-tail-energy"></a>
We give the tail argument for the sharp coefficient $2s$ in Equation <a href="#eq:critical-entropy-defect" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-entropy-defect">[eq:critical-entropy-defect]</a>. Put $v_{j,L}=(|u_j|-L)_+$ and $m_{j,L}=\|v_{j,L}\|_2^2$. Strong $L^2$ convergence implies $\lim_{L\to\infty}\sup_jm_{j,L}=0$. The energies of $v_{j,L}$ and $|u_j|\wedge L$ sum to $\|Du_j\|_2^2$. Lower semicontinuity for the clipped functions therefore gives $$\label{eq:critical-tail-energy}
 \lim_{L\to\infty}\limsup_j\|Dv_{j,L}\|_2^2
 \le E_*-\|Du\|_2^2.$$ For a nonnegative $v$ of squared norm $m\le1$, the negative part of $v^2\log v^2$ has integral at most $C\sqrt m$ (use $-z\log z\le C\sqrt z$ on $0\le z\le1$). Since $m\log m\le0$, Equation <a href="#eq:critical-defective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-defective">[eq:critical-defective]</a> thus gives $$\int v^2\log_+v^2d\nu
 \le2(s+\varepsilon)\|Dv\|_2^2
          +C_\varepsilon m+C\sqrt m.$$ If $|u_j|>ML$, where $M>1$ is fixed, then $|u_j|\le c_M v_{j,L}$ with $c_M=M/(M-1)$. Consequently $$u_j^2\log_+u_j^2
 \le c_M^2v_{j,L}^2\log_+v_{j,L}^2
                +c_M^2\log(c_M^2)v_{j,L}^2.$$ The integrals of bounded truncations of $z^2\log z^2$ pass to the limit by strong $L^2$ convergence; its negative part does as well, being bounded and continuous. The last two bounds and Equation <a href="#eq:critical-tail-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-tail-energy">[eq:critical-tail-energy]</a>, with limits in the order $j$, $L$, $M$, $\varepsilon$, show that the lost positive entropy is at most $2s(E_*-\|Du\|_2^2)$. The normalization terms are zero because $\|u\|_2=\|u_j\|_2=1$. ◻

</details>

The threshold $s$ in the entropy-defect estimate controls escape into the Gaussian tails. The Poincaré constant controls maximizing sequences that converge to constants. The next lemma proves attainment when the logarithmic Sobolev constant exceeds both thresholds.

<a id="stmt-lem-optimizer"></a>
<div id="lem:optimizer" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:critical-optimizer"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Existence and positivity of an optimizer). *Let $\nu=\mu*\gamma_s$, where $s>0$ and $\mu$ is compactly supported. Suppose its logarithmic Sobolev and Poincaré constants $R=R(\nu)$ and $P=R^P(\nu)$ satisfy $\infty>R>\max(P,s)$. Then there is a smooth strictly positive nonconstant $f$ with $$\label{eq:critical-optimizer}
 \int f^2d\nu=1,\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_\nu(f^2)=2R\int|Df|^2d\nu,\qquad
 A_\nu f=\lambda f\log f,\quad\lambda=R^{-1}.$$ The last equation is initially distributional and then classical.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-optimizer"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-optimizer</summary>

*Proof.* Take nonnegative unit-norm maximizing tests $f_j$. Replacing a test by its absolute value preserves entropy and energy, so this choice loses nothing. Equation <a href="#eq:critical-defective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-defective">[eq:critical-defective]</a>, with $s+\varepsilon<R$, bounds their energies. We first exclude energies tending to zero; this is the only place the strict inequality $R>P$ is required.

<a id="eq:critical-small-energy-start"></a>
<a id="eq:critical-scalar-Q"></a>
Suppose $\varepsilon_j^2=\|Df_j\|_2^2\to0$, and write $c_j=\mathbb E_\nu f_j$ and $h_j=(f_j-c_j)/\varepsilon_j$. Poincaré gives $\|h_j\|_2^2\le P$, while $\|Dh_j\|_2^2=1$ and $1-c_j^2=\varepsilon_j^2\|h_j\|_2^2$. Thus $c_j\to1$. By compactness, after extraction, $h_j\to h$ in $L^2$, where $$\mathbb Eh=0,\qquad \|Dh\|_2^2\le1,\qquad
 \|h\|_2^2\le P\|Dh\|_2^2.$$ For $c>0$, $\varepsilon>0$ and $z\ge-c/\varepsilon$ define $$Q_{c,\varepsilon}(z)=\varepsilon^{-2}
 \left[(c+\varepsilon z)^2\log\frac{(c+\varepsilon z)^2}{c^2}
                  -(c+\varepsilon z)^2+c^2\right].$$ Normalization and $-\log c_j^2-1+c_j^2\ge0$ give $$\label{eq:critical-small-energy-start}
 \varepsilon_j^{-2}\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}(f_j^2)
 \le\int Q_{c_j,\varepsilon_j}(h_j)\,d\nu.$$ Here is the scalar estimate needed to control unbounded $h_j$. For $1/2\le c\le1$ and $0<\varepsilon\le1/2$, $$\label{eq:critical-scalar-Q}
 0\le Q_{c,\varepsilon}(z)
 \le(2+2\log2)z^2+z^2\log_+z^2,
 \qquad Q_{c,\varepsilon}(z)\longrightarrow2z^2$$ uniformly on bounded $z$-intervals as $c\to1$ and $\varepsilon\to0$. To verify the bound, put $t=\varepsilon z/c$. Then $Q=z^2B(t)$, where Taylor’s integral formula gives $$B(t)=2+4\int_0^1(1-r)\log(1+rt)\,dr,
 \qquad -1\le t<\infty.$$ For $t\le0$, $B(t)\le2$. For $t\ge0$, use $t\le z$ to obtain $B(t)\le2+2\log(1+z)\le2+2\log2+\log_+z^2$. Continuity at $t=0$ proves the compact convergence.

<a id="eq:critical-general-tail"></a>
The tail proof in Lemma <a href="#lem:critical-defect" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:critical-defect">9</a> did not use unit norm until its last sentence. In particular, for any bounded form sequence $u_j\to u$ in $L^2$ with energies tending to $E_*$, it proves $$\label{eq:critical-general-tail}
 \lim_{K\to\infty}\limsup_j
 \int_{|u_j|>K}u_j^2\log_+u_j^2d\nu
 \le2s(E_*-\|Du\|_2^2).$$ Apply the compact convergence in Equation <a href="#eq:critical-scalar-Q" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-scalar-Q">[eq:critical-scalar-Q]</a> on $|h_j|\le K$ and its upper bound on the complement. Strong $L^2$ convergence removes the quadratic tail as $K\to\infty$; Equation <a href="#eq:critical-general-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-general-tail">[eq:critical-general-tail]</a> controls the entropy tail. Equation <a href="#eq:critical-small-energy-start" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-small-energy-start">[eq:critical-small-energy-start]</a> consequently gives $$\limsup_j\varepsilon_j^{-2}\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}(f_j^2)
 \le2\|h\|_2^2+2s(1-\|Dh\|_2^2)
 \le2\max(P,s)<2R.$$ This contradicts the maximizing property.

We may therefore extract $f_j\to f$ strongly in $L^2$ with $\|Df_j\|_2^2\to E_*>0$. Put $E=\|Df\|_2^2$. The entropy defect and the sharp inequality give $$2RE_*\le\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}(f^2)+2s(E_*-E)
         \le2RE+2s(E_*-E).$$ Since $R>s$ and $E\le E_*$, equality forces $E=E_*>0$. Thus $f$ is a nonnegative, nonconstant optimizer.

The homogeneous functional $\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}(g^2)-2R\|Dg\|_2^2$ is nonpositive and is zero at $f$. Its derivative along every compact smooth variation $\zeta$ is $$4\int f\log f\,\zeta\,d\nu
       -4R\int Df\cdot D\zeta\,d\nu=0.$$ The entropy normalization term cancels the additional linear term. Differentiation is legitimate also at zeros because $z^2\log z^2$ has derivative zero at zero. This proves the Euler equation in Equation <a href="#eq:critical-optimizer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-optimizer">[eq:critical-optimizer]</a>.

We have obtained a nonnegative optimizer with positive energy. To use its logarithmic derivative, we still need local regularity and strict positivity. We spell out the weak-solution regularity step, using the interior Newtonian-potential $L^p$ and Schauder estimates; see (Viaclovsky 2004, Lecture 24, p. 2; Lecture 10, Proposition 1) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Viaclovsky2004](#ref-Viaclovsky2004)]. Write $\nu=e^{-V}$ and set $v=e^{-V/2}f\ge0$. The function $V$ is smooth, and direct differentiation of the weak equation gives $$-\Delta v=\lambda v\log v+
 \left(\frac{\lambda V}{2}-\frac{|DV|^2}{4}
                         +\frac{\Delta V}{2}\right)v.$$ The conjugating factor and its reciprocal are bounded on each fixed ball. Since $|z\log z|\le C_\delta(1+z^{1+\delta})$ for $z\ge0$, local $L^p$ integrability of $v$ puts the right side in $L^q_{\rm loc}$, where $q=p/(1+\delta)>1$. Localize that right side on a larger ball and take its Newtonian potential with the sign solving $-\Delta u=F$. The cited $L^q$ estimate gives $u\in W^{2,q}_{\rm loc}$; the difference $v-u$ is distributionally harmonic and hence smooth by Weyl’s lemma (Viaclovsky 2004, Lecture 3, Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Viaclovsky2004](#ref-Viaclovsky2004)]. This proves the required $W^{2,q}$ gain directly for the weak solution.

For $n\ge2$, start with $p=2$ and choose $0<\delta<\min(1,2/n)$. Before boundedness is reached, Sobolev embedding improves reciprocal integrability from $1/p$ to $(1+\delta)/p-2/n$, a decrease greater than $1/n$ because $p\ge2$. At a critical exponent use any sufficiently large finite integrability exponent and continue once more. After finitely many steps $q>n/2$, so $v$, and thus $f$, is locally bounded and Hölder continuous. In dimension one the same conclusion follows by twice integrating the local Poisson equation. The function $z\log z$ is locally Hölder of every exponent below one on $[0,\infty)$; reducing the exponent if necessary, the Schauder estimate therefore gives $f\in C^{2,\alpha}_{\rm loc}$ for some $\alpha>0$. All constants in this paragraph concern one fixed law and dimension; none enters a uniform estimate along the counterexample sequence.

If $f(x_0)=0$, choose a ball $B$ about $x_0$ with $0\le f\le b<1$ on its closure. Start the $\nu$-diffusion at $x_0$ and stop at its exit time $\tau$ from $B$. For $Z_t=f(Y_{t\wedge\tau})$, $m(t)=\mathbb EZ_t$, and $g(z)=-\lambda z\log z$, the stopped Itô formula gives, for almost every $t$, $$m'(t)=\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_{t<\tau}g(Z_t)]\le\mathbb Eg(Z_t)\le g(m(t)),
 \qquad m(0)=0.$$ Indeed $g$ is nonnegative and concave on $[0,b]$; the first inequality adds its nonnegative stopped boundary contribution. Also $m'\ge0$. If $m(t_1)>0$, let $t_a$ be the first time at which $m=a$, for $0<a<m(t_1)$. Integration gives $$\int_a^{m(t_1)}\frac{dz}{-\lambda z\log z}
 \le t_1-t_a\le t_1,$$ contradicting divergence as $a\downarrow0$. Hence $m=0$. The killed nondegenerate diffusion has positive probability of reaching each open subset of $B$ while staying in $B$: one can follow a smooth interior path by requiring Brownian motion to remain in a small tube about its corresponding driving path, and use local Lipschitz continuity of the drift. Brownian tube probabilities are positive by its finite increment densities and bridge continuity. Consequently $f$ vanishes throughout $B$. Its zero set is both open and closed, impossible in connected $\mathbb R^n$ for a unit-norm function. Thus $f>0$, and smooth elliptic bootstrap makes $f$ smooth everywhere. ◻

</details>

An optimizer supplies a second probability with its own logarithmic Sobolev inequality. The next identity explains why no convexity assumption on this second probability is needed.

<div class="samepage">

<a id="stmt-lem-critical-change-reference"></a>
<div id="lem:critical-change-reference" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:critical-score-equation"></a>
<a id="eq:critical-curvature-mean"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Change of reference at an optimizer). *Under Lemma <a href="#lem:optimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:optimizer">10</a>, suppose in addition that $\nu=e^{-V}$ is log-concave with bounded $K=D^2V$. Define $$\eta=f^2\nu,\qquad h=\log f^2,\qquad w=Dh,\qquad
 I=\mathbb E_\eta|w|^2.$$ Then $I=2\lambda H(\eta\mid\nu)>0$, $R(\eta)\le1/\lambda$, each component of $w$ belongs to $\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(A_\eta)$, and $$\label{eq:critical-score-equation}
 A_\nu h=\lambda h+\tfrac12|w|^2,
 \qquad A_\eta w=(\lambda\mathrm{Id}-K)w.$$ Moreover, $$\label{eq:critical-curvature-mean}
 \mathbb E_\eta\langle w,Kw\rangle\le\lambda|\mathbb E_\eta w|^2.$$*

</div>

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-critical-change-reference"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-critical-change-reference</summary>

*Proof.* Since $w=2Df/f$, $I=4\|Df\|_{L^2(\nu)}^2$; optimizer equality gives $I=2\lambda H(\eta\mid\nu)>0$. The chain rule applied to its Euler equation gives the first identity in Equation <a href="#eq:critical-score-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-score-equation">[eq:critical-score-equation]</a>. Commuting one derivative with $A_\nu$ gives $$A_\nu w=D(A_\nu h)-Kw
       =\lambda w+(Dw)w-Kw.$$ Since $A_\eta=A_\nu-w\cdot D$ and $Dw$ is symmetric, the second identity follows distributionally. Its right side is in $L^2(\eta)$, since $K$ is bounded and $w\in L^2(\eta)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:dirichlet-core" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dirichlet-core">4</a> therefore puts $w$ in the operator domain; no unproved integrability of higher derivatives has been used.

<a id="eq:critical-exact-deficit"></a>
Let $p=g^2\eta$ have unit mass, where $g$ is compactly supported and smooth. Direct expansion and compactly supported integration by parts give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal I(p\mid\nu)
 &=\mathcal I(p\mid\eta)+\int g^2|w|^2d\eta
                    +2\int D(g^2)\cdot w\,d\eta\\
 &=\mathcal I(p\mid\eta)+\int g^2\{|w|^2+2A_\eta h\}\,d\eta\\
 &=\mathcal I(p\mid\eta)+2\lambda\int h\,dp.
\end{aligned}$$ These formulas may be read in square-root form at zeros of $g$. On the other hand $H(p\mid\nu)=H(p\mid\eta)+\int h\,dp$. Thus $$\label{eq:critical-exact-deficit}
 \mathcal I(p\mid\nu)-2\lambda H(p\mid\nu)
 =\mathcal I(p\mid\eta)-2\lambda H(p\mid\eta).$$ Apply the inequality for $\nu$ to the compact smooth function $gf$. Its deficit is nonnegative, so the right side is nonnegative. Homogeneity and the Sobolev approximation of Section <a href="#sec:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:calculus">2</a> prove $R(\eta)\le1/\lambda$. Finally the domain identity gives $$\mathbb E_\eta|Dw|^2=\lambda I-\mathbb E_\eta\langle w,Kw\rangle,$$ while Poincaré for $\eta$ gives the lower bound $\lambda(I-|\mathbb E_\eta w|^2)$. Subtract to obtain Equation <a href="#eq:critical-curvature-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-curvature-mean">[eq:critical-curvature-mean]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Uniform estimates in the two alternatives

<a id="stmt-lem-translation-gain"></a>
<div id="lem:translation-gain" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:critical-translation-gain"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Translation gain). *Let $\mu$ satisfy Equation <a href="#eq:critical-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-normalization">[eq:critical-normalization]</a>, with $a\le1$. Fix a compact interval $J\subset(0,1/2)$. There is a constant $C_J$, independent of the dimension and of the support radius of $\mu$, with the following property. For $s\in J$, put $\lambda=R_s^{-1}$. If $\sigma$ is any probability with $$D_0=H(\sigma\mid\mu_s)<\infty,\qquad
 F_0=\mathcal I(\sigma\mid\mu_s)<\infty,\qquad p=\mathbb E_\sigma Y,$$ then $$\label{eq:critical-translation-gain}
 F_0-2\lambda D_0
 \ge(s^{-2}-\lambda/s)|p|^2
                   -C_J\sqrt a\,(F_0+D_0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-translation-gain"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-translation-gain</summary>

<a id="eq:critical-entropic-means"></a>
*Proof.* The centered linear moment generating functions under $\mu_s$ obey $$\log\mathbb E_{\mu_s}e^{v\cdot Y}\le C_J|v|^2,
 \qquad
 \log\mathbb E_{\mu_s}e^{v\cdot b_s(Y)}\le Ca^2|v|^2.$$ The first follows by independent Gaussian addition and Equation <a href="#eq:critical-linear-mgf" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-linear-mgf">[eq:critical-linear-mgf]</a>; the second follows by conditional Jensen from that equation. Entropy duality, optimized over the scalar multiple of each unit vector, gives $$\label{eq:critical-entropic-means}
 |p|\le C_J\sqrt{D_0},\qquad
 |\mathbb E_\sigma b_s(Y)|\le Ca\sqrt{D_0}.$$ All expectations exist. More explicitly, compact support of $X$ makes $\mathbb E_{\mu_s}e^{c|Y|^2}<\infty$ for some $c>0$; entropy duality then implies $\mathbb E_\sigma|Y|^2<\infty$. The latter fact is used only for integrability, not for a uniform bound in dimension.

<a id="eq:critical-translated-entropy"></a>
Let $\sigma_u$ be the translate of $\sigma$ by $-up$, $0\le u\le1$, and let $D_u=H(\sigma_u\mid\mu_s)$. Change of variables in entropy and the Hessian bound give $$\begin{aligned}
 D_u
 &=D_0+\mathbb E_\sigma[V_s(Y-up)-V_s(Y)]\\
 &\le D_0-u|p|^2/s+u\,p\cdot\mathbb E_\sigma b_s(Y)/s
                                +u^2|p|^2/(2s)
 \le C_JD_0.
\end{aligned}$$ Thus Equation <a href="#eq:critical-entropic-means" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-entropic-means">[eq:critical-entropic-means]</a>, applied to $\sigma_u$, implies $|\mathbb E_\sigma b_s(Y-up)|\le C_Ja\sqrt{D_0}$ uniformly in $u$. Differentiating the exact entropy expression, and then integrating, gives $$\label{eq:critical-translated-entropy}
 D_1=D_0-\frac{|p|^2}{2s}+O_J(aD_0).$$ Indeed its derivative is $-(1-u)|p|^2/s+p\cdot\mathbb E_\sigma b_s(Y-up)/s$.

<a id="eq:critical-score-mean"></a>
Write $r(Y)=D\log(d\sigma/d\mu_s)(Y)$ in the weak square-root sense. Its mean is $$\label{eq:critical-score-mean}
 \mathbb E_\sigma r=\mathbb E_\sigma DV_s=(p-\mathbb E_\sigma b_s)/s.$$ To justify this for finite Fisher information, the Lebesgue density $q$ of $\sigma$ has weak derivative $Dq=q(r-DV_s)\in L^1$: $r$ is square integrable and $DV_s$ has linear growth. Integrating against expanding cutoffs gives $\int Dq=0$ and hence Equation <a href="#eq:critical-score-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-score-mean">[eq:critical-score-mean]</a>.

<a id="eq:critical-translated-information"></a>
In the moving coordinates $Y$, the score of $\sigma_1$ relative to $\mu_s$ is $$r(Y)-p/s+d(Y)/s,\qquad d(Y)=b_s(Y)-b_s(Y-p).$$ Since $0\le Db_s\le\mathrm{Id}$, integration along the segment gives $$|d(Y)|^2
 =\left|\int_0^1M_s(Y-up)p\,du\right|^2
 \le\int_0^1|M_s(Y-up)p|^2du
 \le p\cdot d(Y).$$ The mean estimates at the two endpoints therefore imply <a id="eq:critical-posterior-translation-error"></a>$$\label{eq:critical-posterior-translation-error}
 \mathbb E_\sigma|d|^2\le C_JaD_0.$$ Expanding the score square and using Equation <a href="#eq:critical-score-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-score-mean">[eq:critical-score-mean]</a>, $$\mathbb E_\sigma|r-p/s|^2
 =F_0-|p|^2/s^2+2p\cdot\mathbb E_\sigma b_s/s^2.$$ The remaining cross term is bounded by $C_J\sqrt{aD_0}\,(\sqrt{F_0}+\sqrt{D_0})$, and the $d$-square term by $C_JaD_0$. Consequently the translated information is finite and satisfies $$\label{eq:critical-translated-information}
 F_1=F_0-|p|^2/s^2+O_J(\sqrt a\,(F_0+D_0)).$$ These calculations require only the weak derivatives already justified, so they apply also when the density of $\sigma$ vanishes. Apply logarithmic Sobolev to $\sigma_1$, namely $F_1\ge2\lambda D_1$, and combine Equations <a href="#eq:critical-translated-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-translated-entropy">[eq:critical-translated-entropy]</a> and <a href="#eq:critical-translated-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-translated-information">[eq:critical-translated-information]</a>. Since $2/3\le\lambda\le1$ on the time interval in question, this proves Equation <a href="#eq:critical-translation-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-translation-gain">[eq:critical-translation-gain]</a>. ◻

</details>

We can now obtain estimates that are uniform along the counterexample sequence. Write $R_s^P$ for the optimal Poincaré constant of $\mu_s$. By linearization $R_s^P\le R_s$. Since $R_s\ge1>s$ on $(0,1/2)$, the strict alternative $R_s>R_s^P$ meets all the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:optimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:optimizer">10</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-critical-two-alternatives"></a>
<div id="lem:critical-two-alternatives" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Critical fields in both alternatives). *Fix $J\subset(0,1/2)$ compact and $s\in J$, and put $\lambda=1/R_s$. There are a smooth positive probability $\eta$ and a smooth vector field $w$, with $0<I:=\mathbb E_\eta|w|^2<\infty$, such that <a id="eq:critical-common-identities"></a>$$\label{eq:critical-common-identities}
 R(\eta)\le\lambda^{-1},\qquad
 w\in\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(A_\eta)^n,\qquad
 A_\eta w=(\lambda\mathrm{Id}-K_s)w.$$ They can be chosen as follows.*

1.  *If $R_s>R_s^P$, take the optimizer $f$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:optimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:optimizer">10</a> and set $$\eta=f^2\mu_s,\qquad w=D\log f^2,
     \qquad I=2\lambda H(\eta\mid\mu_s).$$*

2.  *If $R_s=R_s^P$, there is a centered nonconstant gap eigenfunction $\varphi$ satisfying $A_{\mu_s}\varphi=\lambda\varphi$. Take $$\eta=\mu_s,\qquad w=D\varphi,
     \qquad I=\lambda\mathbb E_{\mu_s}\varphi^2.$$*

<a id="eq:critical-uniform-smallness"></a>
*In either alternative, $$\label{eq:critical-uniform-smallness}
 |\mathbb E_\eta w|^2+\mathbb E_\eta|K_sw|^2\le C_J\sqrt a\,I,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E_\eta\langle w,K_sw\rangle\le\lambda|\mathbb E_\eta w|^2.$$ In alternative <span class="upright">(ii)</span> the factor $\sqrt a$ can be replaced by $a^2$. Coordinates belong to $\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(A_\eta)$ in both alternatives.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-critical-two-alternatives"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-critical-two-alternatives</summary>

*Proof.* In the first alternative the identities and the last inequality are Lemma <a href="#lem:critical-change-reference" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:critical-change-reference">11</a>. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:translation-gain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:translation-gain">12</a> to $\sigma=\eta$, where $F_0=I$ and $D_0=I/(2\lambda)$, so the deficit vanishes. The coefficient $s^{-2}-\lambda/s$ is bounded below by a positive constant on $J$. Thus, with $p=\mathbb E_\eta Y$, $$|p|^2\le C_J\sqrt a\,I,
 \qquad |\mathbb E_\eta b_s|\le C_Ja\sqrt I.$$ Equation <a href="#eq:critical-score-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-score-mean">[eq:critical-score-mean]</a> applied to $\eta$ now gives $|\mathbb E_\eta w|^2\le C_J\sqrt a\,I$. Since $K_s^2\le s^{-1}K_s$, the curvature-mean inequality gives the asserted estimate for $K_sw$.

In the second alternative, compact form embedding supplies a minimizer of the Rayleigh quotient among mean-zero functions of unit norm. Its positive eigenvalue is $1/R_s^P=\lambda$. The nullspace consists of constants, since zero weak gradient on $\mathbb R^n$ implies constancy. Compactness therefore makes this a positive gap, and interior elliptic regularity makes its eigenfunction $\varphi$ smooth. Commuting a derivative gives $A_{\mu_s}D\varphi=(\lambda\mathrm{Id}-K_s)D\varphi$. Its right side is in $L^2$, so Lemma <a href="#lem:dirichlet-core" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dirichlet-core">4</a> gives the operator-domain assertion. Taking the energy pairing and then Poincaré gives, exactly as before, $$\mathbb E\langle w,K_sw\rangle\le\lambda|\mathbb Ew|^2.$$ Coordinates belong to the operator domain because both $Y$ and $A_{\mu_s}Y=(Y-b_s)/s$ belong to $L^2(\mu_s)$. Integration by parts, or self-adjointness with these coordinate tests, yields $$\mathbb Ew=\lambda\mathbb E[\varphi Y]
      =\frac{\mathbb E[\varphi Y]-\mathbb E[\varphi b_s]}s,
 \qquad
 (1-\lambda s)\mathbb E[\varphi Y]=\mathbb E[\varphi b_s].$$ Conditional Jensen gives $$\mathbb E_{\mu_s}b_sb_s^*\le\mathbb E_\mu XX^*\le Ca^2\mathrm{Id}.$$ For every unit vector $v$, Cauchy–Schwarz thus bounds $|\mathbb E[\varphi\,v\cdot b_s]|$ by $Ca\|\varphi\|_2$. Taking the supremum over $v$ introduces no dimension factor. Since $1-\lambda s\ge1/2$ and $I=\lambda\|\varphi\|_2^2$, it follows that $|\mathbb Ew|\le C_Ja\sqrt I$. The bound for $K_sw$ follows from $K_s^2\le s^{-1}K_s$ once again.

It remains to justify the coordinate assertion for the first alternative. Finite $H(\eta\mid\mu_s)$ and the Gaussian tails of $\mu_s$ imply $\mathbb E_\eta|Y|^2<\infty$, by the entropy argument in the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:translation-gain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:translation-gain">12</a>. The distributional identity <a id="eq:critical-coordinate-generator"></a>$$\label{eq:critical-coordinate-generator}
 A_\eta Y=(Y-b_s)/s-w$$ has an $L^2(\eta)$ right side, because $b_s$ is bounded for the fixed law and $w\in L^2(\eta)$. The maximal-domain assertion of Lemma <a href="#lem:dirichlet-core" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dirichlet-core">4</a> applies. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-regularized-constants"></a>
<div id="prop:regularized-constants" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:critical-R-limit"></a>
<a id="eq:critical-contact-derivative"></a>
**Proposition 14** (Limit of the regularized constants). *For the sequence in Lemma <a href="#lem:critical-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:critical-normalization">7</a>, $$\label{eq:critical-R-limit}
 R_s\longrightarrow1\qquad\text{for every }0<s<1/2.$$ At every differentiability point of $R_s$, the fields of Lemma <a href="#lem:critical-two-alternatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:critical-two-alternatives">13</a> satisfy the contact identity $$\label{eq:critical-contact-derivative}
 R_s'=R_s\,\frac{\mathbb E_\eta\langle w,K_sw\rangle}{I}.$$ In particular $0\le R_s'\le C_J\sqrt a$ almost everywhere on each fixed compact $J\subset(0,1/2)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-regularized-constants"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-regularized-constants</summary>

<a id="eq:critical-transport-energy"></a>
*Proof.* Fix $s$, and let $\Phi_{s,t}$ be the heat continuity flow from Lemma <a href="#lem:critical-flow-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:critical-flow-bounds">8</a>, for $t$ on either side of $s$. Transport a form-domain test $g$ by $g_t=g\circ\Phi_{s,t}^{-1}$. Its distribution under $\mu_t$ is fixed, so both its entropy and its variance are constant. The Jacobian equation $\partial_tD\Phi_{s,t}=K_tD\Phi_{s,t}/2$ gives $$\label{eq:critical-transport-energy}
 \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=s}
 \int|Dg_t|^2d\mu_t
 =-\int\langle Dg,K_sDg\rangle d\mu_s.$$ For compact smooth $g$ this follows by differentiating the inverse-transpose Jacobian in the gradient. For a form-domain $g$, the change-of-variables formula still holds by Sobolev approximation. The flow derivatives and their time derivatives are uniformly bounded on a compact time interval, so the derivative of the integrand is dominated by $C|Dg|^2$. Dominated convergence proves Equation <a href="#eq:critical-transport-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-transport-energy">[eq:critical-transport-energy]</a> without a smoothness or compact-support restriction on $g$.

In the strict alternative take $g=f$ and set $Q(t)=\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_{\mu_t}(f_t^2)/(2\int|Df_t|^2d\mu_t)$. The denominator is positive near $s$, and $Q(t)\le R_t$ for both signs of $t-s$, with $Q(s)=R_s$. At a differentiability point of $R_s$, this two-sided contact implies $Q'(s)=R_s'$. Equation <a href="#eq:critical-transport-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-transport-energy">[eq:critical-transport-energy]</a> and $w=2Df/f$ give Equation <a href="#eq:critical-contact-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-contact-derivative">[eq:critical-contact-derivative]</a>. In the gap alternative take $g=\varphi$ and instead use $Q(t)=\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mu_t}(\varphi_t)/\int|D\varphi_t|^2d\mu_t$. Now $Q(t)\le R_t^P\le R_t$ and $Q(s)=R_s^P=R_s$. The same contact argument gives the identical derivative formula, with $w=D\varphi$.

Equation <a href="#eq:critical-uniform-smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-uniform-smallness">[eq:critical-uniform-smallness]</a> bounds this derivative by $C_J\sqrt a$ in either alternative, with constants independent of which alternative holds at each time. Lipschitz continuity makes $R_s$ absolutely continuous. For fixed $0<\delta<s<1/2$, integration on $[\delta,s]$ gives $$1\le R_s\le R_\delta+C_{[\delta,s]}\sqrt a\,(s-\delta)
             \le1+\delta+C_{[\delta,s]}\sqrt a\,(s-\delta).$$ First take the counterexample-sequence limit and then let $\delta\downarrow0$. This proves Equation <a href="#eq:critical-R-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-R-limit">[eq:critical-R-limit]</a>. ◻

</details>

We finish by fixing the Gaussian time and collecting precisely the objects used in the following sections. The fixed time will not be sent to zero.

<a id="stmt-prop-critical-sequence"></a>
<div id="prop:critical-sequence" class="proposition">

**Proposition 15** (The critical sequence). *If Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> fails, there is a sequence of dimensions and centered compactly supported log-concave laws $\mu$ satisfying Equation <a href="#eq:critical-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-normalization">[eq:critical-normalization]</a>. Fix $s=1/8$, form $\mu_s,b_s,M_s,K_s$ as in Equation <a href="#eq:critical-posterior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-posterior">[eq:critical-posterior]</a>, and put $\lambda=R_s^{-1}$. After passage to a subsequence there are smooth positive probabilities $\eta$ and smooth vector fields $w$ such that, with $A=A_\eta$ and $I=\mathbb E_\eta|w|^2$, <a id="eq:critical-sequence-data"></a><a id="eq:critical-near-eigenfunction"></a><a id="eq:critical-sequence-energy"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 \lambda\longrightarrow1,\qquad R(\eta)\le\lambda^{-1},
 \qquad 0<I<\infty,\qquad w\in\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(A)^n,
 \label{eq:critical-sequence-data}\\
 A w=(\lambda\mathrm{Id}-K_s)w,\qquad
 \|(A-1)w\|_{L^2(\eta)}+|\mathbb E_\eta w|=o(\sqrt I),
 \label{eq:critical-near-eigenfunction}\\
 \mathbb E_\eta|K_sw|^2=o(I),\qquad
 \mathbb E_\eta|Dw|^2=(1+o(1))I.
 \label{eq:critical-sequence-energy}
\end{gathered}$$ The spectral gap of $A$ is at least $\lambda$. Each coordinate function belongs to $\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(A)$. Exactly one of the following constructions can be used throughout the subsequence:*

1.  *$R_s>R_s^P$, $\eta=f^2\mu_s$ for the positive unit-norm optimizer, $w=D\log f^2$, and $I=2\lambda H(\eta\mid\mu_s)$. The exact deficit identity in Equation <a href="#eq:critical-exact-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-exact-deficit">[eq:critical-exact-deficit]</a> holds for compact square-root tests relative to $\eta$, and $A Y=(Y-b_s)/s-w$.*

2.  *$R_s=R_s^P$, $\eta=\mu_s$, $A\varphi=\lambda\varphi$, $\mathbb E_\eta\varphi=0$, $w=D\varphi$, and $I=\lambda\mathbb E_\eta\varphi^2$. One may rescale $\varphi$ so that $I\to0$.*

*All $o$-terms refer to the counterexample sequence with fixed finite parameters. In particular no relation between dimension, $I$, and the rate of convergence is asserted or needed here.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-critical-sequence"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-critical-sequence</summary>

*Proof.* Choose an infinite subsequence with the same alternative at $s=1/8$. Lemma <a href="#lem:critical-two-alternatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:critical-two-alternatives">13</a> gives all structural identities and the curvature and mean bounds. Proposition <a href="#prop:regularized-constants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:regularized-constants">14</a> gives $\lambda\to1$. Consequently $$(A-1)w=(\lambda-1)w-K_sw=o_{L^2}(\sqrt I).$$ The energy pairing yields $\mathbb E|Dw|^2=\lambda I-\mathbb E\langle w,K_sw\rangle=(1+o(1))I$. Poincaré with constant $\lambda^{-1}$ is the asserted spectral gap. The coordinate and branch-specific identities have already been proved. In the gap alternative all estimates are homogeneous in $\varphi$, so multiplying it by positive scalars can enforce $I\to0$ without changing any normalized assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:prediction"></a>
### Efficient probes and the prediction curve

<a id="eq:prediction-input"></a>
Fix $s=1/8$ and take one of the two subsequences supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:critical-sequence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:critical-sequence">15</a>. We continue to suppress the counterexample index. Thus $\lambda\to1$, $\eta$ satisfies the logarithmic Sobolev inequality with constant at most $1/\lambda$, and, with $A=A_\eta$, $w$ belongs coefficientwise to $\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(A)$ and satisfies $$I=\mathbb E_\eta|w|^2>0,
 \qquad \|(A-1)w\|_{L^2(\eta)}+|\mathbb E_\eta w|=o(\sqrt I).
 \label{eq:prediction-input}$$ In the strict case, $\eta=f^2\mu_s$, $w=D\log f^2$, and $2H(\eta\mid\mu_s)=I/\lambda$. In the gap case, $\eta=\mu_s$, $w=D\varphi$, and $A_{\mu_s}\varphi=\lambda\varphi$, where $\mathbb E_{\mu_s}\varphi=0$ and $I=\lambda\mathbb E_{\mu_s}\varphi^2$. All limits in this section are along the selected subsequence, with the displayed auxiliary parameters fixed unless a different order is specified.

Our objective is to determine how much of $w(Y_0)$ can be predicted from $Y_0+\sqrt rG$. The strict case first requires an entropy estimate for an additional Gaussian observation of $w$. We then prove an entropy-loss comparison for arbitrary smooth alternatives to a log-concave law. These two estimates determine the prediction curve; linearization of the same comparison handles the gap case.

<a id="stmt-lem-efficient-probes"></a>
<div id="lem:efficient-probes" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:efficient-probes"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Efficient Gaussian probes). *In the strict case, let $Y_0\sim\eta$ and let $G'$ be an independent standard Gaussian vector in the same Euclidean space. For $T\ge0$ define $$L_T=\sqrt T\,w(Y_0)+G',\qquad
 d(T)=H(\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(L_T)\mid\gamma),$$ where $\gamma$ is standard Gaussian measure. For every finite $M$, $$\sup_{0\le T\le M}d(T)=o(I).
 \label{eq:efficient-probes}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-efficient-probes"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-efficient-probes</summary>

<a id="eq:prediction-coordinate-generator"></a>
*Proof.* Run the stationary diffusion with law $\eta$, using Brownian noise independent of $G'$, and write $P_u=e^{-uA}$. Proposition <a href="#prop:critical-sequence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:critical-sequence">15</a> puts each coordinate in $\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(A)$ and gives the identity $$-Ay=-y/s+w+b_s/s.
 \label{eq:prediction-coordinate-generator}$$ In particular, $\mathbb E_\eta|Y_0|^2<\infty$. The logarithmic Sobolev inequality for $\eta$ then gives $$\mathbb E_\eta e^{v\cdot(Y_0-\mathbb EY_0)}
 \le e^{|v|^2/(2\lambda)}.$$ Integrating this inequality over a Gaussian $v$ proves $\mathbb E_\eta e^{c'|Y_0|^2}<\infty$ for a sufficiently small $c'>0$. Here the choice of $c'$ may depend on the individual law and dimension. This exponential-square moment will also permit application of the entropy-loss comparison below.

<a id="eq:probe-reference-decomposition"></a>
For a fixed $T\le M$, set $$J_u=\mathsf I(L_T;Y_u),\qquad
 F_u=\mathbb E\,\mathcal I(\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(Y_u\mid L_T)\mid\eta),\qquad
 Q_u=\mathbb E|\mathbb E[Y_u\mid L_T]|^2.$$ The Gaussian observation identity gives $J_0=TI/2-d(T)$. Entropy dissipation, including its conditional version, gives $-dJ_u\ge F_u\,du$, and in particular $\int_0^1F_u\,du\le J_0$. If $\sigma_{u,l}=\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(Y_u\mid L_T=l)$, then the entropy chain rule and the strict-case normalization give $$\mathbb EH(\sigma_{u,L_T}\mid\mu_s)=J_u+\frac{I}{2\lambda},
 \qquad
 \mathbb E\mathcal I(\sigma_{u,L_T}\mid\mu_s)=F_u+I.
 \label{eq:probe-reference-decomposition}$$ For the second equality, write the conditional relative score with respect to $\mu_s$ as the sum of $w$ and the conditional relative score with respect to $\eta$. The latter scores have conditional average zero at each $Y_u$, since the conditional likelihoods average to one. This cancels the cross term. At the almost every time where $F_u<\infty$, the assertion also follows weakly by differentiating that marginal identity: Cauchy–Schwarz gives locally integrable averaged derivatives.

<a id="eq:probe-decay"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:translation-gain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:translation-gain">12</a> to $\sigma_{u,l}$ and average over $l$. Since $s^{-2}-\lambda/s$ stays positive, Equations <a href="#eq:probe-reference-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:probe-reference-decomposition">[eq:probe-reference-decomposition]</a> give, on $[0,1]$, $$-dJ_u\ge(2\lambda J_u+c_sQ_u)\,du-d\mathcal E_u,
 \qquad \mathcal E([0,1])=o_M(I),
 \label{eq:probe-decay}$$ where $c_s>0$ is fixed and $\mathcal E$ is a nonnegative error measure. Indeed the error in that lemma is bounded by a number tending to zero times $F_u+I+J_u+I/(2\lambda)$, whose integral is $O_M(I)$. The measure formulation avoids imposing differentiability on $J_u$.

<a id="eq:probe-semigroup"></a>
<a id="eq:probe-position-control"></a>
The spectral theorem and Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-input">[eq:prediction-input]</a> imply $$\sup_{0\le u\le1}\|P_uw-e^{-u}w\|_2
 \le \|(A-1)w\|_2=o(\sqrt I).
 \label{eq:probe-semigroup}$$ Reversibility gives $\mathbb E[L_T\mid Y_u]=\sqrt T P_uw(Y_u)$. Relative entropy with respect to a standard Gaussian is at least half the square of the mean, by testing its variational formula with linear functions. Consequently $$J_u+d(T)=\mathbb EH(\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(L_T\mid Y_u)\mid\gamma)
 \ge\frac T2\|P_uw\|_2^2
 =\frac{TI}{2}e^{-2u}+o_M(I).$$ Integrating Equation <a href="#eq:probe-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:probe-decay">[eq:probe-decay]</a> with factor $e^{2\lambda u}$ and comparing at $u=1$ yields $$\int_0^1Q_u\,du\le C_s d(T)+o_M(I).
 \label{eq:probe-position-control}$$ Here the difference between $e^{-2\lambda}$ and $e^{-2}$ contributes only $o_M(I)$.

<a id="eq:probe-coordinate-solution"></a>
It remains to turn control of the predicted position into control of the predicted score. The pushforward of $\mu_s$ by $b_s$ has the centered linear moment bound $$\log\mathbb E_{\mu_s}e^{v\cdot b_s}\le Ca^2|v|^2,$$ by conditional Jensen and the corresponding bound for the original signal $X$. The entropy variational formula, optimized over $v$, thus gives $$\mathbb E|\mathbb E[b_s(Y_v)\mid L_T]|^2
 \le Ca^2\bigl(J_v+I/(2\lambda)\bigr)=o_M(I).$$ Condition Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-coordinate-generator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-coordinate-generator">[eq:prediction-coordinate-generator]</a> on $L_T$ and use variation of constants. By Equation <a href="#eq:probe-semigroup" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:probe-semigroup">[eq:probe-semigroup]</a> and the contraction property of conditional expectation, $$\mathbb E[Y_u\mid L_T]
 =e^{-u/s}\mathbb E[Y_0\mid L_T]
 +\frac{e^{-u}-e^{-u/s}}{1/s-1}\mathbb E[w(Y_0)\mid L_T]
 +o_{L^2,M}(\sqrt I),
 \label{eq:probe-coordinate-solution}$$ uniformly for $0\le u\le1$. The two scalar functions multiplying the conditional means are linearly independent on $[0,1]$: the first is nonzero at zero, whereas the second vanishes there and is nonzero for $u>0$. Their $2\times2$ Gram matrix is therefore positive definite. Applying its smallest-eigenvalue bound to each coordinate and then averaging, Equations <a href="#eq:probe-position-control" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:probe-position-control">[eq:probe-position-control]</a> and <a href="#eq:probe-coordinate-solution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:probe-coordinate-solution">[eq:probe-coordinate-solution]</a> show that $$\mathbb E|\mathbb E[w(Y_0)\mid L_T]|^2\le C_s d(T)+o_M(I).$$ By Lemma <a href="#lem:gaussian-signals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gaussian-signals">6</a>, the left side is $2d'(T)$ for almost every $T$. The error is uniform for $T\in[0,M]$, and $d(0)=0$. Integrating this differential inequality proves Equation <a href="#eq:efficient-probes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:efficient-probes">[eq:efficient-probes]</a>. No lower bound on $I$ is used. ◻

</details>

The next comparison relates the entropy lost under heat flow to the conditional mean of the initial relative score. The conditional mean need not equal the relative score at positive heat time, so entropy dissipation alone does not give this comparison.

The choice of observation directions is related to function-preserving stochastic localization. Eldan–Koehler–Zeitouni (Eldan, Koehler, and Zeitouni 2022, sec. 2, Lemma 2) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[EldanKoehlerZeitouni2022](#ref-EldanKoehlerZeitouni2022)] use controls approximating projection orthogonal to the covariance of the signal with a selected function. Chen and Eldan (Chen and Eldan 2025, sec. 2.4.2 and 3.2.3, Equation (27)) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[ChenEldan2022](#ref-ChenEldan2022)] express the entropy drift through the difference of means under a measure and its normalized likelihood tilt, in a continuous-time setup with positive definite controls. Below we prove the cancellation and its error directly for finite Gaussian observations with singular projections, and use independent copies to control the discarded directions.

<a id="stmt-lem-entropy-loss"></a>
<div id="lem:entropy-loss" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:prediction-entropy-loss"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Entropy loss under a Gaussian observation). *Let $p$ and $q$ be smooth strictly positive probability densities on $\mathbb R^d$, with $q$ log-concave. Suppose $$H_0=H(p\mid q)<\infty,\qquad
 w=D\log(p/q),\qquad I=\mathbb E_p|w|^2<\infty,
 \qquad \mathbb E_p e^{c|Y|^2}<\infty$$ for some $c>0$, where $Y\sim p$. Let $G$ be a standard Gaussian vector independent of $Y$. For $r>0$, let $U_r=Y+\sqrt rG$, let $p_r$ and $q_r$ be the Gaussian convolutions of $p$ and $q$, and put $\bar w_r(U_r)=\mathbb E_p[w(Y)\mid U_r]$ and $H_r=H(p_r\mid q_r)$. Then, for every $z>0$, $$2(H_0-H_z)\le\int_0^z\mathbb E_p|\bar w_r(U_r)|^2\,dr.
 \label{eq:prediction-entropy-loss}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-entropy-loss"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-entropy-loss</summary>

*Proof.* The proof adds weak observations that increase the curvature of the base posterior while losing asymptotically no relative entropy. At each step we remove the direction in which the two posterior means differ. The retained output means then agree, and we will show that its relative entropy, averaged over the retained history, is $O(\delta^2)$ for observation strength $\delta$. Independent copies make the cost of removing these directions vanish after division by the number of copies. That last comparison concerns the score residuals after full observations, whose conditional independence will be essential.

We keep the one-copy problem fixed. The three successive limits will be refinement of a finite observation mesh, increase of the number of independent copies, and increase of the total additional precision. Set $b=1/z$ and take $N$ independent copies. Write $Y=(Y_1,\ldots,Y_N)$ and $W=(w(Y_1),\ldots,w(Y_N))$; the two possible priors are $p^{\otimes N}$ and $q^{\otimes N}$. Expectations in the proof use $p^{\otimes N}$.

*Additional observations and their entropy cost.* First observe $Z_0=\sqrt bY+G_0$. The chain rule says that the remaining posterior relative entropy, averaged over this observation, is $N(H_0-H_z)$. Fix $u>0$ and an integer $m$, and put $\delta=u/m$. For $i=0,\ldots,m-1$, let $\mathcal H_i$ be the retained history before step $i$. Compute the difference of the two posterior means given this history and let $P_i$ be the orthogonal projection onto its span; set $P_i=0$ when the difference vanishes. Retain only $$(1-P_i)Z_{i+1},\qquad Z_{i+1}=\sqrt\delta Y+G_{i+1},$$ where all Gaussian noises are independent. The same measurable rule is used under both priors. One may choose versions of the posterior means by their integral formulas; the positive densities make the rule well defined on all histories of positive probability.

For each realized retained history the likelihood, as a function of $Y$, is a Gaussian quadratic factor of precision <a id="eq:entropy-loss-precision"></a>$$B=b\mathrm{Id}+\delta\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}(1-P_i)\succeq b\mathrm{Id}.
 \label{eq:entropy-loss-precision}$$ There is no extra likelihood factor from the choices of $P_i$: each choice was already determined by the retained past. The factor is the same for both priors. Thus the posterior base has curvature at least $B$, and the posterior relative score is still $W$.

We next bound the relative entropy lost in the additional observations. Condition on a retained history at one step and put $Q=1-P_i$. The projected posterior means agree. After subtracting their common mean, let $X_c$ denote either centered projected signal in $\operatorname{ran}Q$, whose dimension is at most $Nd$. The base posterior has curvature at least $b\mathrm{Id}$. Its centered linear moment bound and integration over a Gaussian vector give $$\mathbb E_q[e^{\kappa|X_c|^2}\mid\mathcal H_i]
 \le (1-2\kappa/b)^{-Nd/2},\qquad 0<2\kappa<b.$$ For the alternative, write $a_i=\mathbb E_p[Y\mid\mathcal H_i]$. For any sufficiently small $\kappa>0$, conditional Jensen gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_p[e^{\kappa|Q(Y-a_i)|^2}\mid\mathcal H_i]
 &\le e^{2\kappa|a_i|^2}
       \mathbb E_p[e^{2\kappa|Y|^2}\mid\mathcal H_i]\\
 &\le\bigl(\mathbb E_p[e^{2\kappa|Y|^2}\mid\mathcal H_i]\bigr)^2.
\end{aligned}$$ Squaring and applying conditional Jensen once more shows that its square, averaged over $\mathcal H_i$, is bounded by $\mathbb E_p e^{8\kappa|Y|^2}<\infty$. These bounds hold for every history sigma-field, so their constants do not depend on the mesh or on the adaptive choices.

<a id="eq:entropy-loss-posterior-lsi"></a>
Let $h_p,h_q$ be the output densities of $\sqrt\delta X_c+G$ in $\operatorname{ran}Q$ and let $\phi$ be standard Gaussian density there. Centering and Jensen give the pointwise bound $$\frac{h_q(x)}{\phi(x)}
 =\mathbb E_q e^{\sqrt\delta x\cdot X_c-\delta|X_c|^2/2}
 \ge e^{-\delta Nd/(2b)}.$$ For either conditional signal, using a conditionally independent copy $X_c'$ and integrating the Gaussian kernel gives $$\left\|\frac h\phi-1\right\|_{L^2(\phi)}^2
 =\mathbb E[e^{\delta X_c\cdot X_c'}\mid\mathcal H_i]-1.$$ The linear term vanishes because $X_c$ is centered. The bound $|e^t-1-t|\le t^2e^{|t|}/2$, followed by $|X_c\cdot X_c'|\le(|X_c|^2+|X_c'|^2)/2$, bounds the last expression by $C\delta^2$ times the square of a small conditional exponential-square moment. The preceding moment bounds therefore give $$\mathbb EH(h_p\mid h_q)
 \le\mathbb E\chi^2(h_p\mid h_q)
 \le2e^{\delta Nd/(2b)}\mathbb E\left(
 \left\|h_p/\phi-1\right\|_2^2+
 \left\|h_q/\phi-1\right\|_2^2\right)
 \le C_{N,b,p}\delta^2.$$ This statement includes the zero-dimensional observed subspace, for which the loss is zero. Summing the $m$ steps loses $O_{N,b,p}(u\delta)$ entropy. Apply the anisotropic curvature inequality, Lemma <a href="#lem:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curvature">3</a>, to the final posterior pair. For fixed $N$ and $u$ we obtain $$2N(H_0-H_z)-o_{\delta\downarrow0}(1)
 \le\mathbb EW^*B^{-1}W.
 \label{eq:entropy-loss-posterior-lsi}$$

<a id="eq:entropy-loss-variational"></a>
<a id="eq:entropy-loss-minimizer"></a>
<a id="eq:entropy-loss-residual"></a>
*Comparison with full observations.* We now estimate the effect of the rank-one exclusions. Work in the Hilbert space of square-integrable vector fields on the signal and all the full observations $Z_0,\ldots,Z_m$. In particular, $\|W\|_2^2=NI$. Completing the square pointwise gives $$b\|W\|_2^2-b^2\mathbb EW^*B^{-1}W
 =\min_v\left\{b\|W-v\|_2^2+
              \delta\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\|(1-P_i)v\|_2^2\right\}.
 \label{eq:entropy-loss-variational}$$ Its minimizer is $v_*=bB^{-1}W$, so $\|v_*\|_2\le\|W\|_2$. Let $\mathcal F_i=\sigma(Z_0,\ldots,Z_i)$ and let $e_i$ be conditional expectation onto $\mathcal F_i$. The sigma-fields are nested, and $P_i$ is $\mathcal F_i$-measurable because the retained history is a function of this full history. We compare the original minimum with one that penalizes the part of $v$ left unpredicted by the full observations: replace $1-P_i$ by $1-e_i$, and denote the resulting minimum by $M_0$. The nested projections make this new minimum explicitly computable in terms of Gaussian prediction. We first find its minimizer, then use its residuals in a dual test to bound the original minimum below in terms of $M_0$. Its minimizer is $$v_0=b\left(b+\delta\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}(1-e_i)\right)^{-1}W.$$ Here is its explicit form. Set $\alpha_k=b/(b+k\delta)$ for $0\le k\le m$. On $\operatorname{ran}e_0$ the multiplier is one; on $\operatorname{ran}(e_k-e_{k-1})$ it is $\alpha_k$; on $\operatorname{ran}(1-e_{m-1})$ it is $\alpha_m$. Consequently $$v_0=\alpha_mW+
       \sum_{k=0}^{m-1}(\alpha_k-\alpha_{k+1})e_kW.
 \label{eq:entropy-loss-minimizer}$$ All coefficients are nonnegative and sum to one. Put $g_i=(1-e_i)v_0$. Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-loss-minimizer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-loss-minimizer">[eq:entropy-loss-minimizer]</a> gives $$g_i=\alpha_m(W-e_iW)+
 \sum_{k=i+1}^{m-1}(\alpha_k-\alpha_{k+1})(e_kW-e_iW).
 \label{eq:entropy-loss-residual}$$ The weights in this formula sum to $\alpha_{i+1}\le1$.

<a id="eq:entropy-loss-block-bound"></a>
We need a bound uniform over every subdivision. The retained histories need not factor across copies. We use independence only for the full observation arrays: those belonging to different copies are independent under $p^{\otimes N}$. Conditioning on $\mathcal F_i$ conditions each such array on its own past. Thus $e_kW_j$ depends only on copy $j$’s observations through step $k$. Conditional on $\mathcal F_i$, the blocks $g_{i,j}$ are independent and centered, even though they involve future full observations. To see this directly, factor the joint law into the independent copy arrays and condition each factor on its own past; the resulting conditional kernel remains a product. By conditional Jensen applied to Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-loss-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-loss-residual">[eq:entropy-loss-residual]</a>, $$Z_{i,j}:=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(g_{i,j}\mid\mathcal F_i)
 \le\mathbb E[|W_j-e_iW_j|^2\mid\mathcal F_i]
 \le\mathbb E[|W_j|^2\mid\mathcal F_i].
 \label{eq:entropy-loss-block-bound}$$ For example, every term with $k>i$ is bounded in conditional squared mean by $\mathbb E[|W_j-e_iW_j|^2\mid\mathcal F_i]$ by the tower property; the nonnegative weights of total at most one preserve this bound.

<a id="eq:entropy-loss-ui"></a>
<a id="eq:entropy-loss-rank-error"></a>
The right sides in Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-loss-block-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-loss-block-bound">[eq:entropy-loss-block-bound]</a> are uniformly integrable over all these sigma-fields. Explicitly, if $Z\ge0$ is integrable and $M=\mathbb E[Z\mid\mathcal F]$, then $$\mathbb E[M\mathbf 1_{\{M>K\}}]\le2\mathbb E[Z\mathbf 1_{\{Z>K/2\}}].
 \label{eq:entropy-loss-ui}$$ Indeed $M\le K/2+\mathbb E[Z\mathbf 1_{\{Z>K/2\}}\mid\mathcal F]$, and on $\{M>K\}$ the last conditional expectation is at least $M/2$. The conditional covariance of $g_i$ is block diagonal. Since $P_i$ is measurable in $\mathcal F_i$ and has rank at most one, $$\mathbb E|P_ig_i|^2\le\mathbb E\max_{1\le j\le N}Z_{i,j}.$$ The maximum is at most $K+\sum_jZ_{i,j}\mathbf 1_{\{Z_{i,j}>K\}}$. Equations <a href="#eq:entropy-loss-block-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-loss-block-bound">[eq:entropy-loss-block-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:entropy-loss-ui" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-loss-ui">[eq:entropy-loss-ui]</a> show that, uniformly in $i,m$, and $\delta$, $$\mathbb E|P_ig_i|^2\le Nq_N,\qquad
 q_N:=\inf_{K>0}\left\{\frac KN+
           2\mathbb E_p[|w(Y)|^2\mathbf 1_{\{|w(Y)|^2>K/2\}}]\right\}\longrightarrow0.
 \label{eq:entropy-loss-rank-error}$$ No moment of the score beyond its second moment is required. In particular this bound permits $P_i$ to depend adaptively on all copies.

<a id="eq:entropy-loss-dual-error"></a>
For completeness, the comparison of the two minima follows from a dual test. For every $v$, $$\|(1-P_i)v\|_2^2
 \ge2\langle v,(1-P_i)g_i\rangle-\|g_i\|_2^2.$$ The normal equation for $v_0$ is $b(W-v_0)=\delta\sum_i g_i$. Therefore the minimum over $v$ of $$b\|W-v\|_2^2+2\delta\sum_i\langle v,g_i\rangle
                  -\delta\sum_i\|g_i\|_2^2$$ is attained at $v_0$ and equals $M_0$. Use the displayed linear bound at $v_*$ in Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-loss-variational" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-loss-variational">[eq:entropy-loss-variational]</a>. By Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-loss-rank-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-loss-rank-error">[eq:entropy-loss-rank-error]</a>, the true minimum is at least $$M_0-2\delta\sum_i\|v_*\|_2\|P_ig_i\|_2
 \ge M_0-2uN\sqrt{Iq_N}.
 \label{eq:entropy-loss-dual-error}$$

<a id="eq:entropy-loss-discrete-minimum"></a>
*The prediction integral.* It remains to compute $M_0$ and take the limits. The same orthogonal decomposition into nested projection increments gives $$\frac{M_0}{N}
 =\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}
 \frac{b^2\delta}{(b+i\delta)(b+(i+1)\delta)}
       \left(I-\|e_iW_1\|_2^2\right).
 \label{eq:entropy-loss-discrete-minimum}$$ Indeed a component first seen after $j$ increments has penalty $bj\delta/(b+j\delta)$, and $$\frac{bj\delta}{b+j\delta}
 =\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}
   \frac{b^2\delta}{(b+i\delta)(b+(i+1)\delta)}.$$ Full observations through step $i$ have the sufficient Gaussian statistic of precision $b+i\delta$. Completing the likelihood square proves sufficiency directly. Hence $$\|e_iW_1\|_2^2
 =\mathbb E_p|\bar w_{1/(b+i\delta)}(U_{1/(b+i\delta)})|^2.$$ This squared prediction norm is nondecreasing with precision, since a weaker observation can be obtained by adding independent Gaussian noise. It is bounded by $I$, so Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-loss-discrete-minimum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-loss-discrete-minimum">[eq:entropy-loss-discrete-minimum]</a> converges as $m\to\infty$ to $$b^2\int_0^u
 \frac{I-\mathbb E_p|\bar w_{1/(b+t)}(U_{1/(b+t)})|^2}{(b+t)^2}\,dt.$$ First take this mesh limit at fixed $N,u$. It removes the entropy error in Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-loss-posterior-lsi" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-loss-posterior-lsi">[eq:entropy-loss-posterior-lsi]</a>, while Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-loss-dual-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-loss-dual-error">[eq:entropy-loss-dual-error]</a> remains uniform in the mesh. Divide by $N$, then let $N\to\infty$, using $q_N\to0$. Equations <a href="#eq:entropy-loss-posterior-lsi" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-loss-posterior-lsi">[eq:entropy-loss-posterior-lsi]</a> and <a href="#eq:entropy-loss-variational" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-loss-variational">[eq:entropy-loss-variational]</a> give $$2(H_0-H_z)
 \le\frac Ib-\int_0^u
 \frac{I-\mathbb E_p|\bar w_{1/(b+t)}(U_{1/(b+t)})|^2}{(b+t)^2}\,dt
 =\frac{I}{b+u}
   +\int_{1/(b+u)}^{1/b}\mathbb E_p|\bar w_r(U_r)|^2\,dr.$$ Finally let $u\to\infty$. Since $1/b=z$ and the integrand is bounded by $I$, this proves Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-entropy-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-entropy-loss">[eq:prediction-entropy-loss]</a> in the claimed order of limits. ◻

</details>

We return to the critical sequence. For $Y_0\sim\eta$ and independent Gaussian noise, define $$U_r=Y_0+\sqrt rG,\qquad
 \bar w_r(U_r)=\mathbb E[w(Y_0)\mid U_r],\qquad
 m(r)=I^{-1}\mathbb E|\bar w_r(U_r)|^2.$$ In the strict case we also use the label $L_T$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:efficient-probes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:efficient-probes">16</a>, constructed with fresh Gaussian noise independent of $(Y_0,G)$. Thus $L_T$ and $U_r$ are conditionally independent given $Y_0$. Set $$p_T(r)=I^{-1}\mathbb E|\mathbb E[w(Y_0)\mid U_r,L_T]|^2,
 \qquad p_0(r)=m(r).$$

<a id="stmt-prop-prediction"></a>
<div id="prop:prediction" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:prediction-curve"></a>
**Proposition 18** (The prediction curve). *For the strict and gap critical sequences, for every fixed $0<r<1/4$, $$m(r)\longrightarrow e^{-r}.
 \label{eq:prediction-curve}$$ In the strict case, for every fixed finite $T\ge0$ one also has $p_T(r)\to e^{-r}$. In the gap case the eigenfunction may be rescaled so that $I\to0$, without changing Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-curve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-curve">[eq:prediction-curve]</a> or Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-input">[eq:prediction-input]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-prediction"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-prediction</summary>

*Proof.* For $0\le r<1/4$ put $$\theta(r)=\exp\left(-\int_0^r\frac{dt}{R_{s+t}}\right).$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:regularized-constants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:regularized-constants">14</a> gives $\theta(r)=e^{-r}+o(1)$, uniformly on compact intervals under consideration. The common heat flow dissipates relative entropy at one half the relative Fisher information. Thus its entropy relative to $\mu_{s+r}$ is at most $\theta(r)$ times its initial entropy, by the logarithmic Sobolev inequality at the contemporaneous base law. This assertion applies to conditional laws after averaging as well.

<a id="eq:prediction-upper"></a>
In the strict case write $H_r=H(\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(U_r)\mid\mu_{s+r})$ and $H_0=I/(2\lambda)$. The entropy of $U_r$ given $L_T$, relative to $\mu_{s+r}$ and averaged over the label, is at most $\theta(r)(H_0+TI/2)$, since initially it is $H_0+\mathsf I(Y_0;L_T)$. The chain rule gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac I2\int_0^T p_v(r)\,dv
 &=\mathbb EH(\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(L_T\mid U_r)\mid\gamma)\\
 &=\mathbb EH(\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(U_r\mid L_T)\mid\mu_{s+r})+d(T)-H_r\\
 &\le\frac I2\theta(r)(1/\lambda+T)+d(T).
\end{aligned}$$ The first equality is Lemma <a href="#lem:gaussian-signals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gaussian-signals">6</a>, conditionally on $U_r$. Fix $v\ge0$ and then $T>v$. Since $p_t(r)$ is nondecreasing in signal strength $t$, Lemma <a href="#lem:efficient-probes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:efficient-probes">16</a> yields $$(T-v)\limsup p_v(r)\le e^{-r}(1+T).$$ The sequence limit is taken with $T$ fixed. Allowing this fixed $T$ to be arbitrarily large proves $$\limsup p_v(r)\le e^{-r}.
 \label{eq:prediction-upper}$$

<a id="eq:prediction-integral"></a>
Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-loss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-loss">17</a> applies to $p=\eta$ and $q=\mu_s$; the exponential-square moment was proved in the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:efficient-probes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:efficient-probes">16</a>. Since $H_z\le\theta(z)H_0$, it gives $$\int_0^z m(r)\,dr\ge\frac{1-\theta(z)}{\lambda}
 \qquad(0<z<1/4).$$ The reverse limiting inequality follows from Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-upper">[eq:prediction-upper]</a>, $0\le m\le1$, and reverse Fatou. Hence $$\int_0^z m(r)\,dr\longrightarrow1-e^{-z}.
 \label{eq:prediction-integral}$$ The function $m$ is nonincreasing in $r$. For $0<h<r$ with $r+h<1/4$, its value at $r$ is bounded below and above by its averages on $[r,r+h]$ and $[r-h,r]$, respectively. Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-integral">[eq:prediction-integral]</a> determines the limits of both averages; letting $h\downarrow0$ gives Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-curve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-curve">[eq:prediction-curve]</a>. Finally $m(r)\le p_T(r)$ and Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-upper">[eq:prediction-upper]</a> give the assertion for every fixed $T$.

<a id="eq:prediction-linearized-loss"></a>
We now prove the gap assertion without using additional probes. First linearize Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-loss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-loss">17</a>. Let $q$ be a bounded smooth centered test under $\mu_s$, with bounded gradient, and let $$d\eta_\varepsilon=(1+\varepsilon q)\,d\mu_s,
 \qquad |\varepsilon|\|q\|_\infty<1.$$ This alternative has a finite exponential-square moment. Write $\bar q_r=\mathbb E_{\mu_s}[q(Y_0)\mid U_r]$ and $\overline{Dq}_r=\mathbb E_{\mu_s}[Dq(Y_0)\mid U_r]$. The output density ratio is $1+\varepsilon\bar q_r$, while the predicted initial relative score is exactly $$\mathbb E_{\eta_\varepsilon}[D\log(1+\varepsilon q)(Y_0)\mid U_r]
 =\frac{\varepsilon\overline{Dq}_r}{1+\varepsilon\bar q_r}.$$ Consequently its squared norm averaged under the alternative output is $\varepsilon^2\mathbb E_{\mu_{s+r}}[|\overline{Dq}_r|^2/
(1+\varepsilon\bar q_r)]$. The entropy expansion $H((1+\varepsilon q)\mu_s\mid\mu_s)
=\varepsilon^2\|q\|_2^2/2+o(\varepsilon^2)$, and its output version, now give $$\|q\|_2^2-\|\bar q_z\|_2^2
 \le\int_0^z\|\overline{Dq}_r\|_2^2\,dr.
 \label{eq:prediction-linearized-loss}$$ Indeed the denominator is bounded away from zero uniformly in $r$, and conditional Jensen bounds the integrand by a constant times $\|Dq\|_2^2$, justifying the limit. Approximation in the form norm extends Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-linearized-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-linearized-loss">[eq:prediction-linearized-loss]</a> to $q=\varphi$: centered compact smooth approximants have the required boundedness, and all conditional expectation maps are $L^2$ contractions. To retain compact support while centering, subtract the approximant’s mean times one fixed compact smooth function of mean one; this correction tends to zero in the form norm.

<a id="eq:prediction-heat-variance"></a>
For clarity, we also give the heat variance identity used here. For a centered scalar or finite-dimensional vector field $g\in L^2(\mu_s)$, let $g_r=\mathbb E[g(Y_0)\mid U_r]$ under the base law. Then $$\frac d{dr}\mathbb E_{\mu_{s+r}}|g_r|^2
 =-\mathbb E_{\mu_{s+r}}|Dg_r|^2.
 \label{eq:prediction-heat-variance}$$ For bounded $g$, both the numerator density $g_r\mu_{s+r}$ and the denominator density $\mu_{s+r}$ solve the heat equation, and the quotient calculation with the square function gives $(\partial_r-\Delta/2)(\mu_{s+r}|g_r|^2)
=-\mu_{s+r}|Dg_r|^2$. Integrate against expanding cutoffs, whose Laplacians tend uniformly to zero. The bound $|g_r|\le\|g\|_\infty$ removes the cutoff terms and gives the integrated identity on positive time intervals. Its initial endpoint follows from $\|g_r(U_r)-g(Y_0)\|_2\to0$: for a bounded continuous test use $g(U_r)$ as a predictor and dominated convergence under the coupling $U_r=Y_0+\sqrt rG$, and then approximate in $L^2(\mu_s)$ and use conditional contraction. Approximate a general $L^2$ field by bounded ones. Applying the same identity to their differences shows that their gradients are Cauchy in the space-time energy norm, while conditional contraction gives convergence of both endpoint norms. This proves the integrated version of Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-heat-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-heat-variance">[eq:prediction-heat-variance]</a> in full generality.

Poincaré at $\mu_{s+r}$ and preservation of the mean therefore imply $$\mathbb E_{\mu_{s+r}}|g_r|^2\le\theta(r)\mathbb E_{\mu_s}|g|^2.$$ Apply this first to $\varphi$, and then to $w-\mathbb Ew$. The latter application and Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-input">[eq:prediction-input]</a> give $m(r)\le\theta(r)+o(1)$. The former and $\|\varphi\|_2^2=I/\lambda$, inserted into Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-linearized-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-linearized-loss">[eq:prediction-linearized-loss]</a>, give $\int_0^z m(r)\,dr\ge(1-\theta(z))/\lambda$. The same integral squeeze and monotonicity argument prove Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-curve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-curve">[eq:prediction-curve]</a>. Multiplying $\varphi$ by any positive scalar multiplies every squared quantity by its square, so we may finally choose these scalars to arrange $I\to0$. ◻

</details>

The hierarchy construction will use the following consequences of the prediction curve. The estimates distinguish conditional covariance tails, which are needed only in the strict case, from bounds on deterministic subspaces, which hold in both cases.

<a id="stmt-lem-posterior-outliers"></a>
<div id="lem:posterior-outliers" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (Posterior covariance and outlier subspaces). *Use the critical sequence and notation of Proposition <a href="#prop:prediction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prediction">18</a>, with $I\to0$ in the gap case. The following assertions hold.*

<a id="eq:prediction-small-subspaces"></a>
1.  *For every fixed $c<\infty$, uniformly over deterministic orthogonal projections $\Pi$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}\Pi\le cI$, $$\mathbb E|\Pi w|^2=o(I).
     \label{eq:prediction-small-subspaces}$$*

<a id="eq:prediction-outlier-subspaces"></a>
2.  *For every $\epsilon>0$, for all sufficiently far members of the sequence there is a deterministic subspace $V_\epsilon$ of dimension at most $C_\epsilon I$ such that, for every $v\perp V_\epsilon$, $$v^*(\mathbb Eww^*)v\le\epsilon|v|^2,\qquad
     v^*(\mathbb Eb_sb_s^*)v\le\epsilon|v|^2,\qquad
     v^*(\mathbb EM_s)v\le\epsilon|v|^2.
     \label{eq:prediction-outlier-subspaces}$$ In the gap case $V_\epsilon$ may be taken to be zero, and $\|\mathbb Eb_sb_s^*\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|\mathbb EM_s\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o(1)$.*

<a id="eq:conditional-tail"></a>
3.  *In the strict case, for every fixed $r\in(0,1/4)$ and $\epsilon>0$, the conditional covariance $C_r=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(w(Y_0)\mid U_r)$ satisfies $$\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(C_r\mathbf 1_{\{C_r>\epsilon\mathrm{Id}\}}\bigr)=o(I).
     \label{eq:conditional-tail}$$ The indicator denotes the spectral projection. No assertion about additional Gaussian probes or Equation <a href="#eq:conditional-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditional-tail">[eq:conditional-tail]</a> is needed for the gap case.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-posterior-outliers"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-posterior-outliers</summary>

*Proof.* We first prove the strict-case spectral estimates. If a random vector $S$ has covariance $C$ and mean $a$, the affine estimator $$a+C(\mathrm{Id}+C)^{-1}(S+G-a)$$ of $S$ from $S+G$ is its least-squares affine predictor. Its mean squared norm is $|a|^2+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(C^2(\mathrm{Id}+C)^{-1})$, by direct expansion of the covariances. Conditional expectation has at least this squared norm, because it is the orthogonal projection onto all functions of the observation. The same calculation holds conditionally on any side information, using fresh independent Gaussian noise.

By the Gaussian signal derivative formula and its monotonicity, $$\mathbb E|\mathbb E[w\mid L_1]|^2
 \le2\bigl(d(2)-d(1)\bigr)\le2d(2)=o(I).$$ Apply the affine prediction bound to $S=w$ and $C=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_\eta w$. For every fixed $\epsilon>0$, <a id="eq:prediction-unconditional-tail"></a>$$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(C\mathbf 1_{\{C>\epsilon\mathrm{Id}\}}\bigr)=o(I),
 \label{eq:prediction-unconditional-tail}$$ since $t^2/(1+t)\ge\epsilon t/(1+\epsilon)$ for $t>\epsilon$. For every projection of rank at most $cI$, $$\mathbb E|\Pi w|^2
 \le\epsilon cI+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(C\mathbf 1_{\{C>\epsilon\mathrm{Id}\}}\bigr)
            +|\mathbb Ew|^2.$$ First take the sequence limit, then $\epsilon\downarrow0$. Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-input">[eq:prediction-input]</a> proves Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-small-subspaces" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-small-subspaces">[eq:prediction-small-subspaces]</a> uniformly in $\Pi$. In the gap case a projection of rank at most $cI$ is eventually zero, which proves the same assertion.

Conditionally on $U_r$, the additional observation $L_1$ has signal $w$ and covariance $C_r$. Orthogonality of successive conditional expectations and the conditional affine prediction bound yield $$I\bigl(p_1(r)-m(r)\bigr)
 \ge\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(C_r^2(\mathrm{Id}+C_r)^{-1}\bigr).$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:prediction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prediction">18</a> makes the left side $o(I)$. The same scalar eigenvalue inequality proves Equation <a href="#eq:conditional-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditional-tail">[eq:conditional-tail]</a>.

<a id="eq:prediction-posterior-domination"></a>
<a id="eq:prediction-subspace-moment"></a>
We finally bound the dimensions of the deterministic outlier spaces. Let $$D=\mathbb E_\eta\mathbb E[XX^*\mid X+\sqrt sG=Y_0],$$ where the inner posterior kernel always refers to the original prior $\mu$. Conditional Jensen and the definition of $M_s$ imply $$\mathbb E_\eta b_sb_s^*\preceq D,\qquad
 \mathbb E_\eta M_s\preceq D/s.
 \label{eq:prediction-posterior-domination}$$ For each deterministic projection $Q$ of rank $l\ge1$, the original subgaussian assumption gives $$\log\mathbb E_\mu\exp\left(\frac{|QX|^2}{Ca^2}\right)\le Cl.
 \label{eq:prediction-subspace-moment}$$ Indeed choose a $1/2$-net of the unit sphere in $\operatorname{ran}Q$ with at most $5^l$ points. The norm $|QX|$ is at most twice the largest absolute linear form over this net, and bounding the exponential of a maximum by the sum proves the claim.

In the strict case, form the joint law by first drawing $Y_0$ from $\eta$ and then drawing $X$ from the original posterior kernel. Its entropy relative to the original joint law of $(X,X+\sqrt sG)$ is exactly $H_0=I/(2\lambda)$. Data processing therefore bounds the entropy of its $X$-marginal relative to $\mu$ by $H_0$. Applying the entropy variational inequality to Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-subspace-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-subspace-moment">[eq:prediction-subspace-moment]</a> gives <a id="eq:prediction-posterior-trace"></a>$$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(QD)\le Ca^2(H_0+l).
 \label{eq:prediction-posterior-trace}$$ If the spectral subspace of $D$ above $\tau>0$ has dimension $l$, then $\tau l\le Ca^2(H_0+l)$. For sufficiently small $a$ this implies $l\le2Ca^2H_0/\tau=O_\tau(I)$; the assertion is trivial when $l=0$. The spectral subspace of $\mathbb Eww^*$ above $\epsilon$ has dimension at most $I/\epsilon$, since its trace is $I$. Take $V_\epsilon$ to be the span of this subspace and the spectral subspace of $D$ above $s\epsilon$. Its dimension is at most $C_\epsilon I$, and Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-posterior-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-posterior-domination">[eq:prediction-posterior-domination]</a> gives all three bounds in Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-outlier-subspaces" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-outlier-subspaces">[eq:prediction-outlier-subspaces]</a>.

In the gap case, averaging the original posterior under $\eta=\mu_s$ gives $D=\mathbb E_\mu XX^*\preceq Ca^2\mathrm{Id}$. Thus Equation <a href="#eq:prediction-posterior-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prediction-posterior-domination">[eq:prediction-posterior-domination]</a> gives the two claimed operator bounds directly. Also $\|\mathbb Eww^*\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le I\to0$, so $V_\epsilon=\{0\}$ suffices eventually. This completes all assertions. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:hierarchy"></a>
### A symmetric hierarchy of nearly first eigenfunctions

The prediction curve from Section <a href="#sec:prediction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prediction">4</a> determines much more than the norm of a conditional mean. Its Gaussian Bessel inequality is asymptotically saturated at every fixed derivative order. We use this saturation to construct symmetric tensors whose derivatives remain nearly first eigenfunctions. We then show that every tensor has negligible energy in the curvature directions of $\mu_s$. Throughout this section, coefficient norms are unnormalized Euclidean norms, including for tensors of high order.

For a tensor field with $d$ slots, differentiation appends a last slot. A matrix subscript, such as $K_1$, specifies the slot on which it acts. Write $\|F\|_2^2=\mathbb E_\eta|F|^2$ and $\langle F,G\rangle_2=\mathbb E_\eta\langle F,G\rangle$. Every occurrence of $A$ on a tensor means its componentwise action. The following formulation records precisely the inputs used in the construction; the dimension and $I$ may vary along the sequence.

<a id="stmt-prop-hierarchy"></a>
<div id="prop:hierarchy" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:hierarchy-initial"></a>
**Proposition 20** (Symmetric tensor hierarchy). *Consider a sequence of smooth positive probability densities $\eta$ on finite-dimensional Euclidean spaces, with finite second moments. Let $A=-\Delta-D\log\eta\cdot D$, and suppose that $\eta$ satisfies Poincaré with constant at most $\lambda^{-1}$, where $\lambda\to1$. Fix $s=1/8$. Suppose smooth vector fields $b,w$ and a symmetric matrix field $K$ satisfy $$D\log\eta=-\frac{y-b(y)}s+\omega(y),\qquad
 Db=\mathrm{Id}-sK,\qquad 0\le K\le s^{-1}\mathrm{Id},$$ where either $\omega=w$ throughout the sequence or $\omega=0$ throughout the sequence. Assume $b,w\in L^2(\eta)$, $w$ is a gradient, and, with $I=\|w\|_2^2>0$, $$\label{eq:hierarchy-initial}
 w\in\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}A,\qquad
 \|(A-1)w\|_2=o(\sqrt I),\qquad
 |\mathbb E_\eta w|=o(\sqrt I),\qquad
 \langle w,Kw\rangle_2=o(I).$$ Assume also the following prediction and projection properties.*

1.  *For $Y\sim\eta$ and an independent standard Gaussian $G$, set $$U_r=Y+\sqrt r\,G,\quad h_r(u)=\mathbb E[w(Y)\mid U_r=u],\quad
     m(r)=I^{-1}\mathbb E|h_r(U_r)|^2.$$ For every fixed $0<r<1/4$, one has $m(r)\to e^{-r}$.*

<a id="eq:hierarchy-input-projection"></a>
<a id="eq:hierarchy-input-outliers"></a>
2.  *For every fixed $c<\infty$, $$\label{eq:hierarchy-input-projection}
     \sup_{\substack{\Pi^2=\Pi=\Pi^*\ \mathrm{deterministic}\\
                       \mathop{\mathrm{rank}}\Pi\le cI}}
           \|\Pi w\|_2^2=o(I).$$ For every $\epsilon>0$ there is a deterministic projection $\Pi_\epsilon$ of rank at most $C_\epsilon I$ such that $$\label{eq:hierarchy-input-outliers}
     \left\|\mathbb E[(1-\Pi_\epsilon)bb^*(1-\Pi_\epsilon)]\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
     +\left\|\mathbb E[(1-\Pi_\epsilon)\omega\omega^*
                      (1-\Pi_\epsilon)]\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\epsilon$$ eventually along the sequence.*

<a id="eq:hierarchy-input-tail"></a>
3.  *If $\omega=w$, the conditional covariance $C_r=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(w(Y)\mid U_r)$ satisfies, for every fixed $r\in(0,1/4)$ and $a>0$, $$\label{eq:hierarchy-input-tail}
     \mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(C_r\mathbf 1_{\{C_r>a\}}\bigr)=o(I).$$ No conditional covariance-tail assumption is made when $\omega=0$.*

<a id="eq:hierarchy-eigen"></a>
<a id="eq:hierarchy-curvature"></a>
<a id="eq:hierarchy-projection"></a>
*Then there are totally symmetric tensor fields $T_l$ of order $l+1$, for every fixed integer $l\ge0$, with $T_0=w$, such that $$\label{eq:hierarchy-eigen}
 \begin{gathered}
 \|T_l\|_2^2=(1+o(1))I,\qquad
 \|(A-1)T_l\|_2=o(\sqrt I),\\
 \|DT_l-T_{l+1}\|_2=o(\sqrt I).
 \end{gathered}$$ $$\label{eq:hierarchy-curvature}
 \langle T_l,K_1T_l\rangle_2=o(I).$$ $$\label{eq:hierarchy-projection}
 \sup_{\substack{\Pi^2=\Pi=\Pi^*\ \mathrm{deterministic}\\
                   \mathop{\mathrm{rank}}\Pi\le cI}}
      \|\Pi_1T_l\|_2^2=o(I)
 \qquad\text{for every fixed }c<\infty.$$ Every $T_l$ belongs componentwise to $\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}A$. For $l\ge1$ it can be chosen in $\bigcap_{p\ge1}\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}A^p$. By symmetry, the specified slot in Equations <a href="#eq:hierarchy-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-curvature">[eq:hierarchy-curvature]</a> and <a href="#eq:hierarchy-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-projection">[eq:hierarchy-projection]</a> may be any slot. All assertions can be made simultaneous for any fixed finite collection of levels and parameters.*

</div>

In the application, take $b=b_s$ and $K=K_s$. Proposition <a href="#prop:critical-sequence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:critical-sequence">15</a> supplies the drift, Poincaré, and initial estimates; Proposition <a href="#prop:prediction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prediction">18</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:posterior-outliers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:posterior-outliers">19</a> supply the remaining inputs. In the gap branch, $\omega=0$ and $\|\mathbb Ebb^*\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o(1)$, so one may take $\Pi_\epsilon=0$ in Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-input-outliers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-input-outliers">[eq:hierarchy-input-outliers]</a>. The normalization $I\to0$ makes Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-input-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-input-projection">[eq:hierarchy-input-projection]</a> automatic: eventually the only projection of rank at most $cI$ is zero. Thus no strict-branch probe estimate has been added to the gap-branch hypotheses.

We prove the proposition in three steps. First we construct the eigenfunction and derivative hierarchy. Second we expand its evolution along stationary diffusion and, in the strict branch, deduce operator-norm tail estimates. Finally those estimates justify a curvature integration by parts.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Gaussian Bessel saturation and the induction

We use the classical correspondence between Hermite polynomials and multiple Wiener integrals (Itô 1951, secs. 3–5) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Ito1951](#ref-Ito1951)], with the ordered-index normalization recorded below. The saturation argument will come from the prediction curve, in addition to this orthogonality.

<a id="eq:hierarchy-hermite-definition"></a>
<a id="eq:hierarchy-hermite-isometry"></a>
For a standard Gaussian vector $G\in\mathbb R^n$, define its symmetric Hermite tensors $\mathfrak h_j(G)$ by $$\label{eq:hierarchy-hermite-definition}
 e^{\xi\cdot G-|\xi|^2/2}
