 =\sum_{j\ge0}\frac1{j!}\,\mathfrak h_j(G):\xi^{\otimes j}.$$ Here a colon contracts all indicated slots and $\mathfrak h_0=1$. Comparing coefficients in the expectation of the product of two such exponentials gives, for symmetric order-$j$ and order-$k$ tensors $a,b$, $$\label{eq:hierarchy-hermite-isometry}
 \mathbb E[(a:\mathfrak h_j(G))(b:\mathfrak h_k(G))]
 =\mathbf 1_{\{j=k\}}j!\langle a,b\rangle.$$ This identity uses ordered coefficient indices; it explains all factorials below.

<a id="stmt-lem-hierarchy-bessel"></a>
<div id="lem:hierarchy-bessel" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:hierarchy-bessel"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Bessel inequality for the prediction). *For the data of Proposition <a href="#prop:hierarchy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hierarchy">20</a>, define $g_r(y)=\mathbb E_G h_r(y+\sqrt r\,G)$. For each $r>0$, $$\label{eq:hierarchy-bessel}
 \langle w,g_r\rangle_2=m(r)I,\qquad
 \sum_{j\ge0}\frac{r^j}{j!}\|D^jg_r\|_2^2\le m(r)I.$$ In particular, every fixed derivative appearing here is in the form domain of $A$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-bessel"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hierarchy-bessel</summary>

*Proof.* The first equality is conditional expectation: $$\mathbb E\langle w(Y),h_r(U_r)\rangle=\mathbb E|h_r(U_r)|^2.$$ For fixed $y$, Gaussian integration by parts identifies the order-$j$ Hermite coefficient of $h_r(y+\sqrt r\,G)$ as $r^{j/2}D^jg_r(y)$. Its orthogonal projection onto that chaos is $$\frac{r^{j/2}}{j!}D^jg_r(y):\mathfrak h_j(G),$$ where the vector output slot is not contracted. By Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-hermite-isometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-hermite-isometry">[eq:hierarchy-hermite-isometry]</a>, the squared norm of this projection is $r^j|D^jg_r(y)|^2/j!$. Bessel’s inequality, integrated in $y\sim\eta$, proves the asserted sum.

These calculations require only $\mathbb E|h_r(Y+\sqrt rG)|^2<\infty$. To justify the derivatives, on a compact set of starting points use positivity of $\eta$ on a slightly larger compact set. Integrating translated Gaussian kernels over that larger set bounds each fixed differentiated kernel by a constant times the mixture density of $U_r$. The enlargement absorbs the polynomial factors from differentiation. Thus the kernel integrals are classically smooth locally; alternatively all identities first hold distributionally. The Bessel bounds for orders $j$ and $j+1$ put $D^jg_r$ and its weak derivative in $L^2(\eta)$, hence in the form domain by cutoff and local smoothing. The local constants need not be uniform in the dimension: only the exact integrated Bessel inequality is used quantitatively. ◻

</details>

Two elementary Hilbert-space facts will prevent dimension factors and unjustified differentiations in the induction.

<a id="stmt-lem-hierarchy-smoothing"></a>
<div id="lem:hierarchy-smoothing" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:hierarchy-crosscov"></a>
<a id="eq:hierarchy-smoothing"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Cross-covariance and spectral smoothing). *If $F$ is a square-integrable random variable with values in a finite-dimensional Hilbert space and $Z$ is a centered Euclidean random vector with $\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}Z\le c\mathrm{Id}$, then $$\label{eq:hierarchy-crosscov}
 |\mathbb E(F\otimes Z)|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le c\mathbb E|F|^2.$$ Next, let $A$ be a sequence of nonnegative Dirichlet operators with kernel the constants and spectral gap at least $\lambda\to1$. If fields $S$ in their form domains satisfy $$|\mathbb ES|^2=o(I),\qquad \|S\|_2^2=(1+o(1))I,\qquad
 \|DS\|_2^2\le(1+o(1))I,$$ then $\widetilde S=e^{1-A}S$ satisfies $$\label{eq:hierarchy-smoothing}
 \|\widetilde S-S\|_2+\|D(\widetilde S-S)\|_2=o(\sqrt I),
 \qquad \|(A-1)\widetilde S\|_2=o(\sqrt I).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-smoothing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hierarchy-smoothing</summary>

*Proof.* The map $u\mapsto u\cdot Z$ from Euclidean space to scalar $L^2$ has operator norm at most $\sqrt c$. Apply its adjoint to each scalar coefficient of $F$ and sum the squared bounds. This proves Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-crosscov" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-crosscov">[eq:hierarchy-crosscov]</a> without a factor equal to the number of coordinates of either variable.

For the second assertion, sum the coefficient spectral measures of $S-\mathbb ES$ and call the resulting measure $\nu$. Then $$\int(x-\lambda)\,d\nu(x)
 =\|DS\|_2^2-\lambda\|S-\mathbb ES\|_2^2=o(I),$$ where the left side is nonnegative. For every fixed $\delta>0$, the spectral mass and the spectral energy outside $[1-\delta,1+\delta]$ are $o(I)$: the part below $1-\delta$ is absent eventually, and on $x\ge1+\delta$ both $1$ and $x$ are bounded by a $\delta$-dependent constant times $x-\lambda$. The constant component has squared norm $o(I)$ as well.

On the remaining interval the multipliers $e^{1-x}-1$ and $(x-1)e^{1-x}$ tend uniformly to zero as $\delta\downarrow0$. Outside it, $(1+x)|e^{1-x}-1|^2\le C(1+x)$ and $(x-1)^2e^{2-2x}\le C$. Integrate these inequalities against the spectral measure, first take the sequence limit and then let $\delta\downarrow0$. This gives Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-smoothing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-smoothing">[eq:hierarchy-smoothing]</a>. The same functional calculus shows that $e^{1-A}S$ belongs to every $\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}A^p$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-hierarchy-construction"></a>
<div id="lem:hierarchy-construction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Construction before the curvature estimate). *Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:hierarchy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hierarchy">20</a>, there exist totally symmetric fields $T_l$ satisfying Equations <a href="#eq:hierarchy-eigen" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-eigen">[eq:hierarchy-eigen]</a> and <a href="#eq:hierarchy-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-projection">[eq:hierarchy-projection]</a>, with the stated operator domains. Moreover $|\mathbb ET_l|=o(\sqrt I)$ for every fixed $l$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-construction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hierarchy-construction</summary>

*Proof.* Start with $T_0=w$. Suppose the assertions have been constructed through level $l$, and put $L=l+1$ and $V=DT_l$. Self-adjointness gives <a id="eq:hierarchy-raw-derivative"></a>$$\label{eq:hierarchy-raw-derivative}
 \|V\|_2^2=\langle T_l,AT_l\rangle_2=(1+o(1))I.$$ If $\Pi$ is an admissible deterministic projection acting on an existing tensor slot, it commutes with $A$ and differentiation. Consequently $$\|\Pi_1V\|_2^2
 =\langle\Pi_1T_l,A\Pi_1T_l\rangle_2
 \le\|\Pi_1T_l\|_2^2+
       \|\Pi_1T_l\|_2\|(A-1)T_l\|_2=o(I).$$ The error is uniform over projections of rank at most $cI$ for fixed $c$.

<a id="eq:hierarchy-correlations"></a>
Fix $0<r<1/4$. Set $V_j=T_j$ for $0\le j\le l$ and $V_L=V$. Successive form integration by parts gives $$\label{eq:hierarchy-correlations}
 I^{-1}\langle D^jg_r,V_j\rangle_2\longrightarrow e^{-r}
 \qquad(0\le j\le L).$$ Indeed the assertion at $j=0$ is Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-bessel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-bessel">[eq:hierarchy-bessel]</a>. For $j\le l$, $$\langle D^{j+1}g_r,DT_j\rangle_2
 =\langle D^jg_r,AT_j\rangle_2
 =\langle D^jg_r,T_j\rangle_2+o(I).$$ Replacing $DT_j$ by $T_{j+1}$ when $j<l$ costs $o(I)$. All test derivatives and their form energies are finite by Lemma <a href="#lem:hierarchy-bessel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hierarchy-bessel">21</a>; its bound controls every error for fixed $r$ and $j$. No uniform estimate as $r\downarrow0$ has been used.

Here is the precise Bessel budget. Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-correlations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-correlations">[eq:hierarchy-correlations]</a> and $\|V_j\|_2^2/I\to1$ imply $$\liminf I^{-1}e^{2r}\|D^jg_r\|_2^2\ge1
 \qquad(0\le j\le L).$$ Multiply Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-bessel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-bessel">[eq:hierarchy-bessel]</a> by $e^{2r}/I$ and subtract these minimum contributions. The resulting bounds are <a id="eq:hierarchy-budget-error"></a><a id="eq:hierarchy-budget-energy"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \limsup\frac{\|e^rD^Lg_r-V\|_2^2}{I}
 &\le\frac{L!}{r^L}
       \left(e^r-\sum_{j=0}^{L}\frac{r^j}{j!}\right)=O_L(r),
 \label{eq:hierarchy-budget-error}\\
 \limsup\frac{e^{2r}\|D^{L+1}g_r\|_2^2}{I}
 &\le\frac{(L+1)!}{r^{L+1}}
       \left(e^r-\sum_{j=0}^{L}\frac{r^j}{j!}\right)=1+O_L(r).
 \label{eq:hierarchy-budget-energy}
\end{aligned}$$ For the first line, expand the squared difference and use the correlation at order $L$; its normalized limit subtracts exactly one from the normalized squared derivative norm. Thus the exponential-series tail accounts for the entire excess, rather than merely bounding the norms.

Choose $r\downarrow0$ sufficiently slowly along the sequence in these two estimates. The field $S_0=e^rD^Lg_r$ then satisfies $$\|S_0-V\|_2=o(\sqrt I),\qquad
 \|DS_0\|_2^2\le(1+o(1))I.$$ It is symmetric in its last $L$ slots; $V$ is symmetric in its first $L$ slots. For $L\ge2$, the permutations of those two sets of slots generate every permutation of the $L+1$ slots. Indeed their adjacent transpositions together are all the adjacent transpositions. If a permutation fixes $S_0$, its action changes $V$ by at most $2\|S_0-V\|_2$; a permutation fixing $V$ costs zero. Expressing each permutation as a fixed finite word therefore shows that $V$ is $o(\sqrt I)$ from its full symmetrization. For $L=1$, $V=Dw$ is already symmetric because $w$ is a gradient. Let $S$ be the full symmetrization of $S_0$. Averaging coefficient permutations is an orthogonal contraction and commutes with differentiation, so <a id="eq:hierarchy-symmetric-approximant"></a>$$\label{eq:hierarchy-symmetric-approximant}
 \|S-V\|_2=o(\sqrt I),\qquad
 \|S\|_2^2=(1+o(1))I,\qquad
 \|DS\|_2^2\le(1+o(1))I.$$ The preceding projection estimate for $V$ now holds on its last slot too, since a coefficient-slot permutation moves it to an old slot with an $o(\sqrt I)$ error uniform over projections.

<a id="eq:hierarchy-two-center-identities"></a>
To apply Poincaré sharply we still need to center $V$. Write $C_y=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(T_l,y)$, $C_b=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(T_l,b)$, and $C_\omega=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(T_l,\omega)$, with the vector variable occupying the last slot. Testing the equation for $AT_l$ against centered coordinates gives $$\label{eq:hierarchy-two-center-identities}
 \mathbb EV=C_y+E_l,\qquad |E_l|=o(\sqrt I),\qquad
 \mathbb EV=s^{-1}(C_y-C_b)-C_\omega.$$ For clarity, the first exact identity before the error is $\mathbb EV=\mathbb E[(AT_l)\otimes(y-\mathbb Ey)]$. Poincaré gives $\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_\eta y\le\lambda^{-1}\mathrm{Id}$, and Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-crosscov" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-crosscov">[eq:hierarchy-crosscov]</a> bounds its error by $\lambda^{-1/2}\|(A-1)T_l\|_2$. This is the needed dimension-free estimate. The second identity follows by integration by parts using $Ay=(y-b)/s-\omega$. These coordinate tests are legitimate in the form domain; their distributional $A$-images are in $L^2$ by the stated second-moment assumptions, hence they are also in $\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}A$.

Project the last slot onto $1-\Pi_\epsilon$. The two covariance terms involving $b$ and $\omega$ have norm at most $C\sqrt{\epsilon I}$ by Equations <a href="#eq:hierarchy-input-outliers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-input-outliers">[eq:hierarchy-input-outliers]</a> and <a href="#eq:hierarchy-crosscov" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-crosscov">[eq:hierarchy-crosscov]</a>. Subtracting the two identities in Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-two-center-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-two-center-identities">[eq:hierarchy-two-center-identities]</a>, and using $s\ne1$, therefore bounds this part of $\mathbb EV$ by $C_s\sqrt{\epsilon I}+o(\sqrt I)$. On the $\Pi_\epsilon$ part, $$|(\Pi_\epsilon)_{L+1}\mathbb EV|
 \le\|(\Pi_\epsilon)_{L+1}V\|_2=o(\sqrt I)$$ by the projected-energy estimate and $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}\Pi_\epsilon\le C_\epsilon I$. First take the sequence limit and then let $\epsilon\downarrow0$. This proves $|\mathbb EV|=o(\sqrt I)$, and thus $|\mathbb ES|=o(\sqrt I)$.

Lemma <a href="#lem:hierarchy-smoothing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hierarchy-smoothing">22</a> applies to $S$. Define $$T_{l+1}=e^{1-A}S.$$ It is symmetric, lies in every operator-power domain, is $o(\sqrt I)$ from $V$, and satisfies the strong near-eigenfunction estimate. Its mean is $e\mathbb ES=o(\sqrt I)$. Finally, coefficient projections commute with the semigroup, and $\|e^{1-A}\|_{2\to2}\le e$. Together with $S-V=o_{L^2}(\sqrt I)$, this preserves the uniform projected-energy estimates. The induction is complete. Each step used only a finite collection of limits; diagonal choices give the assertion for all fixed levels. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Expansion along stationary diffusion

The fields just constructed retain the same norm under repeated differentiation. This makes their stochastic Taylor expansion summable with a factorial remainder. We use the ordered-integral form of the Wiener–Itô correspondence (Itô 1951, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Ito1951](#ref-Ito1951)]. Let $Y_t$ be the stationary diffusion with invariant density $\eta$, $$dY_t=\sqrt2\,dB_t+D\log\eta(Y_t)\,dt,\qquad Y_0\sim\eta,$$ where $Y_0$ is independent of the driving Brownian motion.

<a id="stmt-lem-hermite-expansion"></a>
<div id="lem:hermite-expansion" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:hierarchy-diffusion-expansion"></a>
**Lemma 24** (Hermite expansion of the stationary evolution). *Let symmetric fields $T_l$ satisfy Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-eigen" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-eigen">[eq:hierarchy-eigen]</a>. For fixed integers $l,m\ge0$ and fixed $t>0$, set $N=B_t/\sqrt t$. Then $$\label{eq:hierarchy-diffusion-expansion}
 T_l(Y_t)=e^{-t}\sum_{j=0}^m\frac{(2t)^{j/2}}{j!}
       T_{l+j}(Y_0):\mathfrak h_j(N)+\mathcal E_{l,m,t},$$ where the colon contracts the last $j$ tensor slots and <a id="eq:hierarchy-diffusion-remainder"></a>$$\label{eq:hierarchy-diffusion-remainder}
 \limsup\frac{\|\mathcal E_{l,m,t}\|_2}{\sqrt I}
 \le\frac{(2t)^{(m+1)/2}}{\sqrt{(m+1)!}}.$$ For $0\le t\le T<\infty$ the same bound holds uniformly with $t$ replaced on the right by $T$ and with the expansion at $t=0$ interpreted by continuity. The limits are also simultaneous for any fixed finite set of starting levels. In particular the normalized remainder tends to zero when the sequence limit is followed by $m\to\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hermite-expansion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hermite-expansion</summary>

*Proof.* The operator-domain Itô formula from Lemma <a href="#lem:dirichlet-core" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dirichlet-core">4</a> gives, for each fixed $d$, $$\begin{aligned}
 e^vT_d(Y_v)
 ={}&T_d(Y_0)+\sqrt2\int_0^v e^uT_{d+1}(Y_u):dB_u\\
 &+\int_0^v e^u(1-A)T_d(Y_u)\,du
   +\sqrt2\int_0^v e^u(DT_d-T_{d+1})(Y_u):dB_u.
\end{aligned}$$ Stationarity, Cauchy–Schwarz for the time integral, and Itô isometry show that the last two terms are $o(\sqrt I)$, uniformly for $v\in[0,T]$ in squared-mean norm. Substitute this formula for the integrand at the next level, and repeat $m+1$ times. For fixed $l,m,T$, every replacement error remains $o(\sqrt I)$; iterated isometry sums coefficient squares and introduces no dimension factor.

After multiplication by $e^{-t}$, the remaining integral has coefficient $2^{(m+1)/2}$, is over the ordered time simplex of length $t$, and has innermost integrand $e^{u-t}T_{l+m+1}(Y_u)$. Since $u\le t$, its squared norm is at most $$2^{m+1}\frac{t^{m+1}}{(m+1)!}
       \|T_{l+m+1}\|_2^2
 =(1+o(1))I\frac{(2t)^{m+1}}{(m+1)!}.$$ This proves the remainder estimate and its uniform version. The other terms have coefficients evaluated at $Y_0$. Symmetry in their Brownian slots turns their ordered iterated integrals into $t^{j/2}\mathfrak h_j(N)/j!$. To verify the factor directly, expand the Brownian exponential martingale $\exp(\xi\cdot B_t-t|\xi|^2/2)$ by iterated Itô integration and compare its coefficients with Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-hermite-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-hermite-definition">[eq:hierarchy-hermite-definition]</a>. The additional $\sqrt2$ at each stochastic integration gives exactly $(2t)^{j/2}/j!$ in Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-diffusion-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-diffusion-expansion">[eq:hierarchy-diffusion-expansion]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Operator tails in the strict branch

For a tensor $F$, a one-slot flattening is the matrix whose row index is one specified tensor slot and whose column index is the tuple of all remaining slots. Its Hilbert–Schmidt norm is exactly $|F|$.

In the strict branch, put $Q=Dw$. The drift decomposition gives $D^2(-\log\eta)=K-Q$. Viewing $DF$ as a matrix with its derivative slot as row index, commuting a derivative with $A$ formally gives $$D(AF)=A(DF)+(K-Q)DF.$$ For $F=T_l$, both $Q$ and $DF$ belong to $L^2$, but their product $QDF$ need not belong to $L^2$. We cannot yet use this formula to deduce the curvature estimate. The conditional covariance bound will instead let us approximate $DF$ by tests $P$ whose uniform operator norms tend to zero. Then $\|QP\|_2\le\|P\|_{\mathrm{op},\infty}\|Q\|_2=o(\sqrt I)$, which is the product bound needed for the justified identity below. This step is needed only when $\omega=w$; in the gap branch $Q=0$.

<a id="stmt-lem-hierarchy-operator-tails"></a>
<div id="lem:hierarchy-operator-tails" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:hierarchy-operator-tail"></a>
**Lemma 25** (Vanishing operator tails). *In the strict branch of Proposition <a href="#prop:hierarchy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hierarchy">20</a>, $Q=Dw$ and the fields $T_l$ constructed in Lemma <a href="#lem:hierarchy-construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hierarchy-construction">23</a> satisfy the following property. For every fixed $\delta>0$, each one-slot flattening $F$ of $T_l$, $l\ge1$, and also $F=Q$, obeys $$\label{eq:hierarchy-operator-tail}
 \mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(FF^*\mathbf 1_{\{FF^*>\delta^2\}}\bigr)=o(I).$$ Equivalently, clipping every singular value at any fixed positive cutoff changes the field by $o(\sqrt I)$ in $L^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-operator-tails"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hierarchy-operator-tails</summary>

<a id="eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket"></a>
*Proof.* We first prove the assertion for $Q$. Couple the heat observations by $U_v=Y_0+W_v$, where $W$ is a standard Brownian motion independent of $Y_0\sim\eta$, and retain $h_v=\mathbb E[w(Y_0)\mid U_v]$. Put $H_v=Dh_v(U_v)$ wherever this derivative is square integrable. The conditional covariance has the exact representation $$\label{eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket}
 C_r=\int_0^r\mathbb E[H_vH_v^*\mid U_r]\,dv,
 \qquad
 \int_0^r\mathbb E|H_v|^2\,dv=(1-m(r))I.$$ To check the first identity and its normalization, let $p_v$ denote the density of $U_v$. The entries of $p_vh_v$ solve the heat equation with generator $\Delta/2$. Direct differentiation gives the matrix identity $$(\partial_v-\tfrac12\Delta)(p_vh_vh_v^*)
 =-p_vDh_v(Dh_v)^*.$$ Integrate it against the later heat kernel and divide by $p_r$. The initial term becomes $\mathbb E[ww^*\mid U_r]$, and the final term is $h_rh_r^*$, which proves the first formula. Taking the trace and expectation proves the second formula. For nonsmooth or unbounded initial $w$, perform the calculation on scalar projections with convex truncations increasing to the square, and then use polarization. Spatial cutoffs and the square-integrability of $w$ justify the positive-time passage. At time zero, approximation of $w$ in $L^2(\eta)$ by bounded continuous functions gives $\mathbb E|w-\mathbb E[w\mid U_v]|^2\to0$. Thus there is no missing boundary term in Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket">[eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket]</a>.

By Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-initial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-initial">[eq:hierarchy-initial]</a>, $\|Q\|_2^2=\langle w,Aw\rangle_2=(1+o(1))I$. For almost every $v>0$, convolution differentiation and form integration by parts give <a id="eq:hierarchy-derivative-correlation"></a>$$\label{eq:hierarchy-derivative-correlation}
 \mathbb E\langle Q(Y_0),H_v\rangle
 =\langle Dw,Dg_v\rangle_2
 =\langle Aw,g_v\rangle_2=m(v)I+o(I).$$ The error is uniform in $v$: Jensen’s inequality gives $\|g_v\|_2\le\sqrt I$, so it is bounded by $\|(A-1)w\|_2\sqrt I$. The convolution derivative identity follows first with cutoffs and bounded functions, then by the square-integrable derivative bounds in Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket">[eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket]</a>.

<a id="eq:hierarchy-integrated-discrepancy"></a>
Fix a small $z>0$ with $2z<1/4$. Combining the last two equations and using $0\le m(v)\le1$ yields the exact limiting discrepancy $$\begin{aligned}
 \lim\frac1I\int_0^{2z}\mathbb E|Q(Y_0)-H_v|^2\,dv
 &=2z+1-e^{-2z}-2\int_0^{2z}e^{-v}\,dv\\
 &=2z-1+e^{-2z}=O(z^2).
 \label{eq:hierarchy-integrated-discrepancy}
\end{aligned}$$ Dominated convergence is applicable to $m(v)$, so this calculation does not require uniform convergence of the prediction curve near zero.

<a id="eq:hierarchy-selected-time"></a>
We select a time in $[z,2z]$ while preserving a covariance-tail bound. For a fixed $\kappa>0$, define $$a(r)=I^{-1}\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\left(\frac{C_r}{r}
                   \mathbf 1_{\{C_r/r>\kappa\}}\right).$$ For fixed $r\in[z,2z]$, Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-input-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-input-tail">[eq:hierarchy-input-tail]</a> gives $a(r)\to0$, and $a(r)\le1/z$ follows from $\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}C_r\le I$. Consequently $\int_z^{2z}a(r)\,dr\to0$. On the other hand, Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-integrated-discrepancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-integrated-discrepancy">[eq:hierarchy-integrated-discrepancy]</a> and Markov’s inequality show that the set of times satisfying $$\mathbb E|Q(Y_0)-H_r|^2\le C zI$$ has measure at least $z/2$, with an absolute $C$ and for sufficiently far terms of the sequence. The set where $a(r)$ exceeds a quantity tending to zero has measure tending to zero. Select $r\in[z,2z]$ in the intersection. Thus $r$ may vary with the sequence index, but $$\label{eq:hierarchy-selected-time}
 \mathbb E|Q(Y_0)-H_r|^2\le CzI,\qquad
 \mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\left(\frac{C_r}{r}\mathbf 1_{\{C_r/r>\kappa\}}\right)=o(I).$$ All constants in this selection are for fixed $z,\kappa$.

<a id="eq:hierarchy-product-trace"></a>
<a id="eq:hierarchy-bracket-comparison"></a>
We compare $B=H_rH_r^*$ with $C=C_r/r$ in trace norm. For matrices $U,V$, $$\label{eq:hierarchy-product-trace}
 \|UU^*-VV^*\|_1
 \le |U-V|_{\mathrm{HS}}(|U|_{\mathrm{HS}}+|V|_{\mathrm{HS}}).$$ Compare both $B$ and $C$ with $\mathbb E[QQ^*\mid U_r]$. For $B$, conditional Jensen’s inequality and the first bound in Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-selected-time" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-selected-time">[eq:hierarchy-selected-time]</a> give an $O(\sqrt z)I$ bound on the expected trace-norm difference. For $C$, use the first identity in Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket">[eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket]</a>, followed by Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-product-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-product-trace">[eq:hierarchy-product-trace]</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz in both probability and time. The integrated discrepancy is $O(z^2)I$, whereas $$\int_0^r\mathbb E(|H_v|+|Q|)^2\,dv
 \le 2(1-m(2z))I+4z\|Q\|_2^2.$$ Here we used $r\le2z$ and the nonnegative integral in the second identity of Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket">[eq:hierarchy-conditional-bracket]</a>. The normalized limsup of the right side is $O(z)$, by prediction at the fixed time $2z$. No prediction limit at the selected varying time is needed. Division by $r\ge z$ therefore also costs at most $O(\sqrt z)I$. We have proved, in normalized limsup, $$\label{eq:hierarchy-bracket-comparison}
 \mathbb E\|B-C\|_1\le O(\sqrt z)I.$$

Fix $\delta>0$, take $\kappa=\delta^2/2$ above, and set $P=\mathbf 1_{\{B>\delta^2\}}$. Positivity of $C$ implies $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(BP)\le\frac{\delta^2}2\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}P
      +\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(C\mathbf 1_{\{C>\delta^2/2\}}\bigr)+\|B-C\|_1.$$ Since $\delta^2\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}P\le\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(BP)$, the first term is absorbed into the left side. Equations <a href="#eq:hierarchy-selected-time" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-selected-time">[eq:hierarchy-selected-time]</a> and <a href="#eq:hierarchy-bracket-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-bracket-comparison">[eq:hierarchy-bracket-comparison]</a> bound the remaining high singular-value energy by $O(\sqrt z)I+o(I)$. Clip $H_r$ at $\delta$; its squared distance from the operator-norm ball of radius $\delta$ is at most this energy. The first estimate in Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-selected-time" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-selected-time">[eq:hierarchy-selected-time]</a> then gives the same bound, up to constants, for the distance of $Q$ from that ball. Let $z\downarrow0$ after the sequence limit. The squared distance is $o(I)$ for every fixed $\delta>0$. Applying this distance assertion at cutoff $\delta/2$ bounds the energy of singular values above $\delta$ by four times the squared distance, proving Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-operator-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-operator-tail">[eq:hierarchy-operator-tail]</a> for $Q$. Since $\|T_1-Q\|_2=o(\sqrt I)$, it also proves the assertion for $T_1$.

<a id="eq:hierarchy-random-projection"></a>
It remains to propagate this estimate to higher levels. Fix $l\ge1$ and $t>0$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:hermite-expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hermite-expansion">24</a> with starting level $1$. Conditional on $Y_0$, the Hermite chaoses are orthogonal even after applying an arbitrary measurable projection $P(Y_0)$ to the first tensor slot. The chaos containing $T_l(Y_0)$ has squared coefficient $$c_{l,t}=e^{-2t}\frac{(2t)^{l-1}}{(l-1)!}>0.$$ Use the expansion to order $m\ge l-1$, first take the sequence limit, and then let $m\to\infty$ in its remainder bound. The resulting inequality is $$\label{eq:hierarchy-random-projection}
 \liminf\frac{
   \mathbb E|P(Y_0)_1T_1(Y_t)|^2-c_{l,t}\mathbb E|P(Y_0)_1T_l(Y_0)|^2}{I}\ge0.$$ The remainder estimates are uniform over such projections, since their operator norms are at most one.

Choose $P(Y_0)$ to project onto the singular values greater than a fixed $\epsilon>0$ of the first-slot flattening of $T_l(Y_0)$. Then $$\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}P\le\epsilon^{-2}\|T_l\|_2^2
 =(1+o(1))\epsilon^{-2}I.$$ For every $\delta>0$, let $S_\delta$ be the singular-value clipping of $T_1$ at $\delta$. Pointwise, $$|P(Y_0)S_\delta(Y_t)|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le\delta^2\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}P(Y_0).$$ Stationarity controls the $o(I)$ clipping error for $T_1(Y_t)$. Hence the normalized limsup of the first expectation in Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-random-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-random-projection">[eq:hierarchy-random-projection]</a> is at most $\delta^2/\epsilon^2$. Send $\delta\downarrow0$ to obtain the tail claim at threshold $\epsilon$ for $T_l$. No independence between $P(Y_0)$ and $T_1(Y_t)$ was required. Tensor symmetry supplies every other one-slot flattening. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The curvature estimate and its domains

It remains to prove the curvature assertion of the hierarchy. We first justify the differentiated operator identity against a test whose product with the curvature is in $L^2$. The preceding tail estimate will supply such a test in the strict branch; the gap branch will use $T_{l+1}$ directly.

<div class="samepage">

<a id="stmt-lem-hierarchy-divergence"></a>
<div id="lem:hierarchy-divergence" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:hierarchy-divergence-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:hierarchy-differentiated-operator"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Weighted divergence and the differentiated operator). *Let $\eta$ be a smooth positive probability density, let $A=-\Delta-D\log\eta\cdot D$, and put $H=D^2(-\log\eta)$. Let $P=(P_{i\alpha})$ have one vector slot $i$ and a finite-dimensional coefficient index $\alpha$. Suppose every coefficient belongs to $\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}A$ and $HP\in L^2(\eta)$. Then the weighted adjoint divergence $$(D^*P)_\alpha=-\sum_i\partial_iP_{i\alpha}
                   -\sum_i(\partial_i\log\eta)P_{i\alpha}$$ belongs to $L^2(\eta)$ and satisfies $$\label{eq:hierarchy-divergence-bound}
 \|D^*P\|_2^2\le\|DP\|_2^2+\langle P,HP\rangle_2.$$ For every coefficient field $F\in\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}A$, $$\label{eq:hierarchy-differentiated-operator}
 \langle AF,D^*P\rangle_2
 =\langle DF,AP\rangle_2+\langle DF,HP\rangle_2.$$ In particular this identity requires $HP\in L^2$, not $HDF\in L^2$.*

</div>

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-divergence"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hierarchy-divergence</summary>

<a id="eq:hierarchy-divergence-identity"></a>
*Proof.* For compactly supported smooth $P$, commuting two first derivatives in weighted integration by parts gives $$\label{eq:hierarchy-divergence-identity}
 \|D^*P\|_2^2
 =\sum_{i,j,\alpha}
       \langle\partial_jP_{i\alpha},\partial_iP_{j\alpha}\rangle_2
   +\langle P,HP\rangle_2.$$ Cauchy–Schwarz applied to the two arrays with $i,j$ interchanged bounds the sum by $\|DP\|_2^2$, without a dimension factor.

For the stated $P$, membership in $\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}A$ gives $P\in H^2_{\rm loc}$ by local elliptic regularity and $DP\in L^2(\eta)$. Choose smooth cutoffs $0\le\chi_R\le1$ tending to one with $\|D\chi_R\|_\infty\to0$. Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-divergence-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-divergence-identity">[eq:hierarchy-divergence-identity]</a> applies to $\chi_RP$ by local Sobolev approximation. Since $$\|D(\chi_RP)-DP\|_2\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 \langle\chi_RP,H\chi_RP\rangle_2
   \longrightarrow\langle P,HP\rangle_2,$$ its right side is bounded. The second convergence uses $|\langle P,HP\rangle|\in L^1$, a consequence of $P,HP\in L^2$. Weak compactness in $L^2$ and distributional convergence of $D^*(\chi_RP)$ identify the limit as $D^*P$. Lower semicontinuity proves Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-divergence-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-divergence-bound">[eq:hierarchy-divergence-bound]</a>. Only first derivatives of the cutoffs have entered the estimate.

For compactly supported smooth $F$, differentiate the scalar operator: $$D(AF)=A(DF)+HDF.$$ Pair this identity with $P$, use symmetry of $H$, and integrate by parts to obtain Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-differentiated-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-differentiated-operator">[eq:hierarchy-differentiated-operator]</a>. For general $F\in\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}A$, use the operator core from Lemma <a href="#lem:dirichlet-core" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dirichlet-core">4</a> to choose compact smooth $F_j$ with $F_j\to F$ and $AF_j\to AF$ in $L^2$. This convergence also holds in the form norm, since $$\|D(F_j-F)\|_2^2
 =\langle F_j-F,A(F_j-F)\rangle_2\longrightarrow0.$$ All terms in Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-differentiated-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-differentiated-operator">[eq:hierarchy-differentiated-operator]</a> therefore converge: the three fixed factors are $D^*P$, $AP$, and $HP$, each in $L^2$. This proves the claimed global identity. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-divergence-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hierarchy-divergence</summary>

<a id="eq:hierarchy-curvature-test"></a>
*Completion of the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:hierarchy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hierarchy">20</a>.* Lemma <a href="#lem:hierarchy-construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hierarchy-construction">23</a> supplies the fields and all assertions except Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-curvature">[eq:hierarchy-curvature]</a>. Fix $l\ge0$ and write $F=T_l$. View $DF$ as a matrix with its derivative slot as row index and all old tensor slots as column index. We construct a matrix field $P$ satisfying $$\label{eq:hierarchy-curvature-test}
 \|P-DF\|_2+\|(A-1)P\|_2=o(\sqrt I),\qquad
 \|DP\|_2=O(\sqrt I),\qquad \|QP\|_2=o(\sqrt I),$$ where $Q=Dw$ in the strict branch and $Q=0$ in the gap branch.

In the strict branch, apply Lemma <a href="#lem:hierarchy-operator-tails" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hierarchy-operator-tails">25</a> to the corresponding flattening of $T_{l+1}$. Choose a singular-value cutoff $\delta\downarrow0$ sufficiently slowly that the clipped field $C$ obeys $$\|C-T_{l+1}\|_2=o(\sqrt I),\qquad
 \|C(y)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\delta.$$ Set $P=e^{1-A}C=eP_1C$. Markov averaging and convexity of the operator-norm ball give $\|P(y)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le e\delta$. The spectral multipliers $e^{1-x}$, $(x-1)e^{1-x}$, and $\sqrt x\,e^{1-x}$ are bounded for $x\ge0$. Moreover $|e^{1-x}-1|\le C|x-1|$ there. These bounds, the approximation of $C$, and the near-eigenfunction assertion for $T_{l+1}$ give the first two estimates in Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-curvature-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-curvature-test">[eq:hierarchy-curvature-test]</a>. Finally $\|Q\|_2^2=(1+o(1))I$ and $$\|QP\|_2\le\|P\|_{\mathrm{op},\infty}\|Q\|_2=o(\sqrt I).$$ The clipping is only used to construct $P$, so it need not preserve tensor symmetry. In the gap branch take $P=T_{l+1}$ directly. The first two estimates follow from Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-eigen" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-eigen">[eq:hierarchy-eigen]</a>, and the last is immediate from $Q=0$. No operator-tail argument is needed in this branch.

The drift hypothesis gives $H=D^2(-\log\eta)=K-Q$ in either case. Both $KP$ and $QP$ are in $L^2$, and the operator-domain assertion for $P$ was retained by its construction. Lemma <a href="#lem:hierarchy-divergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hierarchy-divergence">26</a> therefore applies. In particular, $$\|D^*P\|_2^2
 \le\|DP\|_2^2+s^{-1}\|P\|_2^2+\|P\|_2\|QP\|_2=O(I).$$ Insert $AF=F+(A-1)F$ and $AP=P+(A-1)P$ into Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-differentiated-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-differentiated-operator">[eq:hierarchy-differentiated-operator]</a>. The terms $\langle F,D^*P\rangle_2$ and $\langle DF,P\rangle_2$ cancel. Consequently $$\begin{aligned}
 \langle DF,KP\rangle_2
 ={}&\langle(A-1)F,D^*P\rangle_2
     -\langle DF,(A-1)P\rangle_2
     +\langle DF,QP\rangle_2
 =o(I).
\end{aligned}$$ Since $\|K\|_{\mathrm{op},\infty}\le s^{-1}$ and $P-DF=o_{L^2}(\sqrt I)$, this gives $$\langle DF,KDF\rangle_2=o(I).$$ Replacing $DF$ by $T_{l+1}$ costs $o(I)$ by the derivative estimate. It proves Equation <a href="#eq:hierarchy-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-curvature">[eq:hierarchy-curvature]</a> at level $l+1$ on the derivative slot, and hence on every slot by symmetry. Level zero was an initial assumption. All claims of the proposition follow. ◻

</details>

To use many tensor degrees at once, first impose the estimates on a fixed finite list of levels, projection-rank constants, and times. Then increase that list sufficiently slowly along the original sequence. For example, one may choose $k\to\infty$ so that the normalized errors in Proposition <a href="#prop:hierarchy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hierarchy">20</a> tend to zero uniformly through level $3k$, while $\sup_{l\le3k}\|T_l\|_2^2/I$ stays bounded. The same diagonal applies to any fixed number of terms in Lemma <a href="#lem:hermite-expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hermite-expansion">24</a> and any fixed bounded time interval. Thus averaging over a band of degrees or shifting that band by a fixed integer does not change any of the preceding normalized estimates.

<a id="sec:matrices"></a>
### The reduced square root and a frozen matrix algebra

The hierarchy of Proposition <a href="#prop:hierarchy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hierarchy">20</a> contains tensors of arbitrarily high order whose derivatives have almost the same energy as the tensors themselves. We first transfer this near equality to the positive square root of their one-slot reduction. We then describe that square root along the stationary diffusion by matrices frozen at the initial point. The construction uses weighted traces throughout; in particular, no lower bound on a nonzero eigenvalue will be required.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Sharp energy for the reduced square root

All limits in this section are along the counterexample sequence. We make the following diagonal choice explicit. For each finite integer $K$, impose the conclusions of Proposition <a href="#prop:hierarchy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hierarchy">20</a> through level $3K$, the fixed-projection conclusions for rank coefficients at most $K$, and the estimates of Lemma <a href="#lem:hermite-expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hermite-expansion">24</a> for every starting level $0\le l<2K$ and every truncation order $0\le m\le K$, on time intervals $0\le t\le K$. Only levels through $3K$ enter these expansions, including their remainders. For each such finite list, the hierarchy defects and the excesses over the normalized Hermite remainder bounds can be made arbitrarily small. Choose an integer $k\to\infty$ slowly enough that these defects and excesses tend to zero, uniformly over the corresponding list. Further auxiliary parameters below will be chosen by the same finite-list procedure. In particular, normalized endpoint energies remain bounded when a band of $k$ levels is shifted by a fixed number of levels.

Regard the direct sum $$\Psi=\bigoplus_{k\le l<2k}\frac{T_l}{\sqrt{kI}}$$ as a matrix by separating the first tensor index from the remaining indices and the direct-sum label. Define <a id="eq:matrix-reduction"></a>$$\label{eq:matrix-reduction}
 \rho=\Psi\Psi^*,\qquad R=\rho^{1/2}.$$ High tensor degree provides many interchangeable slots, while averaging adjacent degrees makes each fixed shift cost only endpoint terms of order $k^{-1}$. These two features will force sharp energy estimates for the positive matrix field $R$.

<a id="eq:matrix-normalization"></a>
Here $R$ is a positive matrix field, distinct from the scalar log-Sobolev constant $R(\nu)$. Write $\|\cdot\|_2$ for the field norm formed with ordinary Hilbert–Schmidt coefficient norms. The hierarchy gives $$\label{eq:matrix-normalization}
 \mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}R^2=\|\Psi\|_2^2\longrightarrow1,
 \qquad \|D\Psi\|_2^2\longrightarrow1.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-matrix-centering"></a>
<div id="lem:matrix-centering" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27** (Centering and the square-root energy). *The field $R$ belongs to the Dirichlet form domain and satisfies $$\|\mathbb ER\|_{\mathrm{HS}}=o(1),\qquad
 \liminf\|DR\|_2^2\ge1,\qquad \|DR\|_2^2=O(1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-matrix-centering"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-matrix-centering</summary>

<a id="eq:matrix-root-lipschitz"></a>
*Proof.* For rectangular matrices $X,Y$, the Araki–Yamagami inequality (Araki and Yamagami 1981, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[ArakiYamagami1981](#ref-ArakiYamagami1981)] gives the following square-root bound. We give a finite-dimensional proof by self-adjoint dilation: $$\label{eq:matrix-root-lipschitz}
 \|\sqrt{XX^*}-\sqrt{YY^*}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le 2\|X-Y\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.$$ Indeed the absolute value of the dilation of $X$ has diagonal blocks $\sqrt{XX^*}$ and $\sqrt{X^*X}$. If $A,B$ are self-adjoint and $f$ is scalar Lipschitz, expansion in an eigenbasis of $A$ and an eigenbasis of $B$ shows that $\|f(A)-f(B)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le\operatorname{Lip}(f)\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{HS}}$. Apply this with $f(x)=|x|$. Difference quotients, followed by form approximation, prove that $R$ is in the form domain and that $\|DR\|_2^2\le2\|D\Psi\|_2^2$.

<a id="eq:matrix-independent-centering"></a>
The curvature conclusion of the hierarchy yields $$\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((1-M_s)R^2)=o(1).$$ We also have $$\label{eq:matrix-independent-centering}
 \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((\mathbb EM_s)(\mathbb ER^2))=o(1).$$ To see the latter assertion in the strict branch, fix $\delta>0$ and let $P_\delta$ be the spectral projection of $\mathbb EM_s$ onto $(\delta,\infty)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:posterior-outliers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:posterior-outliers">19</a> gives $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}P_\delta=O_\delta(I)$; the deterministic-projection conclusion of Proposition <a href="#prop:hierarchy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hierarchy">20</a> therefore gives $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(P_\delta\mathbb ER^2)=o(1)$. Since $0\le M_s\le1$, the left side of Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-independent-centering" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-independent-centering">[eq:matrix-independent-centering]</a> is at most $o(1)+\delta\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}R^2$. Send $\delta$ to zero after the sequence limit. In the gap branch use directly $\|\mathbb EM_s\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o(1)$.

Let $R'$ be evaluated at an independent point. Insert $1-M_s+M_s$ between $R$ and $R'$ in $\|\mathbb ER\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(RR')$. Hilbert–Schmidt Cauchy–Schwarz bounds the term containing $1-M_s$ by $$\bigl(\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((1-M_s)R^2)\bigr)^{1/2}
 \bigl(\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}R^2\bigr)^{1/2}=o(1).$$ The other term is bounded by $$\bigl(\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(M_sR^2)\bigr)^{1/2}
 \bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((\mathbb EM_s)(\mathbb ER^2))\bigr)^{1/2}=o(1).$$ Poincaré for $\eta$, whose constant tends to one, now gives the lower bound on $\|DR\|_2^2$ from Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-normalization">[eq:matrix-normalization]</a>. ◻

</details>

The next estimate is the reason for using tensors with many symmetric slots. It compares averaging an action over those slots with the geometric, rather than arithmetic, weighting by the marginal matrix.

<a id="stmt-lem-matrix-copied-slots"></a>
<div id="lem:matrix-copied-slots" class="lemma">

**Lemma 28** (Averaging over symmetric tensor slots). *Let $\psi$ be a finite direct sum of symmetric tensors, each with at least $k\ge2$ slots. Let $\rho$ be its one-slot reduction and $R=\sqrt\rho$. For a self-adjoint matrix $H$, let $H_{\mathrm{av}}$ act on each component by averaging the action of $H$ over its slots. Then <a id="eq:matrix-copied-slots"></a>$$\label{eq:matrix-copied-slots}
 \|H_{\mathrm{av}}\psi\|^2
 \le\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(RHRH)+\frac{2}{k}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho H^2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-matrix-copied-slots"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-matrix-copied-slots</summary>

*Proof.* For a component $\psi_d$ with $d$ slots, put $$a_d=\langle\psi_d,H_{(1)}^2\psi_d\rangle,
 \qquad c_d=\langle\psi_d,H_{(1)}H_{(2)}\psi_d\rangle.$$ Symmetry and Cauchy–Schwarz give $|c_d|\le a_d$, and direct expansion gives $$\|H_{\mathrm{av}}\psi\|^2
 =\sum_d\left(\frac{a_d}{d}+\left(1-\frac1d\right)c_d\right)
 \le\sum_d c_d+\frac2k\sum_d a_d.$$ It remains to prove $|\sum_d c_d|\le\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(RHRH)$. This correlation bound can also be deduced from the Wigner–Yanase case of Lieb’s trace-concavity theorem (Lieb 1973) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Lieb1973](#ref-Lieb1973)]; we give a proof by weighted interpolation.

Define the linear map $C$ by $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(XC(Y))=\sum_d\langle\psi_d,X_{(1)}Y_{(2)}\psi_d\rangle.$$ Flattening all remaining indices exhibits $C(Y)$ as a sum of terms $A_\alpha Y^{\mathsf T}A_\alpha^*$. Thus $C$ is positive and $C(1)=C^*(1)=\rho$. Restrict to the support of $\rho$, on which inverse powers are defined, and set $$U(Y)=\rho^{-1/2}C(Y)\rho^{-1/2}.$$ The map $U$ is unital and contractive in operator norm: its preceding representation is an isometric compression of copies of $Y^{\mathsf T}$. It also satisfies $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho U(Y))=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho Y)$. For $0\le\Re z\le1$ introduce $$U_z(Y)=\rho^{z/2}
 U(\rho^{-z/2}Y\rho^{-z/2})\rho^{z/2}.$$ On $\Re z=0$, multiplication on either side by imaginary powers of $\rho$ is isometric, so $U_z$ is operator-norm contractive. At $z=1$, $$U_1(Y)=C(\rho^{-1/2}Y\rho^{-1/2}),\qquad U_1^*(1)=1.$$ Its adjoint is a unital positive map with the same compression and transpose representation, hence is operator-norm contractive. Duality makes $U_1$ trace-norm contractive; imaginary powers give the same conclusion on $\Re z=1$.

The interpolation used here is the finite-dimensional weighted Schatten argument; compare (Beigi 2013, sec. III, Theorem 4) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Beigi2013](#ref-Beigi2013)]. We give the details for our positive correlation map. Given matrices of Hilbert–Schmidt norm one, continue the singular values of the input by powers $2z$, and those of the matrix paired with its output by powers $2(1-z)$, keeping the unitary factors fixed. The bilinear trace pairing is analytic in the strip, bounded by one on both boundary lines by the preceding endpoint bounds and trace duality. The three-lines inequality bounds it by one at $z=1/2$. Zero singular values follow by approximation. This proves $\|U_{1/2}(Y)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le\|Y\|_{\mathrm{HS}}$. Take $Y=\rho^{1/4}H\rho^{1/4}$. Then $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(YU_{1/2}(Y))=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(HC(H))=\sum_d c_d,
 \qquad \|Y\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(RHRH).$$ Cauchy–Schwarz proves the claim. Compression to the support leaves the correlation unchanged: every tensor slot lies in that support. Complexification, used only for interpolation, preserves the conclusion for real tensors. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-matrix-saturation"></a>
<div id="lem:matrix-saturation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:matrix-sylvester"></a>
<a id="eq:matrix-saturation"></a>
<a id="eq:matrix-action-errors"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Saturation of the square-root energy). *There are measurable symmetric matrix fields $H_i$, $1\le i\le n$, solving $$\label{eq:matrix-sylvester}
 D_iR=\frac{H_iR+RH_i}{2},$$ with zero kernel–kernel block, such that $$\label{eq:matrix-saturation}
 \|DR\|_2^2\longrightarrow1,\qquad
 \sum_i\mathbb E\|[H_i,R]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=o(1),\qquad
 \sum_i\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(R^2H_i^2)\longrightarrow1.$$ Moreover, $$\label{eq:matrix-action-errors}
 \sum_i\left(\|D_iR-H_iR\|_2^2+
 \|D_i\Psi-(H_i)_{(1)}\Psi\|_2^2\right)=o(1).$$ The first slot can be replaced by any existing slot in each component.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-matrix-saturation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-matrix-saturation</summary>

<a id="eq:matrix-energy-identities"></a>
*Proof.* In an eigenbasis of $R$ with eigenvalues $r_a$, define $(H_i)_{ab}=2(D_iR)_{ab}/(r_a+r_b)$ when $r_a+r_b>0$, and zero otherwise. The kernel–kernel block of $D_iR$ vanishes almost everywhere. One way to check this for a form-domain positive matrix field is to use absolutely continuous line representatives: at a differentiability point, the quadratic form on every vector in the kernel has a local minimum zero, and polarization gives the assertion. The formula is measurable and gives $$\sum_i\|H_iR\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le4|DR|^2.$$ Set $$e=\|DR\|_2^2,\quad a=\sum_i\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(R^2H_i^2),\quad
 c=\sum_i\mathbb E\|[H_i,R]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2,\quad d=\|D\Psi\|_2^2.$$ The Sylvester equation implies the exact identities $$\label{eq:matrix-energy-identities}
 e=a-\frac c4,\qquad
 \sum_i\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(RH_iRH_i)=e-\frac c4.$$ Differentiating $R^2=\Psi\Psi^*$ in the weak form sense gives $$e=\sum_i\mathbb E\langle D_i\Psi,(H_i)_{(1)}\Psi\rangle
   =\sum_i\mathbb E\langle D_i\Psi,(H_i)_{\mathrm{av}}\Psi\rangle.$$ For example, the first equality follows by pairing $D_i(R^2)$ with $H_i/2$; all products are integrable by the displayed bound. The second uses the symmetry of each $D_i\Psi$ on its existing slots. Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-copied-slots" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-copied-slots">28</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz yield $$e^2\le d\left(e-\frac c4+\frac{2a}{k}\right).$$ Since $a\le4e$, $d\to1$, and $\liminf e\ge1$, this first forces $e\to1$ and then $c\to0$; Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-energy-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-energy-identities">[eq:matrix-energy-identities]</a> gives $a\to1$. Finally, $$\sum_i\|D_iR-H_iR\|_2^2=\frac c4,
 \qquad
 \sum_i\|D_i\Psi-(H_i)_{(1)}\Psi\|_2^2=d+a-2e.$$ Both tend to zero. Symmetry gives the assertion for the other slots. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Bounded columns and spectral blocks

We next make the column $(H_i)_i$ bounded in operator norm. This requires a weighted tail estimate; an unweighted truncation would be inadequate when $R$ has many small eigenvalues. Throughout the modifications below, $H_i$ denotes the current column; the exact Sylvester identity <a href="#eq:matrix-sylvester" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-sylvester">[eq:matrix-sylvester]</a> refers to the original column. The approximate derivative-action and balance estimates are preserved as stated in the following lemmas.

<a id="stmt-lem-matrix-bounded-column"></a>
<div id="lem:matrix-bounded-column" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:matrix-balance"></a>
<a id="eq:matrix-column-bound"></a>
**Lemma 30** (Balance and bounded columns). *The matrices in Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-saturation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-saturation">29</a> satisfy $$\label{eq:matrix-balance}
 \mathbb E\left\|\sum_iH_i\rho H_i-\rho\right\|_1=o(1),\qquad
 \mathbb E\left\|R\left(\sum_iH_i^2\right)R-\rho\right\|_1=o(1).$$ They can be replaced by symmetric matrix fields, still denoted $H_i$, so that Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-action-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-action-errors">[eq:matrix-action-errors]</a>, the commutator conclusion of Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-saturation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-saturation">[eq:matrix-saturation]</a>, and Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-balance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-balance">[eq:matrix-balance]</a> hold, and $$\label{eq:matrix-column-bound}
 S:=\sum_iH_i^2\le4\mathrm{Id},\qquad
 \mathbb E\|(S-1)R\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=o(1).$$ The total change satisfies $\sum_i\mathbb E\|(H_i^{\mathrm{new}}-H_i^{\mathrm{old}})R\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=o(1)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-matrix-bounded-column"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-matrix-bounded-column</summary>

<a id="eq:matrix-reduction-perturbation"></a>
*Proof.* We repeatedly use the dimension-free inequality $$\label{eq:matrix-reduction-perturbation}
 \|XX^*-YY^*\|_1
 \le(\|X\|_{\mathrm{HS}}+\|Y\|_{\mathrm{HS}})\|X-Y\|_{\mathrm{HS}}.$$ By Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-action-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-action-errors">[eq:matrix-action-errors]</a>, the first sum in Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-balance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-balance">[eq:matrix-balance]</a> is close in expected trace norm to the one-slot reduction of $D\Psi$, with the derivative index included among the column indices. The derivative coupling identifies this with $$\frac1{kI}\sum_{k\le l<2k}T_{l+1}T_{l+1}^*.$$ The trace-norm error from the coupling is $o(1)$ by Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-reduction-perturbation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-reduction-perturbation">[eq:matrix-reduction-perturbation]</a> and Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-normalization">[eq:matrix-normalization]</a>. Shifting the band changes its reduction only by its two endpoints, whose expected trace-norm cost is $$\frac{\|T_{2k}\|_2^2+\|T_k\|_2^2}{kI}=O(k^{-1}).$$ This proves the first balance equation. Comparing the arrays of matrices $(H_iR)_i$ and $(RH_i)_i$ proves the second by the same inequality and the summed commutator estimate.

For the original, possibly unbounded column write $\mathbf H v=(H_iv)_i$, $S=\mathbf H^*\mathbf H$, and $J=(1+S)^{-1}$. Let $\mathbf K=\mathbf HR-R^{\oplus n}\mathbf H$, so that $\mathbb E\|\mathbf K\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=o(1)$. Algebra gives $$[S,R]=\mathbf H^*\mathbf K-\mathbf K^*\mathbf H,
 \qquad [R,J]=J[S,R]J.$$ The bounds $$\|\mathbf HJ\mathbf H^*\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le1,\qquad
 \|\mathbf HJ\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\tfrac12,\qquad \|J\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le1$$ therefore imply $\|\mathbf H[R,J]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le2\|\mathbf K\|_{\mathrm{HS}}$. For every fixed $f(x)=a+b/(1+x)$, it follows that $$\mathbb E\|\mathbf H[f(S),R]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=o(1).$$ The squared norms of $\mathbf Hf(S)R$ and $\mathbf HRf(S)$ differ by $o(1)$: the latter has bounded mean-square norm, and the preceding bound controls their difference. Testing the second balance equation against the bounded matrix $f(S)^2$ consequently gives $$\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(R^2(S-1)f(S)^2\bigr)=o(1).$$ Use $f=1,1+J,1-J$ and $(S-1)^2/(1+S)=(S-1)-2(S-1)J$ to obtain <a id="eq:matrix-column-tail"></a>$$\label{eq:matrix-column-tail}
 \mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\left(R^2\frac{(S-1)^2}{1+S}\right)=o(1).$$ All these expressions are integrable: their absolute values are bounded by a constant times $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(R^2(1+S))$.

Let $Q=\mathbf 1_{(4,\infty)}(S)$ and $P=1-Q$. On this spectral region, $(S-1)^2/(1+S)$ dominates both a positive constant and a positive multiple of $S$. Thus $$\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(R^2Q)+\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(R^2SQ)=o(1).$$ The input truncation costs $\sum_i\mathbb E\|H_iQR\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(R^2SQ)=o(1)$. The output truncation costs $$\sum_i\mathbb E\|QH_iR\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\left(Q\sum_iH_i\rho H_i\right)
 \le\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Q\rho)+\mathbb E\left\|\sum_iH_i\rho H_i-\rho\right\|_1=o(1).$$ Consequently replacing $H_i$ by $PH_iP$ has total right-$R$ squared cost $o(1)$. The new column obeys $\sum_i(PH_iP)^2\le PSP\le4P$.

<a id="eq:matrix-weighted-change"></a>
Here is the perturbation accounting needed both now and below. If symmetric $\widetilde H_i$ have $$\label{eq:matrix-weighted-change}
 \Delta=\sum_i\mathbb E\|(\widetilde H_i-H_i)R\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=o(1),$$ then symmetry gives the same bound with $R$ on the left. Each derivative error in Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-action-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-action-errors">[eq:matrix-action-errors]</a> changes in norm by at most $\sqrt\Delta$, and the summed commutator norm changes by at most $2\sqrt\Delta$. The two balance errors change in expected trace norm by at most $$\left(\Bigl[\sum_i\mathbb E\|H_iR\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\Bigr]^{1/2}
 +\Bigl[\sum_i\mathbb E\|\widetilde H_iR\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\Bigr]^{1/2}\right)\sqrt\Delta,$$ by Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-reduction-perturbation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-reduction-perturbation">[eq:matrix-reduction-perturbation]</a>, and this is $o(1)$. The new summed energy is bounded by the old one plus the perturbation in norm. These statements apply to the compression just made.

For the bounded column, $[S,R]=\mathbf H^*\mathbf K-\mathbf K^*\mathbf H$ satisfies $\|[S,R]\|_2\le4\|\mathbf K\|_2=o(1)$. Put $T=S-1$, so $\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le3$. The second balance equation says $\mathbb E\|RTR\|_1=o(1)$. Finally, $$\|TR\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(TRTR)+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(T[T,R]R).$$ The expectation of the first term is at most $3\mathbb E\|RTR\|_1$ in absolute value; the second is at most $3\|[T,R]\|_2\|R\|_2=o(1)$. This proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-matrix-index-exchange"></a>
<div id="lem:matrix-index-exchange" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:matrix-index-exchange"></a>
**Lemma 31** (Commutation and exchange of indices). *For the bounded columns just constructed, $$\label{eq:matrix-index-exchange}
 \sum_{i,j}\mathbb E\|[H_i,H_j]R\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=o(1),\qquad
 \mathbb E\sum_{i,a,b}|(H_iR)_{ab}-(H_aR)_{ib}|^2=o(1).$$ These conclusions also hold after a symmetric perturbation satisfying Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-weighted-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-weighted-change">[eq:matrix-weighted-change]</a>, provided the new column has a uniform operator bound.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-matrix-index-exchange"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-matrix-index-exchange</summary>

*Proof.* Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-action-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-action-errors">[eq:matrix-action-errors]</a> implies that moving $H_i$ between any two existing slots costs $o(1)$ in summed mean square. Three slots suffice to exchange two factors. In the following chain, each $\simeq$ means that the difference has squared norm $o(1)$ after expectation and summation over $i,j$: $$\begin{aligned}
 (H_i)_{(1)}(H_j)_{(1)}\Psi
 &\simeq (H_i)_{(1)}(H_j)_{(2)}\Psi
  = (H_j)_{(2)}(H_i)_{(1)}\Psi\\
 &\simeq (H_j)_{(2)}(H_i)_{(3)}\Psi
  = (H_i)_{(3)}(H_j)_{(2)}\Psi\\
 &\simeq (H_i)_{(3)}(H_j)_{(1)}\Psi
  = (H_j)_{(1)}(H_i)_{(3)}\Psi\\
 &\simeq (H_j)_{(1)}(H_i)_{(1)}\Psi.
\end{aligned}$$ The equalities use commutation on different slots. Each move costs at most the one-factor error multiplied by the column bound: for any tensor $v$, $$\sum_i\|(H_i)_{(r)}v\|^2\le4\|v\|^2.$$ The squared norm of the sum of the four errors is at most four times their summed squared norms. Thus the total commutator cost on $\Psi$ is $o(1)$. Its cost on $\Psi$ equals its cost on $R$, since both have reduction $R^2$.

For the second conclusion, write $\Psi=RJ_0$, where $J_0J_0^*$ is the support projection of $R$. The matrix-valued array $Z_{ia,b}=(H_iR)_{ab}-(H_aR)_{ib}$ has its column index in that support, so $\|ZJ_0\|_{\mathrm{HS}}=\|Z\|_{\mathrm{HS}}$. The entries of $ZJ_0$ express the exchange of the derivative index and the first tensor index in $(H_i)_{(1)}\Psi$. Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-action-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-action-errors">[eq:matrix-action-errors]</a> and the approximation of $D\Psi$ by fully symmetric $T_{l+1}$ give the claim. For perturbed matrices, the derivative errors and the slot-moving errors have the bounds established after Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-weighted-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-weighted-change">[eq:matrix-weighted-change]</a>; the same four moves use the new uniform column bound. The index-exchange norm itself changes by at most $2\sqrt\Delta$. ◻

</details>

We have obtained a bounded column acting almost identically on the symmetric tensor slots. To use Gaussian matrix polynomials, we now put its weighted estimates into a tracial form. The following construction is performed only at the initial point of the diffusion, so a varying eigenbasis will never be differentiated.

<a id="stmt-lem-matrix-blocks"></a>
<div id="lem:matrix-blocks" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:matrix-tracial-defects"></a>
<a id="eq:flattening-bound"></a>
**Lemma 32** (Spectral blocks and a bounded input map). *After enlarging the initial probability space by an independent uniform variable, there are a positive block matrix $R_*=\bigoplus_\beta\beta\mathrm{Id}_\beta$, with zero on $\ker R$, and symmetric matrices $H_i$ supported on entries whose row, column, and outer index belong to the same positive block, with the following properties. Their total change satisfies Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-weighted-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-weighted-change">[eq:matrix-weighted-change]</a>, $R_*\le R\le(1+\epsilon)R_*$ on the positive support for $\epsilon\to0$, and the column bound $S\le4\mathrm{Id}$ holds. With the positive weighted trace <a id="eq:matrix-weighted-trace"></a>$$\label{eq:matrix-weighted-trace}
 \tau(U)=\mathbb E\sum_\beta\beta^2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_\beta U_\beta,$$ where the expectation also includes any fresh noises present, we have $\tau(1)\to1$ and $$\label{eq:matrix-tracial-defects}
 \tau((S-1)^2)=o(1),\quad
 \sum_{i,j}\tau(|[H_i,H_j]|^2)=o(1),\quad
 \mathbb E\sum_\beta\beta^2\sum_{i,a,b\in\beta}
 |(H_i)_{ab}-(H_a)_{ib}|^2=o(1).$$ For a vector $v$ in a positive block define $H[v]=\sum_{i\in\beta}v_iH_i$. We can further impose, pointwise, $$\label{eq:flattening-bound}
 \|H[v]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le2|v|.$$ The bound uses ordinary, unnormalized Hilbert–Schmidt norm. All the derivative-action and balance conclusions above continue to hold for these matrices in the initial coordinates.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-matrix-blocks"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-matrix-blocks</summary>

*Proof.* Choose an orthogonal eigenframe $O$ for $R$ at the initial point. Transform all three indices: in that frame the matrices are $$\widehat H_i=\sum_jO_{ji}O^*H_jO.$$ This preserves the column bound, all the summed norms, and the index-exchange estimate, since these are Euclidean tensor contractions. Finite-dimensional spectral calculus and an ordered orthonormal basis completion give measurable choices, including at repeated eigenvalues.

<a id="eq:matrix-block-error"></a>
Let $h=\log(1+\epsilon)$ and take an independent $\theta$ uniform on $[0,h)$. Group positive eigenvalues in $[e^{\theta+mh},e^{\theta+(m+1)h})$, $m\in\mathbb Z$, and round those in each nonempty block down to $\beta=e^{\theta+mh}$. The zero eigenspace is kept separate. Write $$\kappa=\sum_i\mathbb E\|[H_i,R]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=o(1),\qquad
 a=\sum_i\mathbb E\|H_iR\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=O(1).$$ For $0<\delta<h$, a pair of positive eigenvalues with $|\log(r_a/r_b)|\ge\delta$ satisfies $r_b^2\le C\delta^{-2}(r_a-r_b)^2$, when $\delta\le1$. If their logarithmic distance is smaller than $\delta$, the probability that a block boundary separates them is at most $\delta/h$. Consequently the right-$R$ squared cost of deleting entries with row and column in different blocks is bounded by $$\label{eq:matrix-block-error}
 C\delta^{-2}\kappa+\frac{\delta}{h}a+\kappa.$$ The final term covers a zero row and positive column directly by the commutator. A zero column has no right-$R$ cost. For example, choose $\delta\to0$ slowly enough that $\kappa/\delta^2\to0$, and then $h\to0$ slowly enough that $\delta/h\to0$.

Deleting entries whose outer index belongs to another block has vanishing cost as well. On the set where $i$ and $b$ have different block labels, $$|(H_i)_{ab}r_b|^2
 \le2|(H_i)_{ab}r_b-(H_a)_{ib}r_b|^2
   +2|(H_a)_{ib}r_b|^2.$$ Sum over the indices. The first term is the second defect of Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-index-exchange" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-index-exchange">[eq:matrix-index-exchange]</a>; the second is a row–column block exclusion of the kind already bounded in Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-block-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-block-error">[eq:matrix-block-error]</a>. This includes outer indices in the zero eigenspace. Within each block the resulting matrices have the form $P_\beta\widehat H_iP_\beta$ for $i\in\beta$, and vanish otherwise. Thus $$\sum_{i\in\beta}(P_\beta\widehat H_iP_\beta)^2
 \le P_\beta\left(\sum_i\widehat H_i^2\right)P_\beta\le4P_\beta.$$ They are symmetric and their total change has the cost in Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-weighted-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-weighted-change">[eq:matrix-weighted-change]</a>.

<a id="eq:matrix-weight-comparison"></a>
For every block matrix $X$ one has the precise comparison $$\label{eq:matrix-weight-comparison}
 \tau(X^*X)\le\mathbb E\|XR\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le(1+\epsilon)^2\tau(X^*X).$$ In particular $\tau(1)\to1$. The perturbation bounds following Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-weighted-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-weighted-change">[eq:matrix-weighted-change]</a> prove the derivative, commutator, and balance conclusions for these matrices. The bounded-column argument at the end of Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-bounded-column" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-bounded-column">30</a> again gives $\mathbb E\|(S-1)R\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=o(1)$, so Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-weight-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-weight-comparison">[eq:matrix-weight-comparison]</a> proves the first tracial defect. Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-index-exchange" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-index-exchange">31</a> gives the second. For the third, all three indices now lie in one block, and the index-exchange defect is exactly weighted by $r_b^2$; replacing this by $\beta^2$ only decreases it. Matrix symmetry and exchange of $i,a$ together give asymptotic symmetry under every permutation of the three indices.

It remains to bound the map from the outer index to matrix coefficients. On each block let $$Fv=\sum_i v_iH_i,\qquad (Gv)_{ab}=(H_bv)_a.$$ The map $G$ has operator norm at most two because $\|Gv\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\langle v,Sv\rangle\le4|v|^2$. Total symmetry just established says $$\mathbb E\sum_\beta\beta^2\|F-G\|_{\mathrm{HS}(\mathbb R^{\dim\beta},\mathrm{HS})}^2=o(1).$$ Clip the singular values of $F$ to two, obtaining $F'=FB$ for a real positive contraction $B$ on the input space. Metric projection onto the operator-norm ball in Hilbert–Schmidt norm gives $\|F-F'\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le\|F-G\|_{\mathrm{HS}}$; this also follows immediately by singular-value decomposition and the variational characterization of singular values. Thus the block-weighted squared cost is $o(1)$. Writing $H'_i=\sum_j B_{ji}H_j$ shows that every $H'_i$ remains symmetric. For each vector $v$, $$\sum_i\|H'_iv\|^2\le\sum_j\|H_jv\|^2,$$ so the column bound is preserved, as is the block support. The weighted cost controls the right-$R$ cost by Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-weight-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-weight-comparison">[eq:matrix-weight-comparison]</a>. Therefore the derivative and balance errors have exactly the bounds stated after Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-weighted-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-weighted-change">[eq:matrix-weighted-change]</a>; index exchange changes by at most twice the perturbation norm, and the four-move argument controls the commutators. Repeating the bounded-column last step and then Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-weight-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-weight-comparison">[eq:matrix-weight-comparison]</a> proves all three tracial defects for $H'_i$. The defining singular-value clipping proves Equation <a href="#eq:flattening-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flattening-bound">[eq:flattening-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Gaussian moments and the frozen diffusion law

The matrices within a block need not commute exactly. The next lemma shows that their Gaussian linear combinations nevertheless have the scalar Gaussian joint moments in the weighted limit. We include the moment bounds because they also justify the unbounded exponential needed for the diffusion law.

<a id="stmt-lem-matrix-gaussian-moments"></a>
<div id="lem:matrix-gaussian-moments" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:matrix-gaussian-joint-law"></a>
<a id="eq:matrix-gaussian-moment-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:matrix-wick-reduction"></a>
**Lemma 33** (Weighted Gaussian moments). *Let the block matrices and $\tau$ be those of Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-blocks">32</a>. For independent standard Gaussian vectors $N_1,\ldots,N_q$, independent of the initial data, put $D_r=H[N_r]$. Then, for every fixed word $r_1,\ldots,r_m$, $$\label{eq:matrix-gaussian-joint-law}
 \tau(D_{r_1}\cdots D_{r_m})
 \longrightarrow \mathbb E(Z_{r_1}\cdots Z_{r_m}),$$ where $Z_1,\ldots,Z_q$ are independent scalar standard normals. Products of any fixed length can be reordered with error tending to zero in $L^2(\tau)$. For every integer $p\ge1$ and real $u\ge0$, $$\label{eq:matrix-gaussian-moment-bound}
 \mathbb E_{N_r}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_\beta D_r^{2p}
 \le4^p(2p-1)!!\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_\beta1,
 \qquad
 \tau(e^{u|D_r|})\le2e^{2u^2}\tau(1).$$ If $\mathfrak h_j$ is the tensor Hermite polynomial of Lemma <a href="#lem:hermite-expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hermite-expansion">24</a> and $\mathfrak h_j^{(1)}$ is its one-dimensional version, then for each fixed $j$, $$\label{eq:matrix-wick-reduction}
 \tau\left(\left|
 \sum_{i_1,\ldots,i_j}H_{i_j}\cdots H_{i_1}
      \mathfrak h_j(N_1)_{i_1\ldots i_j}
 -\mathfrak h_j^{(1)}(D_1)\right|^2\right)=o(1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-matrix-gaussian-moments"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-matrix-gaussian-moments</summary>

*Proof.* We first prove a conditional moment bound on one fixed block, allowing a general column bound $S\le C\mathrm{Id}$. We use the Gaussian trace-moment integration-by-parts argument of (Tropp 2018, Lemma 6.1 and Section 7) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Tropp2018](#ref-Tropp2018)]; the weighted near-commutation limit will be proved afterward. Write $D=\sum_iN_iH_i$. Gaussian integration by parts gives $$\mathbb E_N\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}D^{2p}
 =\sum_{j=0}^{2p-2}\mathbb E_N\sum_i
   \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(H_iD^jH_iD^{2p-2-j}).$$ In an eigenbasis of $D$ with eigenvalues $d_a$, the absolute value of a summand is bounded by $$\sum_{i,a,b}|(H_i)_{ab}|^2|d_b|^j|d_a|^{2p-2-j}.$$ For $p>1$, the scalar weighted arithmetic–geometric mean inequality bounds the product of powers by $$\frac{j}{2p-2}|d_b|^{2p-2}
 +\left(1-\frac{j}{2p-2}\right)|d_a|^{2p-2}.$$ Since $\sum_{i,b}|(H_i)_{ab}|^2=S_{aa}\le C$ and similarly with $a,b$ exchanged, each summand is at most $C\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}|D|^{2p-2}$. For $p=1$ the assertion is $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}S\le C\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}1$. Induction proves $\mathbb E_N\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}D^{2p}\le C^p(2p-1)!!\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}1$. After multiplying by the block weights, all fixed moments are uniformly bounded. Also $e^{u|x|}\le2\cosh(ux)$, so the even-moment series gives $$\tau(e^{u|D|})
 \le2\sum_{p\ge0}\frac{u^{2p}C^p(2p-1)!!}{(2p)!}\tau(1)
 =2e^{Cu^2/2}\tau(1).$$ This proves Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-gaussian-moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-gaussian-moment-bound">[eq:matrix-gaussian-moment-bound]</a> with $C=4$.

For clarity, the norm $\|X\|_{p,\tau}=\tau(|X|^p)^{1/p}$ is formed on the direct sum of the blocks and then averaged over the classical random variables. Schatten Hölder followed by scalar Hölder gives $$\|X_1\cdots X_m\|_{p,\tau}
 \le\prod_{j=1}^m\|X_j\|_{p_j,\tau},
 \qquad \sum_j\frac1{p_j}=\frac1p.$$ These bounds use the actual weights $\beta^2$, with no factor depending on a block dimension. The finite-matrix Schatten inequality can itself be obtained by the same singular-value three-lines argument as in Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-copied-slots" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-copied-slots">28</a>, interpolating the endpoint bounds with one trace norm and the other norms operator norms, and then applying duality.

For independent $N_r,N_s$, $$\|[D_r,D_s]\|_{2,\tau}^2
 =\sum_{i,j}\tau(|[H_i,H_j]|^2)=o(1).$$ All its higher fixed moments are bounded by Hölder and the moment bound just proved. Interpolation between $L^2(\tau)$ and an arbitrarily high fixed $L^p(\tau)$ therefore makes this commutator tend to zero in every fixed finite power norm. Applying Hölder to the factors on either side of a commutator shows that an adjacent transposition in a word of fixed length costs $o(1)$ in $L^2(\tau)$. A finite sequence of transpositions proves the reordering assertion.

Now expand a mixed moment by Gaussian pairings, conditional on the matrices. Pairings must join indices of the same Gaussian vector. For each pair introduce its own independent standard Gaussian vector, shared only between the two factors in that pair. This represents the contracted matrix word exactly as a conditional expectation. Reorder the word until each pair is adjacent; the error is $o(1)$ even in $L^2(\tau)$. Averaging the independent vectors then gives $S^{m/2}$. For each fixed integer $b$, boundedness of $S$ gives $$\|S^b-1\|_{2,\tau}
 \le\left(\sum_{a=0}^{b-1}4^a\right)\|S-1\|_{2,\tau}=o(1).$$ Each pairing consequently contributes $\tau(1)+o(1)=1+o(1)$. Odd moments vanish. The number of admissible pairings is precisely the scalar independent-normal moment, proving Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-gaussian-joint-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-gaussian-joint-law">[eq:matrix-gaussian-joint-law]</a>.

Finally expand the tensor Hermite polynomial using its defining exponential $\exp(\langle v,N\rangle-|v|^2/2)$. A term consists of free $N$ indices and a number of signed Kronecker-delta pairs. Represent each delta pair by its own independent Gaussian vector, as above, and move its two matrix factors together. Conditional averaging is contractive in $L^2(\tau)$, so the reordering error remains $o(1)$. The resulting factors $S$ can be replaced by one, even between fixed powers of $D_1$, by Hölder and interpolation applied to the bounded matrix $S-1$. The resulting polynomial is $$\sum_{b=0}^{\lfloor j/2\rfloor}
 \frac{(-1)^b j!}{2^bb!(j-2b)!}D_1^{j-2b}
 =\mathfrak h_j^{(1)}(D_1).$$ There are only finitely many terms for fixed $j$, proving Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-wick-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-wick-reduction">[eq:matrix-wick-reduction]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-frozen-law"></a>
<div id="prop:frozen-law" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:matrix-frozen-law"></a>
**Proposition 34** (Frozen diffusion law). *Let $Y_u$ be the stationary diffusion with invariant law $\eta$ and driving Brownian motion $B_u$, and fix $t>0$. Make the matrix construction of Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-blocks">32</a> at $Y_0$, using an independent random block offset. In its orthonormal frame put $N=B_t/\sqrt t$; conditionally on the initial data and block offset, $N$ is a standard Gaussian vector. Define, in the initial spatial coordinates, <a id="eq:matrix-frozen-covariance"></a>$$\label{eq:matrix-frozen-covariance}
 \sigma=\bigoplus_\beta\beta^2
       \exp\bigl(\sqrt{8t}\,H[N]-4t\mathrm{Id}_\beta\bigr),$$ and set $\sigma=0$ on the omitted kernel. Then $$\label{eq:matrix-frozen-law}
 \mathbb E\|R(Y_t)-\sigma^{1/2}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\longrightarrow0.$$ The matrices defining $\sigma$ obey the bounded-column and input-map bounds <a href="#eq:matrix-column-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-column-bound">[eq:matrix-column-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:flattening-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flattening-bound">[eq:flattening-bound]</a>, the weighted defects <a href="#eq:matrix-tracial-defects" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-tracial-defects">[eq:matrix-tracial-defects]</a>, and the mixed moment law of Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-gaussian-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-gaussian-moments">33</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-frozen-law"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-frozen-law</summary>

*Proof.* The proof has three steps: replace the added tensor indices in the hierarchy expansion by products of the initial matrices, identify their Wick contractions, and sum the resulting scalar Hermite series. All limits involving the degree of that series are taken after the counterexample-sequence limit, with $t$ fixed.

<a id="eq:matrix-one-step"></a>
The derivative-action estimate and the hierarchy coupling give $$\label{eq:matrix-one-step}
 \frac1{kI}\sum_{k\le l<2k}\sum_i
 \|T_{l+1,i\,\cdot}-(H_i)_{(1)}T_l\|_2^2=o(1).$$ It also holds when the band is shifted by any fixed integer $a\ge0$. Indeed the same matrices have been constructed using levels $k$ through $2k-1$. Removing or adding at most $a$ levels in the error sum costs $O_C(a/k)$, since the unnormalized squared norm of each one-step error is at most $$2\|T_{l+1}\|_2^2+2C\|T_l\|_2^2=O_C(I),$$ by the bounded column and the diagonal endpoint control. This also explains why the matrices may already include the block and input clipping modifications: their cost on $\Psi$ is the right-$R$ cost in Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-weighted-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-weighted-change">[eq:matrix-weighted-change]</a>.

Iterating Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-one-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-one-step">[eq:matrix-one-step]</a>, for each fixed $j$ we may replace the extra-index tensor $T_{l+j}$ by $$(i_1,\ldots,i_j)\longmapsto
 (H_{i_j})_{(1)}\cdots(H_{i_1})_{(1)}T_l$$ in the normalized band mean-square norm, with error $o(1)$. More explicitly, if $e_a$ is the root mean-square error in the one-step estimate on the band shifted by $a$, the root error after $j$ steps is at most $$\sum_{a=0}^{j-1}C^{a/2}e_{j-1-a}.$$ Here multiplication by a column of $H_i$ costs at most $\sqrt C$ in the summed coefficient norm. Each $e_a=o(1)$ at fixed $a$, so the displayed bound vanishes. Contraction with $\mathfrak h_j(N)$ multiplies squared coefficient norm by at most $j!$: it first symmetrizes the contracted indices, an orthogonal projection, and Gaussian Hermite orthogonality then gives the factor $j!$.

<a id="eq:matrix-hermite-error"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:hermite-expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hermite-expansion">24</a> to the band of levels in $\Psi$. For each fixed $m$, the preceding replacements and Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-wick-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-wick-reduction">[eq:matrix-wick-reduction]</a> give $$\Psi(Y_t)=P_{m,t}(D)\Psi(Y_0)+\mathcal E_{m,t},\qquad D=H[N],$$ where $$P_{m,t}(x)=e^{-t}\sum_{j=0}^m
          \frac{(2t)^{j/2}}{j!}\mathfrak h_j^{(1)}(x)$$ and $$\label{eq:matrix-hermite-error}
 \limsup\|\mathcal E_{m,t}\|_2
 \le \frac{(2t)^{(m+1)/2}}{\sqrt{(m+1)!}}.$$ To justify the conversion of a tracial polynomial error to the norm of its action on $\Psi(Y_0)$, use $\|X\Psi(Y_0)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}=\|XR\|_{\mathrm{HS}}$ and Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-weight-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-weight-comparison">[eq:matrix-weight-comparison]</a>. The band version of the remainder bound follows from the uniform finite-list diagonal fixed at the start of the section. Its right side tends to zero as $m\to\infty$.
