The spectral measures of $D$ averaged with $\tau$ have total mass tending to one, converge in moments to the standard Gaussian by Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-gaussian-joint-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-gaussian-joint-law">[eq:matrix-gaussian-joint-law]</a>, and have uniformly bounded exponential moments at every fixed linear parameter by Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-gaussian-moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-gaussian-moment-bound">[eq:matrix-gaussian-moment-bound]</a>. These facts imply weak convergence to the standard Gaussian and convergence of the integrals of every continuous function bounded by a polynomial times a fixed linear exponential. Here is a direct justification: the second moment gives tightness; the higher moment bounds give uniform integrability of each polynomial, so every subsequential limit has the Gaussian moments. Its exponential moments determine its characteristic function by its power series, hence determine the law. Using a larger exponential parameter makes the tails of any of the specified test functions uniformly small.

The scalar Hermite generating identity gives, with $Z$ standard normal, $$e^{-t}\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{(2t)^{j/2}}{j!}\mathfrak h_j^{(1)}(Z)
 =\exp(\sqrt{2t}\,Z-2t)$$ in squared mean. In fact its squared tail norm is $e^{-2t}\sum_{j>m}(2t)^j/j!$. Define the matrix multiplier $$M=\exp(\sqrt{2t}\,D-2t\mathrm{Id}).$$ The just-proved convergence of spectral integrals, applied with each fixed $m$, yields <a id="eq:matrix-exponential-limit"></a>$$\label{eq:matrix-exponential-limit}
 \lim\tau(|M-P_{m,t}(D)|^2)
 =e^{-2t}\sum_{j>m}\frac{(2t)^j}{j!},\qquad
 \tau(M^2)\longrightarrow1.$$ Together with Equations <a href="#eq:matrix-hermite-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-hermite-error">[eq:matrix-hermite-error]</a> and <a href="#eq:matrix-weight-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-weight-comparison">[eq:matrix-weight-comparison]</a>, first sending the sequence index and then $m$ to infinity, this proves $$\mathbb E\|\Psi(Y_t)-M\Psi(Y_0)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=o(1).$$ There is no operator-norm bound on $M$ in this argument; only its weighted trace moments are used.

Write $\Psi(Y_0)=RJ_0$, with $J_0J_0^*$ equal to the support projection. On each block $(R-R_*)^2\le\epsilon^2R_*^2$, and $R_*$ is scalar there. By cyclicity and positivity of trace, $$\mathbb E\|M(R-R_*)J_0\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le\epsilon^2\tau(M^2)=o(1).$$ Thus $\Psi(Y_t)$ is close in squared mean to $MR_*J_0$. Its reduction is exactly $$(MR_*J_0)(MR_*J_0)^*
 =MR_*^2M=\bigoplus_\beta\beta^2
       \exp(\sqrt{8t}\,H[N]-4t\mathrm{Id}_\beta)=\sigma.$$ Finally Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-root-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-root-lipschitz">[eq:matrix-root-lipschitz]</a> transfers the approximation of these rectangular matrices to the approximation of their positive reduced square roots, proving Equation <a href="#eq:matrix-frozen-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-frozen-law">[eq:matrix-frozen-law]</a>. ◻

</details>

The resulting description retains the weights $\beta^2$ and the ordinary trace on every block. It supplies both a bounded matrix column and the input bound of Equation <a href="#eq:flattening-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flattening-bound">[eq:flattening-bound]</a>, while allowing any number of positive blocks and eigenvalues arbitrarily close to zero. These are the properties needed for the covariance observations in Section <a href="#sec:observations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:observations">7</a>.

<a id="sec:observations"></a>
### Covariance observations and the contradiction

The matrices constructed in Section <a href="#sec:matrices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:matrices">6</a> describe fluctuations whose information content is incompatible with the logarithmic Sobolev inequality of the stationary law. We compare two bounds for the same Gaussian covariance observation. The first follows from logarithmic Sobolev and is universal after integration over its scale. The second uses the frozen matrices to recover a scalar lognormal covariance experiment. Integration over scale is essential: it assigns to every spectral block exactly the weight used by the tracial limit, regardless of the sizes of its eigenvalues.

<a id="eq:obs-energy-input"></a>
<a id="eq:obs-sylvester-input"></a>
Throughout this section, limits refer to the counterexample sequence. All observation times, caps, compact parameter intervals, and polynomial degrees are fixed before taking that limit. We suppress its index. Recall that $Y_t$ is stationary with law $\eta$, that $R(\eta)\le\lambda^{-1}$ with $\lambda\to1$, and that $R$ without a measure argument denotes the positive matrix field. In particular, Equations <a href="#eq:matrix-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-normalization">[eq:matrix-normalization]</a> and <a href="#eq:matrix-saturation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-saturation">[eq:matrix-saturation]</a> give $$\label{eq:obs-energy-input}
 \mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}R^2\longrightarrow1,
 \qquad \mathbb E|DR|^2\longrightarrow1.$$ To distinguish the original Sylvester matrices from the subsequently modified block matrices, denote the former by $\widehat H_i$. Equations <a href="#eq:matrix-sylvester" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-sylvester">[eq:matrix-sylvester]</a> and <a href="#eq:matrix-saturation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-saturation">[eq:matrix-saturation]</a> assert $$\label{eq:obs-sylvester-input}
 D_iR=\tfrac12(\widehat H_iR+R\widehat H_i),
 \qquad
 \sum_i\mathbb E\|[\widehat H_i,R]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=o(1).$$ Only the upper bound below uses these original matrices.

<a id="eq:obs-covariance-definition"></a>
Fix $L\ge1$ and define $$\label{eq:obs-covariance-definition}
 f_L(q)=\frac{q}{1+q/L},\qquad
 C_T(y)=\mathrm{Id}+f_L(TR(y)^2),\qquad
 Z_T=C_T(Y_t)^{1/2}G,\quad T>0,$$ where $G$ is an independent standard Gaussian vector. Thus $\mathrm{Id}\le C_T\le(1+L)\mathrm{Id}$. We write $H(P\mid Q)$ for relative entropy and $\mathsf I(U;V)$ for mutual information, including the usual averaged conditional version when a conditioning variable is displayed.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The integrated information upper bound

<a id="stmt-lem-obs-multipliers"></a>
<div id="lem:obs-multipliers" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:obs-multiplier"></a>
**Lemma 35** (Integrated spectral multipliers). *For $v,w\ge0$, not both zero, put $$\label{eq:obs-multiplier}
 M_L(v,w)=(v+w)^2\int_0^\infty
 \frac{dT}{
 (1+Tv^2/L)(1+Tw^2/L)
 (1+(1+1/L)Tv^2)(1+(1+1/L)Tw^2)}.$$ Set $M_L(0,0)=0$. Then <a id="eq:obs-multiplier-bounds"></a>$$\label{eq:obs-multiplier-bounds}
 M_L(v,w)\le4\log(1+L)
 \quad\hbox{for all }v,w\ge0,
 \qquad
 M_L(v,w)\le9
 \quad\hbox{if }\tfrac12\le v/w\le2.$$ The second bound concerns positive $v,w$ and is independent of $L$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-obs-multipliers"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-obs-multipliers</summary>

*Proof.* Write $a=1/L$ and $b=1+1/L$, so $b-a=1$. If $r=\max(v,w)>0$, discard the two denominator factors associated with the smaller eigenvalue and substitute $q=Tr^2$. This gives $$M_L(v,w)\le4\int_0^\infty
 \frac{dq}{(1+aq)(1+bq)}
 =4\log(b/a)=4\log(1+L).$$ For the identity in the middle, integration up to $Q$ gives $\log(1+bQ)-\log(1+aQ)$, which tends to $\log(b/a)$. If $v,w$ are positive and comparable as in the statement, let $r_0=\min(v,w)$. Discard the two factors with coefficient $a$, and use $b\ge1$. The remaining integral is at most $\int_0^\infty(1+Tr_0^2)^{-2}\,dT=r_0^{-2}$. Since $v+w\le3r_0$, this proves the second bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-information-upper"></a>
<div id="prop:information-upper" class="proposition">

**Proposition 36** (Universal integrated upper bound). *There is an absolute constant $C_0$ such that, for every fixed $t>0$ and $L\ge1$, the observations in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-covariance-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-covariance-definition">[eq:obs-covariance-definition]</a> satisfy <a id="eq:obs-information-upper"></a>$$\label{eq:obs-information-upper}
 \limsup\int_0^\infty\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\,\frac{dT}{T^2}\le C_0.$$ In particular, one may take $C_0=9/4$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-information-upper"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-information-upper</summary>

<a id="eq:obs-likelihood-lsi"></a>
*Proof.* Let $p_T(z\mid y)$ denote the density of the centered Gaussian with covariance $C_T(y)$, and let $p_T(z)$ be its marginal density. For each output $z$, the posterior density with respect to $\eta$ is $q_z(y)=p_T(z\mid y)/p_T(z)$. Applying $2\lambda H\le\mathcal I$ to this density and then averaging in $z$ gives $$\label{eq:obs-likelihood-lsi}
 2\lambda\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)
 \le\mathbb E\big|D_y\log p_T(Z_T\mid Y_t)\big|^2.$$ Here and below the expectation on the right includes the observation noise. The posterior normalization does not depend on $y$ and therefore does not contribute to the derivative.

<a id="eq:obs-gaussian-fisher"></a>
We compute that derivative explicitly. For any positive covariance $C$ and its derivative $D_iC$, differentiation of its determinant and inverse yields $$D_i\log p(z\mid y)
 =\tfrac12\left(
 z^*C^{-1}(D_iC)C^{-1}z-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(C^{-1}D_iC)\right).$$ Conditional on $y$, set $B_i=C^{-1/2}(D_iC)C^{-1/2}$ and $g=C^{-1/2}z$. The vector $g$ is standard Gaussian. Expanding its fourth moments gives $\mathbb E(g^*B_ig-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B_i)^2=2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B_i^2$; indeed the three Gaussian pairings give $(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B_i)^2+2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B_i^2$ before subtracting the mean. Consequently Equation <a href="#eq:obs-likelihood-lsi" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-likelihood-lsi">[eq:obs-likelihood-lsi]</a> becomes $$\label{eq:obs-gaussian-fisher}
 \mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\le\frac1{4\lambda}
 \mathbb E\sum_i\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big(C_T^{-1}D_iC_T\,C_T^{-1}D_iC_T\big).$$

These calculations also apply to the Sobolev matrix field $R$. To see this without assuming additional regularity, fix $T,L$ and the finite dimension of one member of the sequence. The map $R\mapsto C_T$ has bounded derivative on positive semidefinite matrices, and its eigenvalues lie in $[1,1+L]$. For each fixed $z$, the map $C\mapsto p(z\mid C)^{1/2}$ has bounded derivative on this covariance interval. The Sobolev chain rule therefore puts $q_z^{1/2}$ in the form domain; $p_T(z)>0$ and is a harmless fixed normalization. The Sobolev approximation at the opening of Section <a href="#sec:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:calculus">2</a> extends the logarithmic Sobolev inequality to this form-domain function. The squared derivatives integrate in $z$ to the nonnegative finite expression in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-gaussian-fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-gaussian-fisher">[eq:obs-gaussian-fisher]</a>. Approximation in the form domain and Tonelli thus justify both that equation and the subsequent scale integration. The finite-dimensional bounds used for form-domain membership do not enter its numerical coefficient.

In an eigenbasis of $R$, write $v,w$ for the row and column eigenvalues. The covariance function is $$c_T(v)=1+f_L(Tv^2)
       =\frac{1+bTv^2}{1+aTv^2},
 \qquad a=1/L,\quad b=1+1/L.$$ Its divided difference, including its derivative at $v=w$, is $$\frac{c_T(v)-c_T(w)}{v-w}
 =\frac{T(v+w)}{(1+aTv^2)(1+aTw^2)}.$$ Multiplying its square by $c_T(v)^{-1}c_T(w)^{-1}$ and integrating against $dT/T^2$ gives exactly the multiplier in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-multiplier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-multiplier">[eq:obs-multiplier]</a> for the squared entry $|(D_iR)_{vw}|^2$. When $v=w=0$, the covariance derivative is zero, which explains the value assigned to $M_L(0,0)$.

It remains to obtain a bound independent of the fixed cap. For noncomparable pairs with at least one positive eigenvalue, Equation <a href="#eq:obs-sylvester-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-sylvester-input">[eq:obs-sylvester-input]</a> gives entrywise $$|(D_iR)_{vw}|^2
 =\frac{(v+w)^2}{4(v-w)^2}
       |[\widehat H_i,R]_{vw}|^2
 \le\frac94|[\widehat H_i,R]_{vw}|^2.$$ This includes a pair with exactly one zero eigenvalue. Their total expected derivative energy is therefore $o(1)$. The kernel–kernel derivative is zero almost everywhere, as established in the Sylvester construction, and in any case has zero covariance multiplier. Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-multipliers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-multipliers">35</a> now yields $$\int_0^\infty\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\,\frac{dT}{T^2}
 \le\frac1{4\lambda}
 \left(9\mathbb E|DR|^2+4\log(1+L)\,o(1)\right).$$ Take the sequence limit at this fixed $L$, and use Equation <a href="#eq:obs-energy-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-energy-input">[eq:obs-energy-input]</a>. Stationarity makes the bound independent of $t$. This proves the proposition. In particular, the cap-dependent bound on noncomparable pairs is used only before its vanishing error has been removed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Stability on the entire scale interval

We next transfer observations to the frozen covariance of Proposition <a href="#prop:frozen-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:frozen-law">34</a>. A bound on a compact interval of $T$ would not suffice, because the useful scale of a block depends on its eigenvalue. The following comparison holds on all positive scales.

<div class="samepage">

<a id="stmt-lem-obs-integrated-stability"></a>
<div id="lem:obs-integrated-stability" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:obs-matrix-stability"></a>
**Lemma 37** (Integrated covariance stability). *For $T>0$ let $h_T(v)=\sqrt{1+f_L(Tv^2)}$, $v\ge0$, and put $$K_L=\int_0^\infty
 \frac{dq}{(1+q/L)^3(1+(1+1/L)q)}.$$ Then $0<K_L\le\log(1+L)$, and for arbitrary positive semidefinite matrices $A,B$ of the same size, $$\label{eq:obs-matrix-stability}
 \int_0^\infty\|h_T(A)-h_T(B)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\,\frac{dT}{T^2}
 \le K_L\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.$$ Let $\sigma$ be the frozen covariance in Proposition <a href="#prop:frozen-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:frozen-law">34</a>, and couple $$\widetilde Z_T=(\mathrm{Id}+f_L(T\sigma))^{1/2}G$$ to $Z_T$ using the same independent Gaussian. For every fixed $t,L$, <a id="eq:obs-observation-stability"></a>$$\label{eq:obs-observation-stability}
 \int_0^\infty\mathbb E|Z_T-\widetilde Z_T|^2\,\frac{dT}{T^2}=o(1).$$*

</div>

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-obs-integrated-stability"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-obs-integrated-stability</summary>

*Proof.* Direct differentiation gives $$h_T'(v)=\frac{Tv}
 {(1+Tv^2/L)^{3/2}(1+(1+1/L)Tv^2)^{1/2}}.$$ For $v>0$, substitution $q=Tv^2$ therefore shows $$\int_0^\infty|h_T'(v)|^2\,\frac{dT}{T^2}=K_L.$$ Discarding two of the three factors $1+q/L$ bounds $K_L$ by the integral evaluated in Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-multipliers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-multipliers">35</a>, namely $\log(1+L)$. If $v\ge w\ge0$, the fundamental theorem of calculus and Cauchy–Schwarz give $$|h_T(v)-h_T(w)|^2
 \le(v-w)\int_w^v|h_T'(r)|^2\,dr.$$ Integrating in $T$ and using Tonelli proves the scalar bound $K_L(v-w)^2$. The value of the derivative integral at the single endpoint $r=0$ does not affect this argument, so it covers $w=0$.

Choose an orthonormal eigenbasis $(e_a)$ of $A$ with eigenvalues $v_a$ and an orthonormal eigenbasis $(f_b)$ of $B$ with eigenvalues $w_b$. The entry of their functional-calculus difference between these two bases is $$\langle e_a,(h_T(A)-h_T(B))f_b\rangle
   =(h_T(v_a)-h_T(w_b))\langle e_a,f_b\rangle.$$ Multiply the scalar bound by $|\langle e_a,f_b\rangle|^2$ and sum over $a,b$. The sum on the right is $K_L\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2$, proving Equation <a href="#eq:obs-matrix-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-matrix-stability">[eq:obs-matrix-stability]</a>. Finally, conditional on all data except $G$, Gaussian isometry gives $$\mathbb E_G|Z_T-\widetilde Z_T|^2
 =\|h_T(R(Y_t))-h_T(\sigma^{1/2})\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.$$ Apply the matrix bound and the squared-mean convergence in Proposition <a href="#prop:frozen-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:frozen-law">34</a>. The resulting factor $K_L$ is fixed while the sequence limit is taken. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Conditional information and blockwise tests

<a id="eq:obs-frozen-covariance"></a>
<a id="eq:obs-block-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:obs-block-defects"></a>
Let $\mathcal D$ be the initial position, random spectral partition, chosen orthonormal frame, and frozen block matrices from Proposition <a href="#prop:frozen-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:frozen-law">34</a>. Block formulas below use components in this $\mathcal D$-measurable frame. Every information term $\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)$ continues to refer to the original observation in ambient coordinates; conditional on $\mathcal D$, conversion to the block coordinates is invertible. Orthogonal invariance makes the components of the fresh Gaussian noise conditionally standard and independent of the diffusion data. Conditional on $\mathcal D$, $N=B_t/\sqrt t$ is standard Gaussian. On a positive block with rounded eigenvalue $\beta$, write $H[v]=\sum_{i\in\beta}v_iH_i$. The frozen covariance is $$\label{eq:obs-frozen-covariance}
 \sigma_\beta=\beta^2
       \exp\big(\sqrt{8t}\,H[N]-4t\mathrm{Id}\big).$$ All $H_i$ here are the modified matrices supported on their initial blocks. Their bounds, from Equations <a href="#eq:flattening-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flattening-bound">[eq:flattening-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:matrix-tracial-defects" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-tracial-defects">[eq:matrix-tracial-defects]</a>, will be used in the form $$\begin{aligned}
 &S=\sum_iH_i^2\le C\mathrm{Id},
 \qquad \|H[v]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le C_1|v|^2,
 \label{eq:obs-block-bounds}\\
 &\tau(\mathrm{Id})\longrightarrow1,
 \qquad\tau((S-\mathrm{Id})^2)=o(1),
 \qquad\sum_{i,j}\tau(|[H_i,H_j]|^2)=o(1).
 \label{eq:obs-block-defects}
\end{aligned}$$ The constants $C,C_1\ge1$ are absolute. The entries of the three-index array $(H_i)_{ab}$ are also asymptotically symmetric in all three indices in the weighted squared norm. As before, $$\tau(U)=\mathbb E\sum_\beta\beta^2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_\beta U_\beta,$$ where expectation includes any Gaussian variables appearing in $U$. No trace in this definition or in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-block-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-block-bounds">[eq:obs-block-bounds]</a> is normalized by block dimension.

<a id="stmt-lem-obs-ou-score"></a>
<div id="lem:obs-ou-score" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:obs-ou-score"></a>
<a id="eq:obs-ou-information"></a>
**Lemma 38** (Conditional Ornstein–Uhlenbeck score). *Let $N$ be a standard Gaussian vector conditional on $\mathcal D$, and let $Z$ be any observation for which $\mathsf I(N;Z\mid\mathcal D)<\infty$. With independent standard Gaussian $G'$ set $$N_u=\alpha N+\sqrt{1-\alpha^2}\,G',\qquad \alpha=e^{-u},\quad u>0.$$ The relative score of its posterior, with respect to the standard Gaussian marginal, is $$\label{eq:obs-ou-score}
 D\log\frac{p_{N_u\mid Z,\mathcal D}}{\gamma}(N_u)
 =\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha^2}
   \mathbb E[N-\alpha N_u\mid N_u,Z,\mathcal D].$$ For every compact interval $\mathcal J\subset(0,\infty)$, $$\label{eq:obs-ou-information}
 \mathsf I(N;Z\mid\mathcal D)
 \ge\int_{\mathcal J}\frac{\alpha^2}{(1-\alpha^2)^2}
 \mathbb E\left|\mathbb E[N-\alpha N_u\mid N_u,Z,\mathcal D]\right|^2\,du.$$ For the covariance observation in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-covariance-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-covariance-definition">[eq:obs-covariance-definition]</a>, <a id="eq:obs-data-processing"></a>$$\label{eq:obs-data-processing}
 \mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\ge\mathsf I(N;Z_T\mid\mathcal D).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-obs-ou-score"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-obs-ou-score</summary>

*Proof.* Put $q=1-\alpha^2$. Given a posterior law $\pi(dn)$ of $N$ at fixed $(Z,\mathcal D)$, the density at $y$ after adding the OU noise is the integral of $(2\pi q)^{-d/2}\exp(-|y-\alpha n|^2/(2q))$ against $\pi$. Differentiating this kernel and dividing by its integral gives $$D\log p(y\mid Z,\mathcal D)
 =-\frac{y-\alpha\mathbb E[N\mid N_u=y,Z,\mathcal D]}q.$$ The score of the standard Gaussian is $-y$. Subtracting it proves Equation <a href="#eq:obs-ou-score" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-ou-score">[eq:obs-ou-score]</a>. Kernel differentiation is justified first with $N$ truncated; on compact positive time intervals its derivatives are bounded by Gaussian kernels times polynomials, and truncation can then be removed by conditional second moments.

The OU semigroup has generator $\Delta-y\cdot D$. For a positive posterior density $r_u$ relative to $\gamma$, integration by parts gives $$\frac{d}{du}\int r_u\log r_u\,d\gamma
 =-\int\frac{|Dr_u|^2}{r_u}\,d\gamma.$$ This is the entropy dissipation calculation of Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-calculus">5</a>; positive-time smoothing, truncation of the density and spatial cutoffs justify it for finite-entropy posteriors. Averaging over $(Z,\mathcal D)$ and integrating over $\mathcal J$ bounds its nonnegative right side by the initial conditional information. Substitution of the score formula proves Equation <a href="#eq:obs-ou-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-ou-information">[eq:obs-ou-information]</a>.

For the last assertion, observation noise is independent of the initial data and diffusion path, and its law depends on them only through $Y_t$. Hence $(\mathcal D,N)\longrightarrow Y_t\longrightarrow Z_T$ is a Markov chain. The chain rule and data processing give $$\mathsf I(N;Z_T\mid\mathcal D)
 \le\mathsf I(\mathcal D,N;Z_T)\le\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T).$$ These informations are finite for each member of the sequence: the covariance interval $[\mathrm{Id},(1+L)\mathrm{Id}]$ bounds the output entropy from above by that of a Gaussian of covariance $(1+L)\mathrm{Id}$ and bounds its conditional entropy from below by that of a standard Gaussian. Thus $\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\le(d/2)\log(1+L)$ in dimension $d$. This finiteness assertion is used only to justify the identities. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-obs-block-tests"></a>
<div id="lem:obs-block-tests" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:obs-block-test-bound"></a>
**Lemma 39** (Block tests and the scale Jacobian). *Fix compact intervals $\mathcal J,\mathcal X\subset(0,\infty)$, and write $\kappa(u)=\alpha^2/(1-\alpha^2)^2$, where $\alpha=e^{-u}$. Let $P(u,x,a,b)$ be a real polynomial, evaluated on matrices with any one fixed ordering of each monomial. On each positive block define $$A_\beta=H_\beta[N_\beta],\qquad
 A_{u,\beta}=H_\beta[(N_u)_\beta],\qquad
 B_{\beta,x}=H_\beta[(Z_{x/\beta^2})_\beta],$$ and $P_{\beta,u,x}=P(u,x,A_{u,\beta},B_{\beta,x})$. Then $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_0^\infty\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\,\frac{dT}{T^2}
 \ge{}&\int_{\mathcal J}\int_{\mathcal X}\kappa(u)
 \mathbb E\sum_\beta\beta^2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_\beta
 \bigl(2(A_\beta-\alpha A_{u,\beta})P_{\beta,u,x}
 \notag\\[-2pt]
 &\hspace{43mm}-C_1P_{\beta,u,x}^*P_{\beta,u,x}\bigr)
 \,\frac{dx}{x^2}\,du.
 \label{eq:obs-block-test-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ Here the constant $C_1$ is the absolute constant in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-block-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-block-bounds">[eq:obs-block-bounds]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-obs-block-tests"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-obs-block-tests</summary>

<a id="eq:obs-adjoint-bound"></a>
*Proof.* For a block, consider the linear map $\mathcal H:v\mapsto H[v]$ between its Euclidean coordinate space and its matrix space with the ordinary Hilbert–Schmidt norm. Its adjoint is $$(\mathcal H^*Q)_i=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(H_iQ),$$ because $H_i$ is real symmetric. Taking the adjoint in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-block-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-block-bounds">[eq:obs-block-bounds]</a> gives $$\label{eq:obs-adjoint-bound}
 \sum_i|\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(H_iQ)|^2\le C_1\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Q^*Q).$$ This step has no factor involving the dimension of the block.

At fixed $u,T$, select precisely those positive blocks for which $x=T\beta^2\in\mathcal X$. On a selected block set $$V_i=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_\beta
 \bigl(H_iP(u,T\beta^2,H[(N_u)_\beta],H[(Z_T)_\beta])\bigr),
 \qquad i\in\beta,$$ and set all other components, including those in the kernel, equal to zero. This vector is measurable with respect to $(N_u,Z_T,\mathcal D)$. If $m=\mathbb E[N-\alpha N_u\mid N_u,Z_T,\mathcal D]$, completing the square gives $$\mathbb E|m|^2\ge2\mathbb E(N-\alpha N_u)\cdot V-\mathbb E|V|^2.$$ The coordinates of distinct selected blocks are orthogonal. Their costs therefore add, and Equation <a href="#eq:obs-adjoint-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-adjoint-bound">[eq:obs-adjoint-bound]</a> bounds the cost by $C_1$ times the sum of their unnormalized squared matrix norms. Their linear terms contract exactly to $$(N-\alpha N_u)\cdot V
 =\sum_{\beta\text{ selected}}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_\beta
 \bigl((H[N_\beta]-\alpha H[(N_u)_\beta])P\bigr).$$ Substitute these two statements in Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-ou-score" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-ou-score">38</a> and integrate in $T$.

<a id="eq:obs-scale-jacobian"></a>
For each realized positive block separately, the change of variable $x=T\beta^2$ has the exact Jacobian $$\label{eq:obs-scale-jacobian}
 T=\frac{x}{\beta^2},\qquad dT=\frac{dx}{\beta^2},\qquad
 \boxed{\displaystyle\frac{dT}{T^2}=\beta^2\frac{dx}{x^2}}.$$ Thus the block selection becomes $x\in\mathcal X$, and the coefficient of each block trace becomes precisely $\beta^2$. This proves Equation <a href="#eq:obs-block-test-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-block-test-bound">[eq:obs-block-test-bound]</a>.

<a id="eq:obs-gaussian-moment-bound"></a>
For completeness, all its polynomial terms are integrable on the compact parameter rectangle. The moment bounds of Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-gaussian-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-gaussian-moments">33</a> control $A_\beta$ and $A_{u,\beta}$ under the weighted trace. Conditional on the initial data, diffusion path, and OU noise, $B_{\beta,x}$ is a Gaussian matrix series whose vector covariance is at most $(1+L)\mathrm{Id}$. If its coefficients are $K_j=\sum_i Q_{ij}H_i$ for a square root $Q$ of that covariance, then for every vector $v$ $$\sum_j|K_jv|^2\le(1+L)\sum_i|H_iv|^2\le C(1+L)|v|^2.$$ The same Gaussian even-moment proof therefore gives, for every fixed integer $p\ge1$, $$\label{eq:obs-gaussian-moment-bound}
 \mathbb E_G\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_\beta|B_{\beta,x}|^{2p}
 \le[C(1+L)]^p(2p-1)!!\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_\beta\mathrm{Id}.$$ Trace Hölder bounds each mixed word by its factors’ moments. After multiplying by $\beta^2$ and summing, the right side is a constant times $\tau(\mathrm{Id})$, which is finite and bounded along the sequence. The measure $\kappa(u)\,du\,dx/x^2$ is finite on the chosen rectangle. Tonelli applies to the square terms, while these moment bounds justify Fubini for the linear terms. One can equivalently first truncate $V$ in the conditional-mean inequality and then remove that truncation in this integrated squared norm. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Transfer of the polynomial tests to scalar observations

The scale substitution has now produced the tracial weights of the matrix limit. We next identify that limit for the matrices containing the observation. Two replacements are needed: first replace $Z_T$ by its frozen version on the whole scale interval, and then use the approximate symmetry of the three-index array to move a bounded matrix function through $H[\,\cdot\,]$.

<a id="stmt-lem-obs-polynomial-replacement"></a>
<div id="lem:obs-polynomial-replacement" class="lemma">

**Lemma 40** (Replacement in integrated polynomial tests). *In the notation of Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-block-tests" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-block-tests">39</a>, put $\widetilde B_{\beta,x}
=H_\beta[(\widetilde Z_{x/\beta^2})_\beta]$. For every fixed polynomial in $u,x,A_\beta,A_{u,\beta},B_{\beta,x}$ and their adjoints, its weighted trace integral over $\mathcal J\times\mathcal X$ changes by $o(1)$ if $B_{\beta,x}$ is replaced by $\widetilde B_{\beta,x}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-obs-polynomial-replacement"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-obs-polynomial-replacement</summary>

*Proof.* The flattening bound and the inverse substitution in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-scale-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-scale-jacobian">[eq:obs-scale-jacobian]</a> give $$\begin{aligned}
 &\mathbb E\sum_\beta\beta^2\int_{\mathcal X}
 \|B_{\beta,x}-\widetilde B_{\beta,x}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\,\frac{dx}{x^2}
 \\
 &\quad=\int_0^\infty\mathbb E\sum_{\beta:T\beta^2\in\mathcal X}
 \|H_\beta[(Z_T-\widetilde Z_T)_\beta]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\,\frac{dT}{T^2}
 \\
 &\quad\le C_1\int_0^\infty
 \mathbb E|Z_T-\widetilde Z_T|^2\,\frac{dT}{T^2}=o(1),
\end{aligned}$$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-integrated-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-integrated-stability">37</a>. All projected errors are summed before this comparison; no small block weight is divided out. Multiplication by the bounded weight $\kappa(u)$ and integration in the compact interval $\mathcal J$ preserve $o(1)$.

To pass from this squared norm to arbitrary fixed products, include the parameter integrals in the finite tracial functional $$\mathcal T(U)=\int_{\mathcal J}\int_{\mathcal X}
 \kappa(u)\mathbb E\sum_\beta\beta^2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_\beta U_{\beta,u,x}
 \,\frac{dx}{x^2}\,du.$$ Every matrix in the statement has bounded $L^p(\mathcal T)$ norm for every fixed finite $p$. For $B$ this was proved in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-gaussian-moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-gaussian-moment-bound">[eq:obs-gaussian-moment-bound]</a>; for $\widetilde B$ the same proof applies because its covariance is also at most $(1+L)\mathrm{Id}$. The remaining matrices are covered by the Gaussian moment lemma. For any $q>p\ge2$, interpolation with $1/p=\theta/2+(1-\theta)/q$ gives $$\|B-\widetilde B\|_{L^p(\mathcal T)}
 \le\|B-\widetilde B\|_{L^2(\mathcal T)}^\theta
       \|B-\widetilde B\|_{L^q(\mathcal T)}^{1-\theta}=o(1).$$ For lower powers, Hölder and boundedness of $\mathcal T(\mathrm{Id})$ give the same conclusion. Expand the difference of two words of length $r$ by replacing one factor at a time. Trace Hölder with all exponents $r$ bounds each resulting trace by the product of its factors’ $L^r(\mathcal T)$ norms. The changed factor tends to zero and the others remain bounded. There are only finitely many such terms. This proves the claim, including polynomial squared norms and linear terms with $A-\alpha A_u$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:obs-g-function"></a>
For fixed $t,L$, define the scalar function $$\label{eq:obs-g-function}
 g_x(v)=\sqrt{1+f_L\big(xe^{\sqrt{8t}\,v-4t}\big)},
 \qquad x>0,\ v\in\mathbb R.$$ Then $1\le g_x\le\sqrt{1+L}$. By Equation <a href="#eq:obs-frozen-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-frozen-covariance">[eq:obs-frozen-covariance]</a>, the frozen observation on a block at $T=x/\beta^2$ is exactly $g_x(A_\beta)G_\beta$. Thus $\widetilde B_{\beta,x}=H_\beta[g_x(A_\beta)G_\beta]$.

<a id="stmt-lem-obs-polynomial-density"></a>
<div id="lem:obs-polynomial-density" class="lemma">

**Lemma 41** (Polynomial density under exponential moments). *Let $\nu$ be a finite positive measure on $\mathbb R^k$ with $\int e^{a|w|}\,d\nu(w)<\infty$ for every $a>0$. Polynomials are dense in $L^p(\nu)$ for every $1\le p<\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-obs-polynomial-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-obs-polynomial-density</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose a continuous linear functional on $L^p(\nu)$ annihilates all polynomials. Its representing density $F$ belongs to $L^{p'}(\nu)$, where $p'=p/(p-1)$ and $p'=\infty$ if $p=1$. Hölder and the hypothesis show that the finite signed measure $F\,d\nu$ has exponential moments of every linear magnitude. For each $\xi\in\mathbb R^k$, the function $$z\longmapsto\int e^{z\xi\cdot w}F(w)\,d\nu(w)$$ is entire: on every compact set of complex $z$, its derivatives are dominated by an integrable exponential. All its derivatives at zero vanish because $(\xi\cdot w)^j$ is a polynomial. Its Taylor series and hence the entire function are zero. Evaluating at $z=\mathrm i$ proves that the Fourier transform of $F\,d\nu$ vanishes. Fourier uniqueness for finite measures can here be seen by convolving with a centered Gaussian: the convolution has zero Fourier transform and an integrable Fourier inversion formula, so is zero for every positive Gaussian variance; letting that variance tend to zero shows the original measure is zero. Thus $F=0$ almost everywhere. Duality, or equivalently separation of a proper closed subspace, proves the density assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-obs-nonlinear-trace-law"></a>
<div id="lem:obs-nonlinear-trace-law" class="lemma">

**Lemma 42** (Nonlinear mixed trace law). *Let $n,g',g$ be independent scalar standard Gaussians and let $n_u=\alpha n+\sqrt{1-\alpha^2}\,g'$. For every fixed mixed polynomial, the weighted trace moments of $$\big(A_\beta,A_{u,\beta},\widetilde B_{\beta,x}\big)$$ converge to the corresponding scalar moments of $$\big(n,n_u,g_x(n)g\big).$$ The assertion holds after integration over compact positive $u,x$ intervals against $\kappa(u)\,du\,dx/x^2$, and permits fixed polynomial dependence on $u,x$. Adjoints in a matrix word become the identical real scalar factors in its limit.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-obs-nonlinear-trace-law"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-obs-nonlinear-trace-law</summary>

<a id="eq:obs-index-swap-comparison"></a>
<a id="eq:obs-index-swap-identity"></a>
<a id="eq:obs-index-swap-error"></a>
*Proof.* First we prove the matrix comparison $$\label{eq:obs-index-swap-comparison}
 H[g_x(A)G]-g_x(A)H[G]\longrightarrow0
 \quad\hbox{in weighted squared Hilbert--Schmidt norm}.$$ All notation in this calculation is on one block. Define $$J(v)_{ab}=(H_bv)_a,
 \qquad D(v)=H[v]-J(v),
 \qquad \Delta_{abi}=(H_i)_{ab}-(H_b)_{ai}.$$ Put $q=g_x(A)$ and $B=H[G]$. The exact columnwise identity is $$\label{eq:obs-index-swap-identity}
 H[qG]-qB=D(qG)+K-qD(G),
 \qquad K_{\cdot b}=[H_b,q]G.$$ Indeed the $b$th column of $J(qG)-qJ(G)$ is $H_bqG-qH_bG$. Conditional Gaussian averaging therefore bounds the square of the left side by $$\label{eq:obs-index-swap-error}
 6(1+L)\sum_{a,b,i}|\Delta_{abi}|^2
       +3\sum_b\|[H_b,g_x(A)]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.$$ For the first and last terms in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-index-swap-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-index-swap-identity">[eq:obs-index-swap-identity]</a>, we used $\|q\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\sqrt{1+L}$ and the Gaussian isometry for the linear map $D$.

The function $g_x$ is Lipschitz with a bound depending only on $t,L$, uniformly for all $x>0$. In fact, with $a=\sqrt{8t}$ and $r=xe^{av-4t}/L$, differentiation gives $$|g_x'(v)|
 =\frac{aLr}{2(1+r)^2g_x(v)}\le\frac{aL}{8}.$$ In an eigenbasis of $A$ with eigenvalues $r_a$, the squared commutator norm is $$\|[H_b,g_x(A)]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =\sum_{a,c}|(H_b)_{ac}|^2|g_x(r_c)-g_x(r_a)|^2
 \le\operatorname{Lip}(g_x)^2\|[H_b,A]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.$$ This is a scalar Lipschitz estimate for matrix entries in the Hilbert–Schmidt norm. Averaging $A=\sum_iN_iH_i$ over $N$ gives $$\mathbb E_N\sum_b\|[H_b,A]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =\sum_{b,i}\|[H_b,H_i]\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.$$ Multiply Equation <a href="#eq:obs-index-swap-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-index-swap-error">[eq:obs-index-swap-error]</a> by the block weight, sum, and average. The total symmetry defect and Equation <a href="#eq:obs-block-defects" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-block-defects">[eq:obs-block-defects]</a> prove Equation <a href="#eq:obs-index-swap-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-index-swap-comparison">[eq:obs-index-swap-comparison]</a>, uniformly in $x$ at fixed $t,L$.

Every fixed higher moment of both sides of this comparison is bounded. Conditional on $A$ and the initial data, $H[g_x(A)G]$ is a Gaussian matrix series with coefficient-square sum at most $(1+L)S$, by the quadratic-form argument used in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-gaussian-moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-gaussian-moment-bound">[eq:obs-gaussian-moment-bound]</a>. Also the Schatten ideal inequality gives $$\|g_x(A)H[G]\|_{p}\le\sqrt{1+L}\,\|H[G]\|_{p}$$ on each block. Interpolation and telescoping products, exactly as in Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-polynomial-replacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-polynomial-replacement">40</a>, show that these two matrices may be interchanged in every fixed polynomial trace.

<a id="eq:obs-functional-approximation"></a>
It remains to identify traces containing $g_x(A)H[G]$. Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-gaussian-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-gaussian-moments">33</a> gives the joint scalar Gaussian moment law for $A=H[N]$, $H[G']$, and $H[G]$. In particular, $A_u=\alpha A+\sqrt{1-\alpha^2}H[G']$ has the required joint law with them. To include the bounded continuous function $g_x(A)$, fix $x$ and a mixed word of length $r$. Choose a polynomial $p$ approximating $g_x$ in a sufficiently high finite $L^q(\gamma_1)$ norm, where $\gamma_1$ is the scalar standard Gaussian law. Choose $q$ larger than all the Hölder exponents needed for this fixed word, and take the approximation error at most $1$; then $\|p\|_{L^q(\gamma_1)}
\le\sqrt{1+L}+1$. Such approximations exist by the polynomial-density argument in Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-polynomial-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-polynomial-density">41</a>, applied to one Gaussian coordinate. The scalar spectral measures of $A$ under $\tau$ converge to $\gamma_1$ by the moment and exponential-moment bounds in Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-gaussian-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-gaussian-moments">33</a>. Their higher moments are uniformly bounded. Consequently, for even $q$, $$\label{eq:obs-functional-approximation}
 \tau\bigl(|g_x(A)-p(A)|^q\bigr)
 \longrightarrow\mathbb E|g_x(n)-p(n)|^q.$$ To justify this last passage directly, cut the scalar spectral variable off at $|v|\le M$, use weak convergence for the bounded continuous integrand there, and bound the complement by a higher uniform moment of $A$. Since $g_x$ is bounded and $p$ has fixed degree, that tail tends to zero as $M\to\infty$.

Replace each occurrence of $g_x(A)$ by $p(A)$. Telescoping the word and applying trace Hölder bounds the replacement error by a constant times the $L^q$ error in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-functional-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-functional-approximation">[eq:obs-functional-approximation]</a>, with all other factors controlled by fixed higher moments. For factors $p(A)$, the preceding $L^q$ bound and moment convergence give a bound independent of how accurately $p$ approximates $g_x$. Thus the error constant stays bounded as that approximation is improved. The remaining word is polynomial in the three independent Gaussian matrix series, so its trace has the scalar Gaussian limit. Let the scalar approximation error tend to zero. This proves the claimed limit at each fixed $u,x$; the scalar third coordinate is $g_x(n)g$.

Finally, the moment bounds for the original, bounded function $g_x$ are uniform on the compact parameter rectangle, as are the bounds on polynomial coefficients in $u,x$ and on $\alpha$. They dominate each mixed trace by an integrable constant. Dominated convergence thus permits parameter integration. This last argument uses the uniform bounds for $g_x$ itself, and does not require choosing one polynomial approximant uniformly in $x$. ◻

</details>

The preceding lemmas identify the polynomial tests of the actual observation with those of the scalar variance mixture. We can now optimize the tests and integrate the scalar OU dissipation. The constant in the resulting comparison is exactly the constant of the unnormalized flattening estimate.

<a id="stmt-prop-information-lower"></a>
<div id="prop:information-lower" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:obs-information-lower"></a>
**Proposition 43** (Scalar information lower bound). *For every fixed $t>0$ and $L\ge1$, let $g_x$ be defined by Equation <a href="#eq:obs-g-function" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-g-function">[eq:obs-g-function]</a>, and let $n,g$ be independent scalar standard Gaussians. Then $$\label{eq:obs-information-lower}
 \liminf\int_0^\infty\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\,\frac{dT}{T^2}
 \ge C_1^{-1}\int_0^\infty
       \mathsf I\big(n;g_x(n)g\big)\,\frac{dx}{x^2}.$$ The constant $C_1$ is universal and is independent of $t,L$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-information-lower"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-information-lower</summary>

<a id="eq:obs-scalar-test-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Fix compact positive intervals $\mathcal J,\mathcal X$, and let $n,g',g$ be independent scalar standard Gaussians. Write $n_u=\alpha n+\sqrt{1-\alpha^2}\,g'$, and fix a scalar polynomial $P(u,x,a,b)$. Give its matrix version any fixed ordering of the factors of each monomial. Lemmas <a href="#lem:obs-block-tests" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-block-tests">39</a>, <a href="#lem:obs-polynomial-replacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-polynomial-replacement">40</a>, and <a href="#lem:obs-nonlinear-trace-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-nonlinear-trace-law">42</a>, in that order, imply $$\begin{aligned}
 &\liminf\int_0^\infty\mathsf I(Y_t;Z_T)\,\frac{dT}{T^2}
 \notag\\
 &\quad\ge\int_{\mathcal J}\int_{\mathcal X}\kappa(u)
 \mathbb E\big[2(n-\alpha n_u)P(u,x,n_u,g_x(n)g)
 \notag\\[-2pt]
 &\hspace{58mm}-C_1P(u,x,n_u,g_x(n)g)^2\big]
       \,\frac{dx}{x^2}\,du.
 \label{eq:obs-scalar-test-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ If the matrix polynomial is not symmetric, its prelimit cost is $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(P^*P)$ as in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-block-test-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-block-test-bound">[eq:obs-block-test-bound]</a>. The mixed trace law takes this cost to the real scalar $P^2$, so symmetry of the chosen ordering is unnecessary.

The sequence limit in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-scalar-test-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-scalar-test-bound">[eq:obs-scalar-test-bound]</a> has been taken for each fixed polynomial. We now optimize the tests in one squared-mean space containing the compact parameters. Give $(u,x)$ the finite measure $\kappa(u)\,du\,dx/x^2$ on $\mathcal J\times\mathcal X$, independently of the scalar Gaussians $n,g',g$, and put <a id="eq:obs-scalar-observed-vector"></a>$$\label{eq:obs-scalar-observed-vector}
 W=(u,x,n_u,g_x(n)g),\qquad r=n-\alpha n_u.$$ The first two coordinates of $W$ are bounded. At each fixed $u,x$, the variable $n_u$ is standard Gaussian, and $|g_x(n)g|\le\sqrt{1+L}|g|$. Cauchy–Schwarz therefore gives all linear exponential moments for its finite law, uniformly on the compact parameter rectangle. Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-polynomial-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-polynomial-density">41</a> shows that polynomials in the four coordinates of $W$ are dense in this $L^2$ space.

<a id="eq:obs-scalar-score-lower"></a>
Since $\mathbb E[r^2\mid u,x]=1-\alpha^2\le1$, the conditional mean $m(W)=\mathbb E[r\mid W]$ belongs to the same space. For the finite joint measure just defined, completion of the square gives $$\int\big(2rP(W)-C_1P(W)^2\big)
 =C_1^{-1}\int m^2-C_1\int(P-m/C_1)^2.$$ Density makes the infimum of the final squared error zero, using single four-variable polynomials in the integrated space. Taking their supremum and then disintegrating with respect to $(u,x)$ turns the right side of Equation <a href="#eq:obs-scalar-test-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-scalar-test-bound">[eq:obs-scalar-test-bound]</a> into $$\label{eq:obs-scalar-score-lower}
 C_1^{-1}\int_{\mathcal J}\int_{\mathcal X}
 \frac{\alpha^2}{(1-\alpha^2)^2}
 \mathbb E\left|\mathbb E[n-\alpha n_u\mid n_u,g_x(n)g]\right|^2
 \,\frac{dx}{x^2}\,du.$$ The conditional expectation here is taken at each fixed $u,x$. All approximation constants used before this optimization may depend on the fixed polynomial, $t,L$, and the compact intervals. The coefficient $C_1$ depends only on Equation <a href="#eq:obs-adjoint-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-adjoint-bound">[eq:obs-adjoint-bound]</a>.

<a id="eq:obs-scalar-ou-identity"></a>
We verify the scalar information identity including its endpoints. Fix $x>0$ and write $z_x=g_x(n)g$. Its information is finite: conditional variance lies in $[1,1+L]$, so Gaussian entropy comparison gives <a id="eq:obs-scalar-information-finite"></a>$$\label{eq:obs-scalar-information-finite}
 0\le\mathsf I(n;z_x)\le\tfrac12\log(1+L).$$ Indeed $\mathbb Ez_x^2\le1+L$ bounds its entropy above by $\frac12\log(2\pi e(1+L))$, while its conditional entropy is $\frac12\log(2\pi e)+\mathbb E\log g_x(n)$ and is at least $\frac12\log(2\pi e)$. Apply the scalar version of Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-ou-score" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-ou-score">38</a> and its entropy dissipation identity between times $a$ and $b$, where $0<a<b<\infty$. This gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathsf I(n_a;z_x)-\mathsf I(n_b;z_x)
 =\int_a^b\frac{\alpha^2}{(1-\alpha^2)^2}
 \mathbb E\left|\mathbb E[n-\alpha n_u\mid n_u,z_x]\right|^2\,du.
\end{aligned}$$ As $a\downarrow0$, the joint pair $(n_a,z_x)$ converges in law to $(n,z_x)$. Lower semicontinuity of relative entropy, applied to the joint law and the product of its marginals, gives $\mathsf I(n;z_x)\le\liminf_{a\downarrow0}\mathsf I(n_a;z_x)$. The reverse inequality follows from the Markov chain $z_x\longrightarrow n\longrightarrow n_a$ and data processing. Thus the initial endpoint is exactly $\mathsf I(n;z_x)$. At the other endpoint, the same chain gives $$0\le\mathsf I(n_u;z_x)\le\mathsf I(n_u;n)
       =-\tfrac12\log(1-\alpha^2)\longrightarrow0.$$ The equality follows by subtracting the conditional Gaussian entropy of variance $1-\alpha^2$ from that of the standard Gaussian marginal. We have proved $$\label{eq:obs-scalar-ou-identity}
 \mathsf I(n;z_x)=\int_0^\infty
 \frac{\alpha^2}{(1-\alpha^2)^2}
 \mathbb E\left|\mathbb E[n-\alpha n_u\mid n_u,z_x]\right|^2\,du.$$

Finally exhaust both positive parameter axes by compact intervals in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-scalar-score-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-scalar-score-lower">[eq:obs-scalar-score-lower]</a>. Its integrand is nonnegative. Monotone convergence and Equation <a href="#eq:obs-scalar-ou-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-scalar-ou-identity">[eq:obs-scalar-ou-identity]</a> prove Equation <a href="#eq:obs-information-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-information-lower">[eq:obs-information-lower]</a>. In particular, the polynomial optimization and the exhaustion take place after the counterexample-sequence limit, so neither requires a uniform approximation over unbounded parameters. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Divergence of the scalar scale integral

The upper and lower comparisons are now in the same information units, with universal constants $C_0,C_1$. To contradict them, it suffices to let the fixed diffusion time and the fixed covariance cap grow together. The following elementary test detects the large values of the scalar lognormal variance.

<a id="stmt-lem-scalar-divergence"></a>
<div id="lem:scalar-divergence" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:obs-scalar-divergence"></a>
**Lemma 44** (Scalar information divergence). *Let $n,g$ be independent scalar standard Gaussians and, for $t\ge1$, define $$\ell=\exp(\sqrt{8t}\,n-4t),\qquad
 z_x=\sqrt{1+f_t(x\ell)}\,g.$$ There are absolute constants $c>0$ and $h_0\ge1$ such that, for all $t\ge h_0$, $$\label{eq:obs-scalar-divergence}
 \int_0^\infty\mathsf I(n;z_x)\,\frac{dx}{x^2}
 \ge c\mathbb E\big[\ell\mathbf 1_{\{\ell\ge e^{2t}\}}\big]
                  \log(t/h_0)
 \ge\frac c2\log(t/h_0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-scalar-divergence"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scalar-divergence</summary>

*Proof.* The Gaussian moment generating formula gives $\mathbb E\ell=1$. Let $P_{x,n}$ be the conditional law of $z_x$ given $n$, and let $Q_x$ be its marginal law. Thus $$\mathsf I(n;z_x)=\mathbb E_n H(P_{x,n}\mid Q_x).$$ Set $p_0=\mathbb P\{|g|\ge\sqrt2\}>0$. Suppose first that $\ell\ge e^{2t}$, and choose a scale with $h=x\ell\in[h_0,t]$. Since $h\le t$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(z_x\mid n)=1+\frac{h}{1+h/t}\ge h/2.$$ It follows that the event $A_h=\{|z|\ge\sqrt h\}$ has conditional probability $p=P_{x,n}(A_h)\ge p_0$.

To bound its marginal probability, use an independent variable $\ell'$ with the law of $\ell$. On $x\ell'\le1$, the conditional variance of the mixture component is at most $2$, so its probability of $A_h$ is at most $2e^{-h/4}$ by the Gaussian exponential bound. On the complementary event, Markov’s inequality and $\mathbb E\ell'=1$ give probability at most $x$. Hence <a id="eq:obs-binary-marginal-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:obs-binary-marginal-bound}
 q=Q_x(A_h)\le2e^{-h/4}+x
       \le2e^{-h/4}+te^{-2t}\le3e^{-h/4}.$$ The last inequality holds for $t\ge1$ and $h\le t$, because $te^{-2t}\le e^{-t/4}\le e^{-h/4}$.

Apply data processing to the event indicator. The binary relative entropy is bounded below by $$\begin{aligned}
 H(P_{x,n}\mid Q_x)
 &\ge p\log(p/q)+(1-p)\log\big((1-p)/(1-q)\big)\\
 &\ge p\log(1/q)-\log2\\
 &\ge p_0(h/4-\log3)-\log2.
\end{aligned}$$ Choose once and for all $$h_0\ge\max\{1,\,8\log3+8\log2/p_0\},
 \qquad c=p_0/8.$$ The preceding expression is then at least $ch$ for $h\in[h_0,t]$. This entropy estimate is made separately for each conditional law $P_{x,n}$; the chosen event may therefore depend on $n$ through $h=x\ell$.

Nonnegativity of relative entropy permits restricting the scale integral to these choices of $n$ and $x$. Tonelli and the change of variable $x=h/\ell$ yield $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_0^\infty\mathsf I(n;z_x)\,\frac{dx}{x^2}
 &\ge\mathbb E\left[\mathbf 1_{\{\ell\ge e^{2t}\}}
       \int_{h_0/\ell}^{t/\ell}
           H(P_{x,n}\mid Q_x)\,\frac{dx}{x^2}\right]\\
 &\ge c\mathbb E\left[\ell\mathbf 1_{\{\ell\ge e^{2t}\}}
                         \int_{h_0}^t\frac{dh}{h}\right]\\
 &=c\mathbb E\big[\ell\mathbf 1_{\{\ell\ge e^{2t}\}}\big]\log(t/h_0).
\end{aligned}$$ Under the probability change of measure with density $\ell$, completion of the square makes $n$ a Gaussian of mean $\sqrt{8t}$ and variance $1$. Therefore $\log\ell$ under that measure is Gaussian of mean $4t$ and variance $8t$. If $\Phi$ is the standard Gaussian distribution function, then $$\mathbb E\big[\ell\mathbf 1_{\{\ell\ge e^{2t}\}}\big]
   =\Phi(\sqrt{t/2})\ge\tfrac12.$$ This proves both inequalities in Equation <a href="#eq:obs-scalar-divergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-scalar-divergence">[eq:obs-scalar-divergence]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-scalar-divergence-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scalar-divergence</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Suppose that the asserted dimension-free bound fails. Lemma <a href="#lem:critical-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:critical-normalization">7</a> gives compact centered counterexamples with normalized logarithmic Sobolev constant $1$ and linear subgaussian parameter tending to zero. Proposition <a href="#prop:regularized-constants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:regularized-constants">14</a> shows that their positive-time regularized constants converge to $1$. Proposition <a href="#prop:critical-sequence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:critical-sequence">15</a> supplies the stationary laws and near-eigenfunction fields at the fixed regularization time $s=1/8$, in either the strict entropy branch or the spectral gap branch. Proposition <a href="#prop:prediction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prediction">18</a> gives the exact prediction curve in both cases. Proposition <a href="#prop:hierarchy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hierarchy">20</a> then gives the symmetric tensor hierarchy, and Proposition <a href="#prop:frozen-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:frozen-law">34</a> constructs its matrix square root and frozen block description. These are precisely the inputs used in the present section.

<a id="eq:obs-final-scalar-bound"></a>
For each fixed $t>0$ and $L\ge1$, combine Propositions <a href="#prop:information-upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:information-upper">36</a> and <a href="#prop:information-lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:information-lower">43</a>. They give $$\label{eq:obs-final-scalar-bound}
 \int_0^\infty
 \mathsf I\left(n;
 \sqrt{1+f_L(xe^{\sqrt{8t}\,n-4t})}\,g\right)
       \,\frac{dx}{x^2}\le C_0C_1,$$ where $C_0C_1$ is independent of both fixed parameters. Choose an arbitrarily large but finite $t\ge h_0$ and the fixed cap $L=t$. Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-divergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-divergence">44</a> makes the left side at least $(c/2)\log(t/h_0)$, which exceeds $C_0C_1$ for a sufficiently large choice of $t$. This is a contradiction.

All counterexample limits used to obtain Equation <a href="#eq:obs-final-scalar-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-final-scalar-bound">[eq:obs-final-scalar-bound]</a> were taken with this $t,L$ fixed. Only afterward was their numerical size chosen. Thus no uniform estimate on the intermediate errors as $t,L\to\infty$ is needed. The contradiction excludes an unbounded ratio $R(\mu)/a^2$ over the measures in the theorem. Finally the convention $\mathop{\mathrm{Ent}}_\mu(f^2)\le2R(\mu)\int|Df|^2\,d\mu$ absorbs the factor $2$ into a universal constant and gives the asserted inequality for every smooth compactly supported real test function. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Main stages of the contradiction argument. At the regularized law, the strict entropy branch means that the logarithmic Sobolev constant exceeds the Poincaré constant; in the gap branch the two constants coincide. The logarithmic Sobolev inequality of the common critical law, together with the square-root energy estimates of Section 6, supplies the information upper bound. The frozen matrix law supplies the scalar lower bound. After the sequence limit at fixed parameters, its scale integral becomes arbitrarily large as the diffusion time and covariance cap grow together.",
      "figure_id": "fig:proof-dependencies",
      "pdf_page": 5,
      "source_evidence": [

          "exact_quote": "Main stages of the contradiction argument. At the regularized\nlaw, the strict entropy branch means that the logarithmic Sobolev\nconstant exceeds the Poincar\\'e constant; in the gap branch the two\nconstants coincide. The logarithmic Sobolev ",

line width=.55pt}\n]\n\\node[stage, text width=6.4cm] (strict) at (-3.65,0)\n  {Strict entropy branch\\\\\n   Entropy optimizer\\\\\n   Lemma~\\ref{lem:critical-two-alternatives}(i)};\n\\node[stage, text width=6.4cm] (gap) at (3.65,0)\n  {Gap branch\\\\\n   Gap eigenfunction\\\\\n   Lemma~\\ref{lem:critical-two-alternatives}(ii)};\n\n\\node[stage, text width=5.8cm] (critical) at (0,-1.55)\n  {Common critical law and vector field\\\\\n   Proposition~\\ref{prop:critical-sequence}};\n\\draw[flow] (strict.south) -- (critical.north west);\n\\draw[flow] (gap.south) -- (critical.north east);\n\n\\node[stage, text width=4.0cm, minimum height=1.5cm]\n  (prediction) at (-4.85,-3.2)\n  {Prediction curve\\\\\n   and posterior estimates\\\\\n   Prop.~\\ref{prop:prediction}, Lemma~\\ref{lem:posterior-outliers}};\n\\node[stage, text width=4.0cm, minimum height=1.5cm]\n  (hierarchy) at (0,-3.2)\n  {Symmetric\\\\tensor hierarchy\\\\\n   Proposition~\\ref{prop:hierarchy}};\n\\node[stage, text width=4.0cm, minimum height=1.5cm]\n  (matrix) at (4.85,-3.2)\n  {Matrix estimates\\\\and frozen law\\\\\n and~\\ref{prop:information-lower},\\\\\n   Lemma~\\ref{lem:scalar-divergence}};\n\\draw[flow] (matrix.south) -- (4.85,-4.5)\n  -- node[above, align=center] {matrix bounds} (0,-4.5)\n  --
